Office, Deputy Assistant Secretary, Arms Control Office, Assistant Secretary, International Security Affairs DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE FEBRUARY 1965 HARVARD LAW LIBRARY

This publication has been prepared by the Department of the Army Library in response to a request from the Office of the Assistant Secretary of Defense for International Security Affairs. It is intended to assist the many people students, private groups, and organizations, and businesses—who have expressed an interest in learning more about arms control and disarmament in relation to our national security. No effort has been made to delete or exclude references by reason of their controversial character. Neither the sponsoring agency nor any other agency of the U.S. Government endorses any of the views. expressed or attests to the accuracy of the information contained herein.

Abraham Lincoln

ANALYSTS' NOTE 

This analytical survey of literature on arms control, disarmament, and U.S. national security was prepared by the research analysts of The Army 

Library at the request of the Deputy Assistant Secretary for Arms Control, Department of Defense. 

It covers the period from October 1961 through January 1965 and aims to record both the progress that was made and the problems that had arisen in the many and varied facets in the sphere of arms control and disarmament. 

A great spate of literature had been published during this period attempting to take stock, or define, or project and forecast the problems and solutions of peace and war in a thermonuclear age and in a world divided by sharply antagonistic ideological and political systems.

The intellectual dialogue and the voluminous outpouring of information resulting from it, testify at once to the responsibility and concern that is felt in the United States for the preservation and protection of freedom and peace in the world. The issues of arms control and disarmament being many-sided, the dialogue reflectively is many-sided also. The Analysts of The Army 

Library made every effort to provide maximum information within the abstracts, in order to make this complicated subject more definable and more utilitarian to the user. Materials on policies, strategies, concepts, doctrines, proposals, negotiations, disagreements and agreements, war-mongering, peacekeeping, military might, destructiveness of nuclear weapons, space as the new politico-military dimension, international cooperation, and many other subjects have been included, and in their aggregate present a thorough delineation of the subject of arms control and disarmament, as well as the all-important implications inherent in the subject to the security of the United States. 

The 750 unclassified papers appearing in this bibliography represent a selection from more than several thousand books, magazine articles, and documents on the subject; some of these from unfriendly sources. For the most part the materials included are available on the open shelves of The Army 

Library. 

The Analysts extend grateful appreciation to Mr. Arvin Kramish and Mr. 

Garry L. Quinn of the Office, Deputy Assistant Secretary for Arms Control, for guidance given during the many fruitful discussions which were held while this study was in preparation. Appreciation is also extended to the 

Staff of the Documents Division, U.S. Arms Control and Disarmament Agency, and to the Staff of the Documents Division, Institute for Defense Analyses for the loan of materials and permission to abstract them. iii

U.S. SECURITY, ARMS CONTROL, AND DISARMAMENT-1961-1965 I. NATIONAL INTERESTS AND ARMS CONTROL AND DISARMAMENT: THE GLOBAL SPECTRUM A. United States: National and International 

Objectives 

1. National Security a. Miscellaneous Aspects THE CHURCHES AND THE BOMB, by 

James Albertson, in America, v. 111, no. 22 (28 

November 1964) 692-693.

In August 1964, a two-day symposium was held on science and religion at the Weapons Laboratory at Kirtland Air Force Base, New Mexico. Among the participants was a conviction that moral factors should play a role in the formulation of defense policy. The author points out that the 

Kennedy-McNamara policy of controlled and graduated response is essentially a Christian response to the problem of defending the free world in the nuclear age. Controlled response to aggression is morally superior to the massive retaliation concept which preceded it. However, what is still needed is a public forum in which questions of nuclear morality can be discussed.

THE CRAFT OF INTELLIGENCE, by Allen Dulles. New York, Harper & Row, 1963. 277 p. 

In dealing with the problems created by Communist imperialism, we need adequate intelligence just as we need rockets and bombs, Mr. Dulles asserts. Intelligence activity is an indispensable and perfectly normal part of government. The widespread suspicion generated by CIA is wholly understandable in a free society which frowns upon secrecy in government. But, CIA does not make policy. Its task is to supply information and estimates for those who do make policy.

THE DESIGN OF WEAPONS SYSTEMS FOR AN ARMS CONTROL ENVIRONMENT, by John P. Craven, in Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1, (January 1963) 14-17. "The role of the weapons system designer in creating military strength consistent with the goal of arms control is discussed by the Chief Scientist for the Department of the Navy on the Polaris Program."

DETERRENCE, ARMS CONTROL, AND DISARMAMENT: TOWARD A SYNTHESIS IN NATIONAL SECURITY, by J. David 

Singer. Columbus, Ohio State University Press, 1962. 279 p. (A Publication of the Mershon Center for Education in National Security.)

The author "looks at the broad range of national security problems in the nuclear missile era. He concludes that 'one of the virtues of an integrated security policy is that it encourages us to apply the same . . criteria to disarmament and arms control as we do to deterrence."" 

DISARMAMENT POLICY AND THE PENTAGON, by Betty Goetz Lall, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 7 (September 

1964) 37-40. "In the United States. . . in the complex area of disarmament and arms control, the role of the military is less defined and less well understood, but no less real." The author discusses the Pentagon's role in disarmament and arms control, commenting that on many occasions the working rela tionships between the U.S. Arms Control and Dis armament Agency and military and defense officials are satisfactory, the rapport excellent, and the criticisms and exchanges of data constructive. "But there are some weaknesses that should be corrected."

THE LEGACY OF HIROSHIMA, by 

Edward Teller and Allen Brown. New York, Doubleday, 1962. 325 p. 

Dr. Teller, "father of the H-bomb, believes that a nuclear armaments race is not likely to lead to an all-out nuclear war

The U.S. could sur vive a nuclear attack, if it were properly prepared.

He calls for an active civil defense program, the build-up of U.S. defense to an invincible secondstrike capability and development of plans for limited nuclear war. In the long-run the author calls for the establishment of a 'lawful and prosperous community of nations to ensure peace'." 766-250-65- 1

THE MCNAMARA STRATEGY, by William 

W. Kaufmann. New York, Harper and Row, 1964. 339 p.

The nature of the changes in the U.S. national security affairs during the past three years and the role played by the Secretary of Defense, Robert 

S. McNamara to bring them about. The chapters are titled: (1) Prelude to Revolution; (2)

The Search for Options; (3) Dialogue with 

NATO; (4) The Long View; (5) Cost and Effec tiveness; (6) Capitol Controversies; (7) Tests of Effectiveness; (8) Agenda for Action. The book includes information on the various aspects of the Arms Control and Disarmament policies of the United States. In the Epilogue the author brings out the close and fruitful collaboration that existed between President Kennedy and Mr. McNamara, and calls it "a unique relationship in the history of American defense policy." As witness to this close collaboration in national strategy he presents Mr. McNamara's speech given by him to the Economic Club of New York on November 18, 1963, just four days before the President was murdered.

NATIONAL SECURITY; POLITICAL, MILITARY AND ECONOMIC STRATEGIES IN THE DECADE AHEAD, ed. by David M. 

Abshire and Richard V. Allen. New York,

Frederick A. Praeger, 1963. 1039 p. (Published for the Hoover Institution On War, Revolution, and Peace.) 

During 23-25 January 1963, the Center for 

Strategic Studies, Georgetown University, spon sored a conference on National Security. The 

Conference was an inquiry into the relationship of strategic and economic issues and it also brought to the conference table specialists in international politics and science. After the Conference, many participants updated or revised their papers, and these, along with panel discussions, are contained in this volume. The topics ranged from Sino- 

Soviet strategy and the political requirements for U.S. strategy to military and economic strategies and aid and trade with underdeveloped nations.

STRATEGIC THINKING AND STUDIES, by Lt. Col. Charles M. Ferguson, Jr., in Military 

Review, v. 44, no. 4 (April 1964) 9-24.

The importance of strategic thinking and studies to the national security of the United States and the extent of contribution by the military profession. To this end examines both the organization and the scope in the U.S. military establishment for strategic thinking and strategic studies, including the set up for arms control: Points out the shortcomings, and suggests some corrective measures. 

b. Role and Effects of Arms Control and 

Disarmament

DISARMAMENT: WEAPON OF CON- QUEST, by Robert Morris. New York, Book mailer, 1963. 148 p.

The author says that this book "is written in the spirit which prompted Socrates, who by constant probing upheld the right to know where he was, and where he was going." He takes a critical look at U.S. disarmament policies from the premise that the Soviets are waging a new World War against the United States and our allies and that to them disarmament is a weapon of conquest and not of peace and that accordingly many of our disarmament policies are unrealistic and have tended to diminish the military strength of the United States.

HOW ARMS-CONTROL DOCTRINE CAN AFFECT U.S. STRATEGY, by Maj. Gen. Dale 

O. Smith, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 45, no. 

12 (December 1962) 71-73 plus. "Attitudes toward arms-control measures are having a strong influence on U.S. military posture and strategy planning. A case in point is the belief that the manned bomber is essentially a firststrike, and hence destabilizing, weapon system.

Whether this is actually true has thus far not been publicly examined in the full context of national policy. Here this question is analyzed by ... [the author]."

NATIONAL STRATEGY, SECURITY, AND ARMS CONTROL, by William C. Foster, in The Department of State Bulletin, v. 49, no.

1274 (25 November 1963) 824-829. 

An address on 31 October before the Southwest

Conference on Arms Control convened by the Hu man Relations Center, University of Oklahoma, 

Norman, Okla., at which time the Director, U.S.

Arms Control and Disarmament Agency stated: 

"Within the last 6 to 8 years the problems of arms control and disarmament have come to be broadly recognized in the defense community as an integral part of our overall national security program." 

In his address Mr. Foster considers this "greatly increased importance of arms control planning in our national security program," and reviews the changes which technology has imposed on our national defense strategy. 2

SOME THOUGHTS ON THE BALANCE OF POWER UNDER GENERAL AND COM- PLETE DISARMAMENT, by Michael Mac donald and others. Menlo Park, Calif., Stanford 

Research Institute, 1961. 87 p.

This study was prepared for the Office of the 

Assistant Secretary of Defense for International Security Affairs. This paper examines some pos sible implication of the balance of power in a general and complete disarmament environment, with specific attention to the nature of the conflict as it might occur and to the influence of the new environment upon alliances. The general objective of the paper is "to illumine some possible symmetries and asymmetries in the balance of power which could occur and their possible consequences." The authors first discuss some possible implications of a GCD environment upon the concept of the balanced power and the constituents of power. They next explore some of the possible ramifications of GCD upon the mechanism for creating and maintaining the balance of power, namely, alliance systems. Finally, they consider the nature of conflict, primarily military conflict, which might still occur within the constraints of a GCD world.

STRATEGIC AMBIGUITY, ASYMMETRY AND ARMS CONTROL, by H. Averch. Santa 

Monica, Calif., Rand Corp., 1963. 34 p. (RM- 3426-PR.) "An examination of some novel arms control questions that have arisen during Project RAND research on central war strategies. It is pointed out that some types of arms control agreement may be used as a tool by the Soviet Union to achieve strategic objectives undesirable to the United States, even if there is adequate inspection and verification that the terms of the agreement are being carried out. Assuming conditions between the United States and the USSR similar to those existing at present, it may be difficult to distinguish between a case in which the Soviet Union seeks superiority and a case in which both sides seek stable deterrence. Furthermore, it is rational for the Soviet Union, if it seeks superiority, to engage in some types of arms control agreements with adequate inspection, provided some of the existing Soviet force becomes superfluous as new weapons are phased in." 

2. Strategy a. Miscellaneous Aspects THE AGE OF DETERRENCE, by George 

E. Lowe. Boston, Little, Brown and Company, 1964. 324 p.

This book "is a history of the development of strategic theory from 1952 to 1963. . . . It can be considered an intellectual history of strategic ideas advanced by the American defense establishment during the 1950's and early 1960's." The author states that he has "tried to select representative statements from both Utopian and 

Traditionalist viewpoints." With selected bibliography.

MISSILES AND MISSIONS, by Lt. Cmdr. 

Wayne Hughes, Jr., in U.S. Naval Institute Proceedings, v. 9, no. 12 (December 1964) 38-43.

Lately naval circles have been resounding with the theory of a "new mission" for the Navy, expounded in 1954 by Prof. Samuel P. Huntington, who contended that the new role consisted of fighting not for the sea, but from the sea. The Polaris submarine has bolstered the "new mission" argument, being on station not to control the seas, but to extend control beyond the seas, and over the enemy's land mass in support of national policy. 

Tracing the development of this new theory, the author warns that control of land masses by naval operations is not a new concept, but that it always has, and will continue to depend on the capability to control the seas. To ignore this fact would be to invite disaster.

SOME REFLECTIONS ON COUNTER- FORCE AND ARMS CONTROL, by John B. 

Phelps, in Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (April 1963) 145–148.

Secretary of Defense Robert S. McNamara made a speech at the University of Michigan, June 1962, which raised again the "counterforce question," and in which he tried to explain some Administration views on independent, relatively small, national nuclear forces. The author states that a decisive strategic counterforce capability is probably unattainable, given a number of practical constraints. He thinks that reacting to our strategy the Russians will try to enhance the invulnerability of their weapons. They will hold up their ends of the arms race. And, so long as we try to maintain a decisive strategic counterforce capability, we can be counted on to hold up ours. This rules out for the foreseeable future any numerical disarmament and probably also any significant arms control in the area of strategic weapons.

THINKING ABOUT THE UNTHINK- ABLE, by Herman Kahn. New York, Horizon 

Press, 1962. 254 p.

The author "presents in less technical terms a wide range of alternative national strategies. 

Since we may fail in our most urgent efforts to solve our problems by peaceful means 'we must face all the possibilities. We cannot wish them away.' He argues that only through 'world government' can we achieve a reasonable degree of safety and he discusses ways in which it may develop." b. Nuclear Weapons

ESCALATION-PERIL OR POPPYCOCK! 

in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 45, no. 6 (June 

1962) 81-82 plus. "Have we become so fascinated with the question of escalation, so involved with the mystique of deterrence, so convinced that the use of our power-should deterrence fail-would doom our planet that we will fatally weaken our nuclear capability which is the single factor that in the last analysis can cause the enemy to pause long enough to ask himself: Wouldn't I rather be Red than dead?"

THE FOUR PARADOXES OF NUCLEAR STRATEGY, by Hans J. Morgenthau, in The 

American Political Science Review, v. 58, no. 1 (March 1964) 23-35. "The nuclear age has ushered in a novel period of history 

There exists a gap between what we think about our social, political, and philosophic problems and the objective conditions which the nuclear age has created. This contradiction between our modes of thought and action. . . and the objective conditions of our existence has engendered four paradoxes in our nuclear strategy: the commitment to the use of force, nuclear or otherwise, paralyzed by the fear of having to use it; the search for a nuclear strategy which would avoid the predictable consequences of nuclear war; the pursuit of a nuclear armaments race joined with attempts to stop it; the pursuit of an alliance policy which the availability of nuclear weapons has rendered obsolete.” 

LIMITED WAR FOR UNLIMITED GOALS, by Col. Albert P. Sights, Jr., in Air 

University Quarterly Review, v. 13, no. 3 (Spring 

1962) 38-42. "As the atomic arms race continues .. the question must be faced as to what are appropriate objectives for military power in the present era."

In this reappraisal of strategy, the author begins with a review of its historical origins. The author states that "the strategy of winning the nuclear war hopefully would impose a rapid attrition of the enemy forces, but nevertheless a considerable number of his bombs and missiles would be expected to reach targets in this country. Since this is undesirable, we must consider how the enemy might be deterred from launching a nuclear attack while at the same time we are methodically reducing his capability and incentive to do so."

THE PROPOSAL TO USE NUCLEAR WEAPONS OVER ONE'S OWN TERRI- TORY, by Thornton Read. Princeton, N.J., 

Princeton University, Center of International 

Studies, 1962. 41 p. "This paper examines a proposed doctrine for tactical nuclear war according to which either side in an interstate war could use [defensively] nuclear weapons in its own territory, that is on the territory it held when the war began. Such a proposal has been put forward several times-by 

Admiral Buzzard, Leo Szilard, and Paul Nitze among others--but it has been largely ignored in the recent detailed analyses of tactical nuclear warfare, which on the whole have been unfavorable to the idea of using tactical nuclear weapons to compensate for an inferiority in conventional combat forces."

STRATEGY AND THE ATLANTIC ALLI- ANCE; A CRITIQUE OF UNITED STATES 

DOCTRINE, by Hedley Bull. Princeton, N.J., Center of International Studies, 1964. 42 p. 

(Policy Memorandum No. 29.) 

In the foreword to the critique, Klaus Knorr, Director of the Center, states: "Dr. Hedley Bull of the London School of Economics and Political 

Science, who wrote the following critique of

United States strategic doctrine concerning 

NATO-or, rather, of the political premises, usually unstated, on which United States advocacy of this doctrine rests-was a visiting research associate at the Center of International Studies from February 1963 to January 1964. Dr. Bull's criticism is remarkably trenchant but, even if not necessarily commanding agreement in all particulars, is surely right in an approaching strategic doctrine from the viewpoint of the basic political realities of any alliance, including NATO. We are convinced that this contribution is especially valuable in provoking thought on the structure of

political purposes in the alliance which strategy is expected to serve." The elements of the critique are: the control of nuclear weapons; the defense of Western Europe; and the arms control policy. 

Dr. Bull's thesis is that the United States strategic doctrine with regard to NATO (in each of the above elements) is defective "and that where the European allies have dissented from it there is nothing fundamentally unsound in the strategic reasoning that had led them to do so." c. "Overkill"

THE AGE OF OVERKILL, by Max Lerner. 

New York, Simon and Schuster, 1962. 329 p.

The author introduces the book with the following: "It is meant as a picture of the world today, which the American people and their leadership must confront, along with the leaders and people of the free-world and Communist powerclusters, and the leaders of the new and largely uncommitted nations of Asia and Africa. I have tried to depict the forces which have broken loose in that world, and what they are doing to the contours of the familiar landscape of world politics. And 

I have tried to suggest how a world leadership, at once prudent and courageous, imaginative and sane, may be able to grapple with these forces."

He points out that his book is not descriptive but analytical. The chapters and some of their sub jects are: I. THE AGE OF OVERKILL (The Deterrence World; The Passing of Classical World Politics); II. POWER CENTER AND POWER CLUSTER ("Imperialism Is Dead, 

Long Live Empire"; Neutralisms and Ghosts;

Grandeurs and Miseries of Empire); III. 

GRAND DESIGN AND POLITICAL RE- LIGION (The Two Faces of Revolution; Armed Doctrine and Political Religion); IV. THE UNDERDEVELOPED WORLD (The Identity 

Revolutions; Overtake and Take-over;

Self-De termination: Wilson Versus Lenin); V. THE VIABLE SOCIETY, ELITE, ELAN, ETHOS (The Ordeal of Leadership; The Education of Democratic Elite; The Death and Life of Civilizations); VI. BEYOND THE POWER PRINCI- 

PLE (Negotiation Without Illusions-on page 264 the author makes the following observation: "If we can't trust them to make or hold to agreements on our territorial and political quarrels," the 

Americans (or the Russians) may say, "how can we expect them to make or hold to agreements on arms control, or on anything?"; The New Federalists; Fear and Conscience in Atomic Man. Includes a note on reading.

KILL AND OVERKILL; THE STRATEGY OF ANNIHILATION, by Ralph E. Lapp. New 

York, Basic Books, 1962. 197 p. 

"The author . . . estimates that the U.S. has enough nuclear explosives to overkill the Soviet 

Union at least 25 times. He feels that the only defense in which mankind can find any real, enduring hope is disarmament. In the meanwhile, he recommends that the U.S. stop building nuclear bomb material and lists other steps leading toward a peaceful world." Dr. Lapp maintains "that the giant bombs have grown beyond being instruments of warfare and have become psychological weapons-a means of striking terror, of intimidating whole populations." He further states that "Piling up more tons of bomb material, more thousands of bombs, does not add to the national security of the United States." Concerning deterrence, Dr. Lapp thinks that "It has generated an endless arms race and created increasingly dangerous forces of instability." The "nuclear game" played by the U.S. and U.S.S.R. "explains the efforts of Britain and France to develop their own nuclear force. In part their motive is to regain their position as major powers in the world. . . also . . . to have a stronger say about the starting of a nuclear war." Dr. Lapp adds that "Each new country joining the nuclear club multiplies the chances of an outbreak of nuclear war." Dr. Lapp concludes by discussing some steps the U.S. could take which in his estimation would improve, rather than endanger, our security (e.g., revising our missile programs to make it truly a retaliatory force rather than one which appears to be prepared for a first strike, "calling off" the production of bomb material, etc.). He also lists some proposals for ending the arms race. With notes and references.

THE MYTH OF OVERKILL; A CRITIQUE OF "A STRATEGY FOR AMERICAN SECU- 

RITY," by Amron H. Katz, in Air Force and 

Space Digest, v. 47, no. 2 (February 1964) 31-41.

This article is an examination and a refutation of theories and proposals made by Prof. Seymour 

Melman of Columbia University, and is divided into the following parts: I. The Road to Meltown-What Does Melman Say?; II. GIRF- The Guaranteed Invulnerable Retaliatory Force- Who's In Charge?; III. Saving Money and What To Do With It; IV. The Economic Argument— 

LO

Can We Survive a Cut in Defense Spending?; 

V. Behind Melman-A Basis for His Beliefs and Actions; VI. After Criticism, What?-A Positive Program. With references. 

"OVERKILL” AND THE MILITARY BUDGET: A REVIEW OF A STRATEGY FOR AMERICAN SECURITY-AN ALTER- NATIVE TO THE 1964 MILITARY BUDGET, by Robert A. Levine. Santa Monica, Calif., Rand 

Corporation, 1964. 16 p. "United States military policy, because of the awesome nature of the questions involved, has long attracted the attention of politically minded intellectuals. Many have tried to contribute; some have succeeded. In recent months, there has bubbled to the political surface a new group of defense-policy activists which has tried harder than most and perhaps succeeded less. A team of college professors, headed by Seymour Melman of

Columbia has devoted itself both in writing and active lobbying, to the proposition that the United

States has the capacity to 'overkill' the Soviet Union some hundreds of times and that therefore the military budget is far too high and can be cut by amounts ranging up to 40 per cent ($22 billion). 

In their lobbying, they have had an odd type of temporary success. They have intrigued a small band of Senators and Representatives of two markedly different tendencies-liberals in the antimilitaristic tradition who are shocked by the height of the defense budget when so many of the government's non-military domestic and foreign programs are being skimped on for lack of funds; and conservatives in the anti-governmental tradition, who are shocked by the height of the total budget, and who would be pretty unlikely to substitute any civilian spending for any military program cut. This coalition seems unstable in the extreme, but it has at least had the effect of stirring up the Department of Defense and causing it to defend its programs against the bifurcated onslaught. 

Since a little stirring up is probably good for any government agency, this effect, at least, might be termed a virtue. But, looking at the writings of the group, such a stirring up may be their only virtue. Intellectually, the whole package is byand-large either incorrect or irrelevant and is occasionally incoherent." Mr. Levine then goes on with an analysis of the report appearing in title (by Seymour Melman, Terence McCarthy, Otto 

Feinstein, Edwin Lieuwen, John Ullman, William 

Vickrey, and Dr. Benjamin Spock) and finds both the arguments presented as well as the statistics quoted in support of the argument "either bad or irrelevant" and "superficial," and that the whole concept of "overkill" ignores the strategic objectives of the United States, and that the proposed budgetary cuts by the proponents of the "overkill" philosophy are not only unrealistic, but would be harmful and crippling to the national defense of the United States. Mr. Levine concludes: "...

It may be that the United States military budget can be cut by $22 billion, it may be that it can be cut by $40 billion. It may be that, as some legislators believe, who have read this document and feel that it is on the right track but overstates, that the budget can be cut by $4-5 billions. The main point made by this review is not that the budget should be untouched, increased, or cut; it is that the methods used for the major portion of 

A Strategy for American Security cannot reason ably be used to justify any military strategy of any military budget. Those who sincerely believe that cuts should be made had better find a stronger analytical reed to lean on than 'overkill'." 

"OVERKILL": THEORIES AND IMPLI- CATIONS, by E. H. Hartsook, in Orbis, v. 7, no. 4 

(Winter 1964) 709-718.

The author in this analysis examines some implications of the "overkill" theory as it relates to defense strategy and planning assumptions, to procurement and research and development, and to political considerations. "A strategy for national defense is not as simple as the 'overkill' thesis would make it appear.

The needs for flexibility and diversity in strategic responses, for constant technological improvement and for military superiority put a high price on national security. But these are the factors that make U.S. strategy effective." 

A RESPONSE TO PROFESSOR MELMAN 

AND "OVERKILL," by Murray Green. Washington, United States Air Force, 1964. 69 p.

Dr. Green is with the Research Analysis Division, Office Administrative Assistant to the Secre tary of the Air Force.

His response is to a report titled A STRATEGY FOR AMERICAN SE- CURITY-AN ALTERNATIVE TO THE 1964 MILITARY BUDGET, by Seymour Melman and others (New York, Lee Service, 1963) an abstract of which appears elsewhere in this bibliography. 

Dr. Green brings out the following salient points in his paper: That Professor Melman questions the annual expenditure of over $50 billions on de-

-b fense, most of it to accumulate more sophisticated means of destroying the enemy when the US already has an overkill of 125 times for every one of 

2,000 major cities around the globe, or an overkill of 1,250 times for each of 140 principal cities in the U.S.S.R., and that Professor Melman proposes two alternative budgets (1) cutback of present forces by $20 billions each year; or (2) a “finite deterrent" budget to reduce procurement in FY 1964 from $16.7 billion to $1 billion, totally eliminating R&D; and that basic research should be transferred from DOD to NSF. Also, that "Melman's Mathematics" show the US in possession of 3,400 strategic delivery weapons yielding 22,000 

MT, but that 600 B-47's which Melman says yield 6,000 MT will be phased from inventory by FY 1966. Dr. Green notes that no allowance is made for the entire B-52 and B-58 force in "soft" configurations, but that one must assume that if there is a Soviet first strike in a general war, at least 50 percent of this force in non-alert status may be written off. Dr. Green charges that computation of U.S. Navy strategic forces is unsupported by published material which Melman pur- ports to cite, and that tabulation of Soviet strategic capability misstates two reputable sources. Dr. 

Green finds that Professor Melman's greatest error is conceptual, not statistical, because the latter's thesis rests on the "Hiroshima Equivalent" that is, 100,000 deaths per 20 KT as a yardstick for casualties in a modern nuclear war. Dr. Green asks why not the Nagasaki equivalent, a more powerful weapon which caused half the casualties, and would reduce Melman's estimates by 2% or more?

Dr. Green shows that if Professor Melman's thesis is accepted the US will need "just 2 B-52's," each to carry one 20 MT weapon, "and no other strategic forces" and that 40 MT according to Melman's ratio "will dispose of 200 million people" which is the present population of the U.S.S.R. 

A STRATEGY FOR AMERICAN SECU- 

RITY, by Seymour Melman and others, in Saturday Review, v. 46, no. 18 (4 May 1963) 10–17. "A task force of independent specialists, arguing that U.S. military policy has reached a point of diminishing returns, offers a blueprint for renewing American leadership." 1. Military Power and 

Money, by Seymour Melman; 2. Alternatives to 

Armaments, by John E. Ullmann; 3. $22 Billion to Civilian Use, by William Vickrey; 4. Security and Instability, by Otto Feinstein; 5. Latin Amer ica and Military Aid, by Edwin Lieuwen. The five sections in the article (an abridged form of a report published by Lee Service, Dept. St., New 

York, N.Y., 1963) attempt to show that the U.S. has the nuclear capacity to "overkill" the Soviet

Union many times over and that this excess power is unnecessary and very costly, and that a second look at the strategic needs of the United States would allow a budget cut of $22 billion, and the resulting savings applied to domestic civilian programs and to foreign aid. In support of their contention the authors present tables that show: a comparison of US and Soviet strategic nuclear weapons systems operational in 1963 with their total yield and number of delivery vehicles; three possible (US) national defense budgets for FY 

1964; illustrations of how first-year military budget reductions of $22 billions could be used effectively to create economic opportunity. In addition to the above section the article includes inserts by Terence McCarthy and by Benjamin 

Spock, titled respectively "The Declining Dol lar" and "The Future: Can We Take the Initia tive?" In the latter Dr. Spock points out that the insecurity caused by the anxieties of the Cold 

War is undermining the American children.

WORLD AFTER NEW YEAR'S.-AMER- ICA: MINDS IN FERMENT, by N. Polyanov. 

(Izvestia, January 5, 1964, p. 2), in Current Digest of the Soviet Press, v. 16, no. 1 (29 January 

1964) pp. 18-19. Condensed text.

A commentator for Izvestia sets forth his impressions of life and trends in the United States as they appeared to him during a "three-week trip through America." Among his sociological and political observations is the following which is pertinent to this bibliography and the subject of "overkill" controversy: ". . . It is the youth of

America, workers, students, and farmers, who have started thinking about the fact that the earth has grown smaller in the space age and that there are two worlds on the planet, between which weapons must not ring and nuclear explosions must not blunder. It is the thoughtful America, with many of whose representatives we could easily find a common language. Prof. Seymour Melman of Columbia University has opened at his own risk a seminar on the 'economic consequences' of disarmament. Four days a week, jointly with his doctoral candidates, he studies this problem, calculates, explores various possibilities, looks for and finds hundreds of answers to hundreds of questions on what would happen to America if a

stable peace should break out tomorrow. During the remaining two days of the week, the professor flies from New York to Washington. There he tries to persuade Congressmen to consider the fact that it is better to adjust the American economy to a world without weapons and wars . . . . Thus, two political poles in America gradually took shape before us. At one of them are madmen. 

They have not laid down their arms 

d. Limited Wars LIMITED WAR IN THE NUCLEAR AGE, by Morton H. Halperin. New York, John Wiley, 

1963. 191 p. (Written under the auspices of the Center for International Affairs, Harvard University.) "Though the policy dilemmas faced by the

United States in using limited force provided the impetus for this study" the author states that his aim throughout "has been to grapple with the theoretical problems of an era in which limited means are used despite the fact that both the United States and the Soviet Union have vastly greater means of destruction, and the latter has far-ranging objectives." In his evaluations he combines both the past experience of limited war in nuclear age (e.g. Korea), as well as the theory about the role of force in the nuclear age, in order to explain past events which involved the use of force with the Soviet Union and the United States on opposite sides. In conclusion the author offers some recommendations for American policy.

Chapter 5 is titled ARMS CONTROL AND LOCAL WAR. Annotated bibliography. 

3. Military Posture a. Miscellaneous Aspects ANTIMISSILE DEFENSE TAKES 

SHAPE, by James Holahan, in Military Review, v. 43, no. 8 (August 1963) 60-65.

This article was digested from Space/Aeronautics, April 1963. During the next five years or so we can expect to see the development of the first family of antimissiles capable of destroying most types of attacking missiles in flight. The principal roles in the increased antimissile effort belong to the Department of Defense, the Army, the Advanced Research Project Agency, the contractors of these agencies, and to a much lesser extent, the Navy. Plans and systems are discussed.

DEFENSE AGAINST BALLISTIC MIS- SILES, by Freemen J. Dyson, in Bulletin of the 

Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 6 (June 1964) 12-18.

The central problem discussed is whether in any foreseeable circumstances the deployment of Bal listic Missile Defense (BMD) by the U.S. would be necessary or advantageous to our security. The decision to deploy BMD must be considered as an indivisible one. The technical, military, and political factors involved in the decision cannot be clearly separated. However, for convenience sake the author makes such a separation in his discussion.

MILITARY RECORD OF CBR/ATOMIC HAPPENINGS. 1963 THEME: EMERGING THREATS TO EXISTING AUTHORITY; 3 COMBAT READINESS. London, Aviation Studies (International) Limited, 1963. 30 p. 

A survey of combat readiness of the U.S. Army, Air Force, Navy, and Marine Corps; also some data on US preparedness to monitor radiological fallout on Federal, State, and local levels. Also included are various statements by the Secretary of 

Defense dealing with the defense and security of the United States. To the question "Do you think that the improvements that you have made in the past 2 years in our general purposes forces were the decisive factor in our facing up to Khrushchev's challenge in Cuba" Mr. McNamara stated: "Certainly the fact that our general purposes forces were in better condition to face up, not only to the challenge in Cuba, but to any other simultaneous challenge which might have been posed to us, was an important factor in the Cuban affair. 

If there was a single decisive factor it was the U.S. determination to use force on the Cuban issue, if necessary. The improvement in our general purposes forces was an element which helped make that determination credible to the Soviets."

NATIONAL SECURITY AND NUCLEAR STRENGTH, by Robert S. McNamara, in Bul letin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 3 (March 

1964) 35-39.

The Secretary of Defense analyzes "U.S. force structure and military strategy to buttress his contention that the defense budget can be cut without affecting U.S. security." This article is a condensed version of his speech before the Economic 

Club of N.Y., November 18, 1963. His remarks are presented together with a summary chart of the budget as later submitted to Congress by 

President Johnson on January 9.

OUR DEFENSE NEEDS; THE LONG VIEW, by Roswell L. Gilpatric, in Foreign Af fairs, v. 42, no. 3 (April 1964) 366–378.

The U.S. may face a dilemma over the extent and use of its military power in the event the cold war with the Soviet Union eases before major steps are taken toward general disarmament. In 

January of this year President Johnson in his

State of the Union message pledged that there would be maintained the "margin of military safety and superiority" now possessed by the U.S.

At the same time, he promised new steps toward the control and eventual abolition of arms and undertook that, even without agreement, the U.S. would "not stockpile arms beyond our needs or seek an excess of military power that could be provocative as well as wasteful." The author feels there is no inconsistency between these two policies, and discusses the kind of military establishment which might be appropriate for the U.S. should there develop a continued easing of the level of East-West tension "such as we have seen during the past year." 99 

THE POSSIBLE UTILITY OF THE U.S. 

OVERSEAS BASE STRUCTURE, by Harold 

W. Rood, Menlo Park, Calif., Stanford Research 

Institute, 1962. 80 p. (Technical Report no. 34.) "This is a study of the political, economic, and military uses of an overseas base system for the support of a national policy of flexible response. 

The paper includes a résumé of the role played by American overseas bases in the past, a summary of conceivable uses of overseas bases at various level of conflict, and recommendations for further research to explore the possible development of an overseas base system for the future." Under discussion among other subjects: some arguments which might favor abandonment of the overseas base system (in light of primary Sino-Soviet objectives to isolate the United States from its allies); the application of the U.S. power and influence abroad through the presence of U.S. forces; tendency of the Communists to exploit crises in areas where there are no U.S. forces present; advantages which can accrue to U.S. policy through constant presence of forces in critical or potentially critical areas; and categorization of overseas bases. Includes a listing of problems that should be explored with the view of future development of an overseas base structure. [For more information on this subject see: U.S. overseas bases; present status and future prospects; A Bibliographic Survey. Washington, Headquarters, Department of the Army, January 1963. 133 p. S. Horowitz and others. Derby, Conn., Monarch 

Books, 1964. 190 p. "The author has been covering Washington affairs since the end of World War II, with emphasis on the military. Levine, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 47, no. 11 (November 1964) 89–90 plus. "What are the economic implications of cutbacks in defense spending that would result from a true detente with the Soviet Union? Would such cutbacks produce societal benefits by freeing funds for expenditures on nonmilitary goods and services? 

Or would they bring on economic hardships?

Neither 'classical' nor Keynesian economics provides fully satisfactory answers to such crucial questions, for different sets of reasons. All of which suggests the need for additional analysis and planning

WHERE THE CUTBACK CUTS DEEP, by 

Don Oberdorfer, in The Saturday Evening Post, no. 31 (12 September 1964) 17-21. "To reduce the military budget, the Defense

Department has been closing bases and cancelling armaments contracts-and thereby throwing thousands of people out of work. Today both government and industry have to face a major problem: 

What should be done to regear the great American defense machine to meet the needs of a civilian economy?" This nationwide report attempts to supply some of the answers. 

4. Foreign Policy a. Miscellaneous Aspects NATO: THE ENTANGLING ALLIANCE, by Robert Endicott Osgood. Chicago, The University of Chicago Press, 1962. 416 p. new This "This book is a study of contemporary American foreign policy as it has been transformed by the conditions of the postwar period 

It examines the ... military-strategic issues that challenge the viability of the 

United States . . . instrument of security in the cold war, the North Atlantic Alliance is a study of the role of military power in the nuclear age of the . . . influence of military strategies and capabilities upon international politics. 

In particular, it explores the political and psychological ramifications of the unprecedented task of peacetime military-strategic collaboration in NATO-the interaction of strategic logic with the demands of allied cohesion, political warfare, and diplomacy." The author has not "presented a comprehensive description or analysis of either the military or non-military aspects of America's relationship with her allies." He has tried "only to elucidate those aspects that seem most directly related to NATO's past, present, and future utility as a regional security alliance in the cold war." Partial contents: The Impact of Soviet 

Nuclear Power; Rearmament and Relaxation;

NATO Goes Nuclear; Toward a Strategy of Lim ited Resistance; The Onset of the Missile Age;

The 

Problem of Nuclear Diffusion; The Control of

THE PHILIPPINES CAN'T BE TAKEN 

FOR GRANTED, by Denis Warner, in The Reporter, v. 31, no. 11 (17 December 1964) 30-32.

The Philippines and our bases there will assume more strategic importance as Peking gains nuclear capability. Recent Filipino trade legislation has not contributed to U.S.-Filipino accord. With elections approaching, the U.S. is likely to be used unsparingly as a political whipping-boy. These events should not be interpreted as a reversal in traditionally friendly relations with the U.S. 

Society in the Philippines is freer than any neighboring society, and its democratic government will 10

remain so regardless of the party in power. Reassurances by President Johnson have been welcomed by the Filipinos. In return there will be no problem for U.S. bases in the Philippines.

FOREIGN SHAPING THE FUTURE: 

POLICY IN AN AGE OF TRANSITION, by 

Robert R. Bowie. New York, Columbia University Press, 1964. 118 p. 

What should we do about Europe and how should the U.S. Government be organized for foreign affairs? The author defines our goal as sharing nuclear responsibility in ways which will reinforce both European unity and Atlantic partnership. MLF, the most promising presently feasible step, will evolve in time. He cautions against possible diversions-notably help to national forces either directly or via some loose multinational combination of these forces. The execution of U.S. foreign policy must make clear the separation of function between the President and the Secretary of State.

The author, a member of the White House staff comments on the large topic of the Presidency and the Peace in which he expresses his conviction that the American Presidency has now become the world's best hope of preventing the unexampled catastrophe of general nuclear war. c. Arms Control and Disarmament Aspects AN APPROACH TO PEACE AND OTHER ESSAYS, by H. Stuart Hughes. New York, 

Atheneum, 1962. 204 p.

In the preface the author states: "This volume is an effort to put together in coherent order the dissenting opinions on our country's foreign policy and cultural situation that have been coming to a boil in the back of my mind for the past ten years. 

Some have a measure of emotional detachment; the majority are frankly polemical. They are intended to stimulate controversy-to contribute toward awakening the American people. I fervently hope they will do so." Part I presents a plea for a radical rethinking of foreign policy assumptions (e.g., the strategy of deterrence); Part II assesses the weakening of ideological commitment in Europe today and its consequences for the political solidarity of the West; Part III contends that 

American intellectuals are not living up to their responsibilities and failing to resist "with enough determination the pressures toward cultural banality and the threat of thermonuclear war."

ARMS, ARMS CONTROL, AND FOREIGN 

POLICY, by Richard B. Roberts and Frank T. 

McClure, Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 3 (July 1963) 163–183. "Arms control is discussed within the framework of national goals and foreign policy objectives as an alternative which provides more support for a wider range of policies than does the current emphasis on nuclear weapons in excess of those required for a credible deterrent." Con tents: National Objectives (Russian and U.S. Ob jectives); Strategy and Tactics of Foreign Policy 

(Techniques for Implementation, Alternatives to Military Force); Military Power in the Modern World; The Ephemeral Nature of Strategic Nuclear Superiority (Civil Defense and Anti-Ballistic Missile Systems, The City-Avoidance Doc trine); A Rational Strategic Force; Other Com ponents of Military Power (Russian Needs, U.S. 

Needs, China, India, Middle East, Europe, Restraining Forces for World Order); Excess Arms and the Role of the Arms Control and Disarmament Agency; Selectivity in Armaments and 

Arms Control; etc. 

A BALANCED APPROACH TO DISARM- 

AMENT, by Charles C. Steele, in The Department of State Bulletin, v. 49, no. 1273 (18 November 1963) 793-798.

A statement by the U.S. Representative to the 

General Assembly, made in Committee I, on 29 12

October 1963, at which time the committee began consideration of the question of disarmament.

Mr. Steele first reviews briefly the landmarks of disarmament in the recent past then reviews the road ahead: a first level of agreement to which efforts must be addressed-general and complete disarmament; a second level of agreement to be sought-a series of measures which in negotiations have come to be called collateral measures (initial measures of disarmament which could, if put into effect without delay, improve international security, and prospects for further disarmament progress).

KEEPING LIMITED WAR LIMITED, by 

Maj. Alton R. Wheelock, in Military Review, v. 43, no. 12 (December 1963) 64-69.

Today, weaponry has progressed to the point where true escalation can develop. Today, too, the U.S. is enhancing her capability to engage in limited war. The presumption can only be that 

U.S. foreign policy provides for the willingness to engage or threaten to engage, in limited war. 

However, our knowledge of the problems and constraints of limited war has not increased accordingly. Three principles, properly understood and applied, must be observed to keep a limited war limited: the limited objective, communication of intent, and political primacy. All are necessary and all are interdependent.

UNITED STATES POST WORLD WAR II ARMS CONTROL POLICY, by Col. Robert B. 

Reppa. College Park, University of Maryland, 1964. 88 p. (Unpublished thesis.)

The main body of the paper is mainly chronological, tying in arms control and world historical/ 

scientific developments. Contents: Retreat from the U.N.-1945-1949; Desultory Disarmament

Dialogue-1950-1959; The Soviet Challenge at its 

Peak, 1960-1962; Post Cuba-1963-1964. With appendices and bibliography. 

5. Arms Control and Disarmament-Organizations and Activities MILITARY RECORD OF CBR/ATOMIC HAPPENINGS, 1963 THEME: EMERGING THREATS TO EXISTING AUTHORITY. 

London, Aviation Studies (International) Limited, 1963. 28 p. (1. CONTROLLED RE- SPONSE THEORY.)

ORGANIZATION OF THE OF THE GOVERN- MENT FOR DISARMAMENT, by Frederic Walton Smith. Washington, The American Uni versity, 1962. 137 p. (Unpublished M.A. thesis.)

A description of the activities of the several departments and agencies of the U.S. Government involved in disarmament and arms control efforts, noting "a few of the organizational deficiencies that have accompanied U.S. disarmament efforts in the past and emphasizing those that... have not been adequately corrected in recent years." The author emphasizes the breadth of the disarmament organization and the extent of the role of the Department of Defense in mak ing arms control policy. The disarmament research activities of the Government are examined, and it is found that research has concentrated on technical problems rather than on political issues.

Also notes the coordination among the various governmental agencies. With bibliography.

B. U.S.S.R.

STRATEGIIA, the first comprehensive work on 13

the subject of military strategy published in the Soviet Union since 1926. The reviewer believes this book to be an important document that affords "valuable insights into Soviet strategic thinking." The chapters in the Memorandum are:

LAOS: A CASE STUDY OF COMMUNIST STRATEGY, by Col. Edwin F. Black, in Military 

Review, v. 44, no. 12 (December 1964) 49-59.

Laos, where the Communists are stepping up their efforts, is a key pawn in the cold war chess game for all of SE Asia. The Communists have considered Indochina a single battlefront within a larger SE Asia theater of war ever since they first took up arms against the French in 1945. As a result, they have conducted their military campaigns across geographic boundaries. History will probably look upon Laos as a laboratory where neutrality as a political technique to advance Communist aims was tested. It was in Laos that neutrality as practiced under Communist ground rules was stripped of much of the superficial appeal it once had. Includes information on types of Soviet aid given.

MARITIME ROUTES, PETROL AND SO- VIET STRATEGY, by Ivan Matteo Lombardo, in NATO Letter, v. 12, no. 10 (October 1964) 22-25.

One of the immediate strategic objectives which motivates the Soviet world is to gain control of petrol-so vital to the industrialized free world.

The Soviets want petrol as a means to exert political pressure, while stifling U.S. industrial growth. 

To achieve this end, the Soviet world employs in turn three complementary methods: supply of Soviet petrol to importing countries; disruption of the world petrol market; and gaining of a political hold on present and prospective petrol-producing countries. The Soviet empire has created a formidable submarine fleet and now seeks to eliminate or to neutralize the threat of the free world's naval air bases, while ensuring the availability to itself of bases, airfields and strategic points. With map. 

MILITARY-STRATEGY. 

Voennaia strategiia, ed. by Marshal V. D. Sokolovskii. Moscow,

Voennoe Izdatel'stvo Ministerstva Oborony SSSR, 

1962. 456 p. In Russian.

While the work was edited by Marshal Sokolovsky it was compiled by an impressive list of Soviet General Officers and is perhaps reflective of the current thinking in the Soviet Union as to what constitutes strategy in modern warfare. The book concerns itself mostly with an evaluation of the strategy of the West and the United States, but the compilers also express themselves broadly in many areas of Soviet strategy. Among the subjects covered: strategy and politics; strategy and economics; strategy and the moral-political factor; strategy and military doctrine; the class nature of Soviet military strategy; the contemporary strategy of U.S. and NATO; development of 

Soviet military strategy 1917-1945; the character of contemporary warfare; determinative factors in the make up of the armed forces; methods of conducting warfare (includes a discussion of the utilization of cosmic space for military purposes); preparing the country to ward off aggression (including civil defense); and directing the armed forces (a discussion of the structure of the higher military echelons in Western armies as well as those of the Soviet Union). Conclusions.

MILITARY STRATEGY. Voennaia strate giia, ed. by Marshal V. D. Sokolovskii. 2nd enl. and rev. ed. Moscow, Voennoe Izdatel'stvo Ministerstva Oborony SSSR, 1963. 502 p. In Russian.

OFFENSIVE DOCTRINE OF THE SO- VIET ARMY, by Rudi Riedel, in Military 

Review, v. 42, no. 11 (November 1962) 61-67.

This article was translated and digested from the original, published in Wehrkunde (Federal 

Republic of Germany) May 1962. Soviet doctrine 

14

holds that a future world war will be a protracted war fought in two phases. The first phase will be of short duration and will begin with a massive exchange of nuclear strikes which will destroy the larger cities and industrial centers. When both sides have expended their nuclear fires, a second phase will begin in which the battle will be fought primarily with conventional weapons. 

Therefore, the Soviet land forces are led, trained, organized, and armed for nuclear as well as for conventional warfare. The author discusses: emphasis on the offensive; tactics of the attack; the technique of attack; equipment based on tactics; speed; dispersion and open flanks; nuclear weapons doctrine; combined-arms operations; air defense; etc.

A POSTSCRIPT ON THE SIGNIFICANCE OF THE BOOK "SOVIET MILITARY STRATEGY," by Thomas W. Wolfe. Santa 

Monica, Rand Corporation, July 1962, 51 p. 

(Memorandum RM-3730-PR.) 

"The present Memorandum has been prepared as part of the continuing study of Soviet military doctrine, strategy, and defense policy undertaken for Air Force Project RAND. It follows a pre vious Memorandum by this author on the Soviet book VOENNAIA STRATEGIIA (MILITARY STRATEGY), the first comprehensive work on the subject in the Soviet Union since 1926. The book, by Marshal V. D. Sokolovskii and a group of Soviet authors, is now available in an annotated English translation under the title SOVIET 

MILITARY STRATEGY, which was prepared for publication by this author and two RAND colleagues, Herbert S. Dinerstein and Leon Gouré 

(The RAND Corporation, R-416-PR, April 1963, also published as a book by Prentice-Hall, Inc., Englewood Cliffs, New Jersey, 1963). The question to which the present Memorandum addresses itself has grown out of a discussion of the Sokolovskii book at a panel on Nuclear War and

Soviet Strategy, conducted in Washington, in 

April 1963 by the Center For Strategic Studies of Georgetown University." Among other things, the present Memorandum attempts to answer the question raised since the publication of Sokolov skii's MILITARY STRATEGY, how much of it is propaganda for export and how much of it is genuine Soviet military doctrine. The various sections of the Memorandum deal with: internal communication functions of the book and also the propaganda functions of the book; the book as a document in an on-going debate; and the role of the military in Soviet policy formulation (the area of state policy; the area of strategy formulation; the area of internal politics).

THE ROLE OF MISSILE DEFENSE IN SOVIET STRATEGY, by John R. Thomas, in 

Military Review, v. 44, no. 5 (May 1964) 46–58. "The Soviets have always appreciated the political utility of military arms. Hence the antimissile missile (AMM), like the other major weapon systems, has not been and will not be considered in a political and economic vacuum." The discussion by the author suggests "that the Soviet Union's foreign policy may require massive AMM capabilities, if those capabilities are needed to enhance the image of Soviet military power as a deterrent force. 

The Soviet Union's strategy also may allot a major role to the AMM should her foreign policy misfire, forcing her to fight a war. The AMM will impose a severe strain on the Soviet economy, with resultant internal political consequences. The experience and subjective attitude suggest that the Soviets are likely to pay the price, given their expectations about the AMM'S political and military utility."

SOVIET STRATEGY AT THE CROSS- ROADS, by Thomas W. Wolfe. Santa Monica, 

Calif. The RAND Corp., 1964. 342 p. (RM- 4085-PR.)

This study of Soviet military strategy and doctrine sheds light on Soviet foreign policy in general, offers many insights into the relationship between Soviet military strategy and Soviet disarmament policy; and explores the signs of stress and the relations between the Soviet military and the Communist party. The author identifies and discusses at least 6 areas of controversy going on within the Soviet Union. He identifies two opposing military schools of thought: the "modernists," who sympathized with and were supported by Khrushchev, argue for more radical adaptation of modern technology to military affairs and suggest that this approach might lighten the strain on resources; and the "traditionalists" who are opposed to discarding tried and true concepts and to putting too many eggs in the nuclear basket.

2. Armed Forces and Military Preparedness a. Miscellaneous Aspects ECONOMICS AND STRATEGY: SOVIET CHOICES, by John P. Hardt, in Military Re view, v. 43, no. 11 (November 1963) 16-22.

The Soviet Union may be said to have closely related strategy and economics throughout the period of her first five-year plans, from 1928 to date.

The potentiality of Soviet aggression is the major reason for our assuming their preparedness is a threat for which we must prepare. While estimates of Soviet capability are primarily the province of our intelligence community, an analysis of Soviet resource allocation trends may provide useful collateral information for evaluating their future national security policy. Specifically, this analysis of Soviet resource allocation policy may furnish guidelines on the scope and direction of their preparedness for future augmentation of 

Soviet power.

U.S.S.R., Bulletin, v. 11, no. 7 (July 1964) 19-27.

The political and military significance of the news on 17 April 1964 that Nikita Khrushchev in addition to being First Secretary of the Party 

Central Committee and head of the Soviet government, is also Supreme Commander of the Soviet 

Armed Forces. The author thinks that the Supreme Command was established, either before or during either the Berlin or the Cuba crisis, and assumes that "this concentration of power was intended to regularize not only control over the 'atomic button' but the transfer of power in the

USSR, which is the most important problem in Soviet domestic politics at the present time."

SOVIET GOALS AND MILITARY FORCE, by Herbett S. Dinerstein, in Orbis, v. 5, no. 4 (Winter 1962) 425-436.

The Soviet Union promises to support wars of national liberation and "revolutionary wars against oppression." The problem for Soviet foreign policy is to determine whether such intervention will indeed advance Soviet goals or set them back, and what, if any, risk of nuclear war is involved. This article focuses on that part of the problem in which the political employment of military force plays a role. First, the Soviet possession of nuclear weapons and the threat to use them is treated; second, the advancement of Soviet goals by the dispersion of conventional weapons is discussed.

SOVIET GUIDED MISSILE DEPLOY- 

MENT, by Lt. Col. Truman R. Bowman, in Military Review, v. 42, no. 11 (November 1962) 75–79.

In the years since 1957, "it is more than apparent that the Soviet Union possesses a wide assortment of guided missiles. The Soviets have fired missiles from Central Asia to the mid-Pacific; they have missiles capable of firing shorter ranges." 

According to Khrushchev, the Soviets possess guided missiles which can deliver nuclear warheads at short, medium, and long ranges. "To an apprehensive world, the validity of this remark is only too apparent. What is not widely appreciated, though, is Soviet doctrine for the deployment of guided missiles and the organizations or echelons that control their deployment." This the author discusses and illustrates.

FORMS, in Interavia, v. 17, no. 11 (November 

1962) 1460-1461.

IN in the Baltic and the English Channel." In practically all cases, the vessels in question are known types which have been provided with new armament to enable them to fulfill additional roles.

STRATEGIC ROCKETS, by Marshal Nikolai 

I. Drylov, in Military Review, v. 44, no. 12 (December 1964) 33-35.

The author, Commander-in-Chief of Soviet strategic rocket forces, claims that the tremendous achievements of heavy industry have allowed the USSR to create a powerful base on which to build enough nuclear weapons to deter any aggressor.

The new series of nuclear warheads of from 50 to 100 MT and the new types of super-powerful missiles tested during the spring of 1963 enable the U.S.S.R. to annihilate whole countries with a few nuclear weapons. He states that automatic guidance systems completely independent of groundguidance equipment make strategic missiles invulnerable in flight. It is impossible to take measures against the rocket, either in flight or when it enters the enemy's antiballistic missile defense zone.

Soviet strategic missiles are relatively easy to use and can be fired literally in minutes, even from unprepared field launching pads. (Digested from 

Survival, Gt. Britain, January-February 1964.) 

b. Military Budgets and Expenditures THE ENIGMA OF SOVIET DEFENSE EX- PENDITURES, by Lynn Turgeon, in The Jour nal of Conflict Resolution, v. 8, no. 2 (June 1964) 116-120. THE SOVIET MILITARY BUDGET, by 

Timothy Sosnovy, in Foreign Affairs, v. 42, no. 3 (April 1964) 487-494. "The great development of modern weapons of mass destruction has caused enormous changes in military science including military doctrine and military strategy. However, these changes have not affected the concept of the military budget. 

Today, as in the past, the military budget, approved by appropriate organs of the state, indicates to a certain extent the defense effort of the nation. On the basis of the analysis. [made in this article], it appears that, as a percentage of 

Gross National Product (for the U.S.S.R.

national income), the Soviet military budget is about double that of the United States." • • 

SOVIET MILITARY OUTLAYS SINCE 

1955, by Abraham S. Becker. Santa Monica,

Calif., The Rand Corp., 1964. 106 p. (Memorandum RM-3886-PR.)

Surveys and elaborates the information available in the open literature on Soviet military outlays since 1955. The starting point is the explicit 

Soviet state budget allocation to "defense." The conclusion is reached that additional military outlays, over and above the "defense" allocation, may be presumed to exist; the rest of the Memorandum is devoted to the estimation of concealed expenditures and the evaluation of these estimates. With statistical tables, charts, and graphs. 

c. Civil Defense CIVIL DEFENSE IN THE SOVIET UNION, by Leon Gouré. Berkeley, University of California Press, 1962. 207 p. "The main purpose of this study is to inquire into the doctrine of Soviet civil defense and describe the actual program that is being carried out, as well as to relate both doctrine and program to the more general attitudes and beliefs they reflect. 

The material and the discussion may also permit some insight into likely Soviet reactions should Western nations launch any large-scale civil defense programs of their own." Concludes that the available evidence leaves no doubt that the Soviet leaders are serious about their civil defense. "Basic to an undertaking of this kind is the belief that major wars may occur in the future. This conviction was probably strongest in Moscow at the time when the Soviet Union did not possess nuclear weapons and had not yet recovered from the weakening effects of World War II. Since 

19

then the Soviet leaders have been maintaining more and more emphatically that, with their growing power to deter the West from aggression, the probability of a total war had declined correspondingly, yet they are obviously not prepared to rely on this deterrent capability exclusively and under all circumstances. In their view, a danger of war remains as long as East-West struggle continues, even though it may be progressively reduced as Soviet military power grows and the number of Communist countries increases. More over, they cannot be certain that the threat of war will continue to recede, for they are doubtless aware that the stability of any deterrent balance may be considerably affected by technological changes, and depends constantly on the military programs and posture of both sides, on alliance systems, the reliability and safety of retaliatory forces, and many other factors. Thus, despite the current thesis that total war is no longer inevitable, Soviet leaders regard the possibility that it may nevertheless occur as sufficiently great to justify preparing for it. Hence they have made a considerable investment in civil defense." Illus trations. Bibliography. Appended: Outline of the 14-hour Civil Defense Training Course for the Soviet Population 1958-1960; Outline of the 

18-hour Training Course for the Soviet Population (1960-1961).

THE RESOLUTION OF THE SOVIET CONTROVERSY OVER CIVIL DEFENSE, by Leon Gouré. Santa Monica, Calif., Rand Corporation, 1962. 42 p. (Memorandum RM-3223– PR.) "Describes some recent development in the Soviet civil defense program. It shows the differences between foreign and domestic Soviet propaganda on civil defense, and describes a debate among the Soviet leaders on the value of civil defense a debate that was resolved at the May 

1962 Congress of DOSAAF (the civil volunteer organization). Recent vigorous endorsement of

DOSAAF by Party and press may be one indication of impending changes in Soviet domestic and foreign policies."

THE ROLE OF CIVIL DEFENSE IN SO- VIET STRATEGY, by Leon Gouré. Santa 

Monica, Calif., Rand Corporation, 1963. 28 p. 

(Memorandum RM-3703–PR.) The memorandum describes, primarily on the basis of recent open Soviet publications and statements on strategy, the Soviet military's views on the role of civil defense in a future war, especially in the light of Soviet concepts of a protracted conflict. "For a number of years Soviet military leaders and writers have publicly declared civil defense to be an integral and important part of Soviet defense capability and a factor in Soviet strategy. Soviet interest in civil defense arises from a concept of war in which the opponent's morale and industrial capabilities are regarded as major targets for strategic attack. The purpose of such attack is to break the enemy's will to resist and to deprive him of the means of continuing the struggle by destroying his capacity to produce new weapons and military equipment. Morale and new defense production, according to the Soviet view, thus become critical factors in determining the outcome of protracted war. The possibility that a general war may become protracted and the need to prepare for such a war have been increasingly stressed in Soviet military literature. In short, Soviet opinion is that the successful outcome of a protracted war depends, among other things, on the uninterrupted functioning of society and the state, including the pursuit and attainment of nuclear superiority over the enemy, during the course of the conflict itself. Civil defense not only helps avoid the breakdowns of morale and social cohesion under the first nuclear blows, but, in connection with a pre-emptive attack and active defense measures, also ensures the preservation of public morale and the continued production of weapons and equipment during subsequent hostilities. Although some Soviet military leaders believe that a general war may be a short one, they appear to have such confidence in the possibility of a long war as to prepare for it at least to the extent that their investment in civil defense program, which has been in effect for over a decade, has placed considerable stress on measures designed to protect industrial and administrative personnel, to ensure the continuity of industrial production under war conditions, and to reduce the shock and casualties among the populations.' 

3. Foreign Policy a. Miscellaneous Aspects 

CEMA'S INFLUENCE ON SOVIET POL- ICIES IN EASTERN EUROPE, by Robert S. 

Jaster, in World Politics, v. 14, no. 3 (April 1962) 505-518.

The Council of Economic Mutual Assistance 

(CEMA) was established by the USSR in 1949 to coordinate the economic activities of its newly 20

acquired empire in Eastern Europe. CEMA's true role in intra-bloc relations has undergone significant changes since the death of Stalin. These changes, and their implications for Soviet foreign and domestic policies, are the subject of speculation in this article.

KHRUSHCHEV AND PEACEFUL COEX- 

ISTENCE, by Capt. John S. Pustay, in Military 

Review, v. 43, no. 3 (March 1963) 47–57. • .. answer 

Why did Premier Khrushchev continue to promote the concept of peaceful coexistence in the 

Soviet Union's foreign policy operations? "The standard and almost universally accepted answer 'because the thermonuclear stalemate forces him to pursue such a policy.' This. does not fully nor truly answer the posed question. It does not permit the policy concept of peaceful coexistence to be viewed in its proper historical perspective. . . ." The answer to the question exists in an equation of three basic factors: material reality, political reality, and the role of ideology in decision making. Thus if Americans are to respond effectively to Russian's peaceful coexistence, it is imperative that we understand and appreciate the complex nature of this Soviet strategy of offense.

Soviet view of the Monroe Doctrine in light of the U.S.-Soviet direct military confrontation in the Cuban crisis in October 1962. Examines the phases of development in Soviet thinking which changed from limited interest in the pre-WW II years, to intense interest in Hispanic America after 

World War II and especially since 1954, the interest that increased to a point of intoxication and resulted in the historic confrontation. Notes, that 

Soviets, having convinced themselves, that "the 

Monroe Doctrine was dead," ignored all warnings, that finally resulted in a resolute action by the United States which led to a partial Soviet withdrawal of offensive weapons from Cuba.

THE THEORY, LAW, AND POLICY OF SOVIET TREATIES, by Jan F. Triska and 

Robert M. Slessor. Stanford, Calif., Stanford 

University Press, 1962. 593 p. 

This book represents a "systematic investigation and analysis of Soviet international legal arrangements." It is based upon five years of research conducted under the auspices of the Hoover Institution on War, Revolution, and Peace of Stanford

University. "A Calendar of Soviet Treaties, 

1917-1957," which was published by the Stanford University Press in 1959, marked the completion of the first part of the task. Its purpose was to identify the treaties and other international asrangements concluded by Soviet Russia from 1917 through the end of 1957. The present volume is essentially "an attempt to extract and analyze the salient features and the more enduring tendencies, conditions, and trends which help to explain the making of Soviet treaty theory, law and policy; to identify their basic common denominators; and to apply the resulting pattern to an over-all long term analysis of Soviet International agreements." 

This present book is chiefly a study of the 19171957 period. With bibliography. 

b. Foreign Aid HOW EFFECTIVE IS SOVIET FOREIGN 

AID? by Marshall I. Goldman, in Challenge, v. 11, no. 4 (January 1963) 7-11.

Since 1961 the Soviets have gone slow on new aid commitments to underdeveloped nations of

Asia and Africa. Soviet aid has strengthened the economies of recipient nations and has made them more independent politically, from both the West and the U.S.S.R. Thus from the Soviet point of view, additional foreign aid seems to hold little promise of tangible benefits. There are indications that the Soviets have decided to retrench, at least temporarily, their aid commitments.

ON SOVIET AID TO DEVELOPING COUNTRIES, by Ernst Kux, in Swiss Review of 

World Affairs, v. 12, no. 8 (November 1962) 9-11.

In trying to make friends for themselves and their economic and political system in the developing countries, the Soviets have encountered increasing difficulties. Somewhat disillusioned, but still far from ready to abandon the field, they are experimenting to find the formulas that will best serve their purposes. This article is condensed from a long report by the Neue Zurcher Zeitung specialist on Soviet Affairs.

THE PROBLEM OF FOREIGN AID, by 

Peter Toma, in East Europe, v. 12, no. 2 (February 1963) 2-9. "East European countries play an important part in Moscow's struggle for influence in the underdeveloped countries. The drive has run into political obstacles in India, Africa, and Latin America, but it is likely to be pressed on a larger scale in the future. What are some of the implications for Western policymakers?" Rather than being a systematic blueprint for economic development, the Soviet aid program "is in the final analysis a very shrewd technique for exploiting the weakness of the West. While the United States has been forced by events to spread its aid thinly over the world, the Communist countries have been able to concentrate on a few spectacular projects in carefully selected countries."

th Translated and digested from Wehr und Wirt schaft (Federal Republic of Germany) May 1961. 

The cold war which the Soviets wage under the guise of "friendly coexistence" is designed to offset the Western pact system and to establish beachheads and bases for further expansion in Africa as well as other countries. Success in this field is the result of flexible employment of Soviet economic aid, including the export of arms and military training personnel. SOVIET RUSSIA AS A MODEL FOR UNDERDEVELOPED AREAS, by W. Donald 

Bowles, in World Politics, v. 14, no. 3 (April 1962) 483-504. "A wave of rising expectations has engulfed the underdeveloped areas of the world, and in its wake there remains an intense desire for economic advancement and national recognition. Until recent years the relatively luxurious level of living in the West, particularly in the United States, served as a natural focal point in search for a pattern of economic life that would lead to growth in these areas It is now suggested by some that U.S.S.R. is a more appropriate model for underdeveloped areas than is the United States. 

Several . . . considerations may in part explain this shift

However, it is the thesis of the present article that in fact the U.S.S.R. provides a most unsatisfactory model for nations interested in rapid economic development." 

WHY DO THE SOVIETS SUCCEED AND THE WEST FAIL IN UNDERDEVELOPED COUNTRIES? by Suzanne Labin, in NATO's 

Fifteen Nations, v. 8, no. 2 (April-May 1963) 7788.

Although Western aid to underdeveloped countries has far surpassed that of the entire Soviet and Chinese bloc, Communist influence in these countries has been much greater than that of the Western countries. The author substantiates this by looking briefly at the various "uncommitted countries." In ten years, fifteen of these countries, all of them beneficiaries of extensive Western economic aid, have fallen within the Soviet orbit. 

In fourteen other countries, Communist influence has increased dangerously. The author discusses the reasons for this massive failure of Western ideas. The author discusses: economic aid alone not being a barrier against Communism; why the 

Soviets willingly let American aid exist side by side with Soviet aid; how the Kremlin makes the 

Americans pay for the intrastructure of the countries it covets; etc.
