DISARMAMENT: THE SOVIET POSI- 

TION, by Andrei Gromyko, in Survival, v. 5, no. 1 (January-February 1963) 29 plus.

A speech to the U.N. General Assembly, 21 

September 1962, revealing some significant new developments in the Soviet approach to disarmament negotiations, and the Soviet government's view on the question of mutual deterrence during disarmament.

IDEOLOGY IN SOVIET DISARMAMENT 

POLICY, by Walter C. Clemens, Jr., in The

Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 8, no. 1 (March 

1964) 7-22. "Whether we seek to control or reduce armaments or merely to improve our propaganda position, a sound knowledge of Soviet attitudes on disarmament is essential." Therefore, this paper attempts to review the history of the Soviet campaign for disarmament from its origins to the present time, to see what light this background sheds on the problems of the present and the future. Particular attention is given to the role of ideology in the formation of Soviet disarmament policy. No attempt is made to downgrade the role of material power considerations in the determination of Soviet policy, but only to focus on what appears to have been the doctrinal framework within which that policy took root. "Rather than juxtaposing ideological and power factors, this paper sees them both as highly important and as interrelated. The manner in which their mutual relationship has developed is suggested as the history of Soviet attitudes toward disarmament is reviewed." With references.

SOVIET ARMS CONTROL THEORY: 

1958-1962, by P. Kenez and others, Waltham, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1962. 65 p. "The paper consists of four parts. The first part deals with Soviet thought on the Cold War and its military problems; changes in military doctrine between 1960 and 1962, the dangers of general thermonuclear war by accident or surprise attack and the dangers that limited war (in particular, wars of national liberation) will escalate. 

The second part concerns Soviet suggested solutions to the dangers of the Cold War short of general and complete disarmament, namely, the deactivization of Western overseas bases, denuclearization of geographic areas and agreed limits on the military use of outer space. These are the more sweeping Soviet analogies to American arms control measures. The third part deals with Soviet views on disarmament: inspection and control measures, the need for an international police force and the economic consequences of disarmament. The fourth and final section of the paper discusses the implications of Soviet arms control theory for United States policy. This policy section considers the question of how the Soviets will perceive various types of arms control initiatives the United States might choose to take, some possible moves to reduce the first-strike and overkill capacity of U.S. overseas bases and areas in which the United States might accept the ideas of denuclearized zones." Includes the current Soviet views on the economic problems of a disarmed world; Soviet views on inspection for disarmament; and Soviet views on an international force to police disarmament.

Clews made a special study of the germ warfare campaign in the context of the more permanent themes of Communist propaganda, and has shown the genesis of the idea in Stalinist secret police mythology before its specific application in charges against the American army during the Korean Mr. Clews surveys the evidence which was adduced as 'proof' of the accusation, and shows conclusively that it cannot, and never could, stand up to political analysis. Washington, but also created suspicions in the minds of many people who had no sympathy for Communism, but thought the Pentagon might be 'up to something'. DISARMAMENT AND THE NEWLY DE- 

VELOPING STATES, by Y. Dolgopolov, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 2 (February 

1963) 56-60.

The thesis of this Soviet writer is that "The struggle for disarmament lends additional strength to the forces combatting colonialism. That is why the struggle for national independence must not be separated from, and even less counterposed to, the * fight for disarmament." In support of the thesis he quotes N. S. Khrushchev: "The struggle for general disarmament facilitates the struggle for U national independence. The achievements of the national-liberation movement, in their turn, promote peace and contribute to the struggle for disarmament." The author elucidates by pointing out 

O that while "disarmament means the abolition of ▪ militarism [destruction] of nuclear delivery vehicles, the withdrawal of troops from foreign territories and dismantling of foreign military bases, this will in no way weaken the defence measures taken by the young national states, neither I would it leave the peoples unarmed in face of the colonialists." The author also describes may of the economic advantages that will accrue to the newly developing nations from general disarmament, but he is against unilateral disarmament by the Soviet 

Union to bring them these advantages. "There are pacifist supporters of disarmament who think that the Soviet Union and the other Socialist countries ought to disarm unilaterally and in this way prompt the imperialist states to do likewise, but their views are open to serious criticism. Such action could only embolden the aggressors to undertake fresh military ventures, including action against young national states." The article also reveals the support that is derived by the Soviet strategy from the position of neutralist states on the question of arms control and disarmament.

African armies and police also have a great part to play in the subjugation of the African armed forces to the French militarists. Such agreements have been signed between France and Senegal, Chad, the Malagasy Republic, the Central African 

Republic, Congo (Brazzaville), Gabon, Cameroun, Ivory Coast, Dahomey, Niger, Upper Volta, Togo and Mauritania." The author views French military bases on the territory of the African states "as a threat to peace in that part of the world."

The author states that the struggle of Africans against the French is being intensified. "This is evidenced by the withdrawal of the French forces from Mali's forts, Upper Volta's refusal to give the imperialists military bases, and the recent phaseout of the French military base at Bizerta. The 

May Conference of Independent African states in

Addis Ababa has given an impetus to this struggle by stressing that there can be no complete national liberation without the eliminations of foreign military bases."

Eatherly that he was the pilot who dropped the A-bomb on Hiroshima and that he was awarded the Distinguished Flying Cross for the operation.

SPACE, by I. Cheprov, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 12 (December 1963) 35–40. 

Takes issue with the American Telephone and

Through such a communications system we will be able to relay a true picture of our American way of life to broadcasting station, which in turn might transmit into the home of the African, the Asians, the Russians, and the Chinese Communists.... We must reach behind the Iron Curtain and into Russia itself." The author finds danger in the operation of space satellites by private corporations and thinks that the responsibility for space activity should be borne by states instead.

NUCLEAR WEAPONS AND THE WEST, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 9 (September 1964) 53–66. 

On 15 August 1964 "an exchange of opinion" was arranged in the offices of International Affairs.

PEACE THROUGH LAW: TWO VIEWS, by Y. Korovin, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 7 (July 1963) 100–102.

A review of the book LAW IN THE SERVICE OF PEACE. TWO CONCEPTIONS. Brus sels, International Association of Democratic Jurists, 1963. (Le Droit au service de la paix. Deux conceptions. Association Internationale de Ju ristes democrates, Bruxelles, 1963.) The reviewer highlights and sides with the book's criticisms of positions taken by the American Bar Association on various issues such as: the means for settling international disputes; the international court of justice; legal aspects of the U.N. structure; an international regime for outer space, and the formation of a U.N. international armed force. On the latter the reviewer makes the following comment: "The American lawyers want an early formation of a U.N. international armed force, in spite of the fact that—as the Soviet proposals for general and completed disarmament point outthe formation of an international U.N. armed force in conditions of the current armed race would be merely another stage in that race and not a stage in general disarmament." Concludes the review with the opinion that the International 

Association of Democratic Jurists had demonstrated in the book that "the activity of the American Bar Association and its committee is aimed at avoiding any discussion of the basic problems of contemporary international relations-peaceful co-existence and co-operation-and at concentrating on secondary issues. Besides, some of the solutions suggested by the American jurists for the later will hardly help normalise the present international situation."

Soviet view of the scope and objectives of U.S. space program which the author feels "increases the danger of the war catastrophe hanging over the world."

THE PUGWASH MOVEMENT, in Interna tional Affairs (Moscow), no. 7 (July 1963) 114115. 

A summary of the activities of the Pugwash

Movement from its inauguration in July 1955 ("when Albert Einstein, Frederic Joliot-Curie, Bertrand Russell and other scientists appealed to the scientists of the world to raise their voice against the use of atomic energy for military purposes") through the Tenth Pugwash Conference in September 1962 in London which was attended by scientists from 35 countries. "The Conference decided that general and complete disarmament must start with the elimination of delivery vehicles for mass destruction weapons in combination with measures to considerably reduce armaments and armed forces, and solve the problems connected with the existence of military bases and troops in foreign territories. The Conference requested the 18-nation Committee to increase the effectiveness of the means of detecting nuclear tests by setting up automatic seismographs to supplement the basic national means of detection. Such stations could be set up by the home country after being sealed by a proper international body."

Notes in conclusion that "The Soviet Government has watched the movement with attention and good will."

THE REDUCTION OF MILITARY EX- PENDITURES, by Igor Glogolev, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 3 (Summer 

1964) 310-316.

The author is now senior scientific research as- 30

sociate of the Institute of World Economics and International Relations of the U.S.S.R. Academy of Sciences. "The U.S.S.R. has reduced its 1964 military budget by 600 million rubles, and is ready for larger reductions if the Western powers decrease their expenditures in the same proportion. Reduction of military expenditure would benefit the economies of all countries: it would permit increased expenditure on their socio-economic needs. It would also have a positive politico-psychological importance. One of the principal ways of decreasing Big-Power military expenditure is the gradual elimination of bombers.

This is easy to control as the size of bombers and the airfields, etc. prevents their concealment. Reduction of military expenditure has diverted much of the productivity of the armaments industry in the U.S.S.R. to constructive purposes."

THE ROAD TO GENERAL AND COM- PLETE DISARMAMENT, by V. A. Kirillin, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Win ter 1963/64) 10-15.

The author is Vice-President of the Soviet Academy of Sciences and Head of the Department of 

Science, Higher Education Institute and School of the Central Committee of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union. "The cessation of nuclear tests does not in itself constitute disarmament but is an important step. Further steps could be the signing of a non-aggression pact between NATO and the Warsaw Treaty Countries, the establishment of nuclear- and missile-free zones, the withdrawal of foreign bases and the establishment of ground control parts in certain areas. In the past undue emphasis was placed on establishing control over armaments. The controls should rather be in the field of disarmament and carried out in step with the disarmament process, becoming gradually more comprehensive as complete disarmament is approached."

SOME LEGAL ASPECTS OF SPACE EX- PLORATION, by Todor Gabrovski, in Interna tional Affairs (Moscow), no. 2 (February 1963) 92-93.

The author, a member of the International Institute of Astronautic Law (Sofia), raises the following two questions: (a) Should a nation's sovereignty over its air space be limited only to the stratosphere or should it extend to the whole of the atmosphere, however rarefied the air may be? and (b) Where are the boundaries of aeronautic space and what rules and regulations must flying machines obey beyond these boundaries? He then renders the opinion (which he says was outlined by him earlier at the Colloquium on International 

Space Law in Washington in 1961) that "the boundaries of sovereignty extend as far as is required for the security of the country over which the given section of space stretches" and that "spy satellite launchings and nuclear tests do not invalidate the conclusions reached on the basis of international law." He also feels that the laws of sovereignty on the open seas are not applicable to the problem of space sovereignty and that the latter should be treated "as a special problem."

He concludes by appealing "to all specialists working in this field to condemn every act of aggression in outer space."

SOME SOVIET VIEWS OF AMERICAN STRATEGY, by Kenneth R. Whiting, in Air 

University Quarterly Review, v. 14, no. 3 (Summer 

1963) 114-130.

In the last three or four years there has been more discussion in the Soviet Union about military strategy than at any time since the early 1920's. This may be partly because of the gap that seems to exist between Soviet global strategy and the structure of its armed forces and partly reaction to shifts in American strategy. If the political and military leaders are now convinced that the opening nuclear exchange can have a decisive effect on the consequent course of the next world war-and Soviet literature on the subject makes this assumption-then the problem of superiority in nuclear weapons and long-range missiles is of overwhelming importance today. It would therefore seem that the Soviets' view of American strategy has an enormous bearing on how the Soviets formulate their own strategical concepts. The two following articles which are appended are illustrative of the type of analysis now being published in the Soviet Union: On Certain Strategic

Concepts of American Imperialism, by Maj. Gen. 

M. Milstein; and Anticosmic Defense, by Maj. Gen.

I. Baryshev. 

SOVIET DEFENCE MIGHT AND PEACE- FUL CO-EXISTENCE, by I. Glagolev and V. 

31

Larionov, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 

11 (November 1963) 27-33. 

"An analysis of the connection between the consistent Leninist policy of peaceful co-existence and the efforts of the Soviet people and Government to strengthen their country's defence capability."

Concludes that "Peaceful co-existence which is the general line of the foreign policy of the Soviet Union and the other Socialist countries does not imply a temporary absence of war, or a breathing space between clashes. Not at all. Peaceful coexistence is the only positive, constructive policy which promotes economic, social and cultural progress all over the world. Peace and disarmament will give scope to the development of the forces of Socialism, democracy and national liberations. 

At the same time they tend to isolate and weaken the force of imperialism and reaction. The 'dissenting' opinion expressed on this issue by the Chinese Communist Party leaders, who are trying to foist their reckless 'blow-for-blow' policy on the

Socialist community, is alien to Marxism-Leninism. The illusion of 'independence', of which there is so much talk in Peking these days, turns out to be nothing but a very prosaic betrayal of the cause of peace and Socialism which puts in jeopardy the future of millions of ordinary men. The 'theories' now being propounded by leading Chinese Communists in their efforts to 'refute' the

Leninist principles of peaceful co-existence merely serve to whet the appetites of the ultra reactionary circles, and are, in essence, provocative. The Soviet Union has always conducted and continues steadfastly to conduct the class, proletarian foreign policy of safeguarding the great gains of Socialism and preserving world peace. Its steadily strengthening armed might has always served-and continues to serve the vital interests of ordinary people."

THE TENTACLES OF "BASES STRAT- 

EGY," by Y. Konovalov, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 7 (July 1963) 52–57. 

Attempts to show that the United States does not need its overseas bases because (1) the advent of Soviet missiles and rockets obsoleted U.S. "bases strategy," (2) that "the Soviet Union has given sufficient proof of its adherence to the principles of peaceful coexistence" and that accordingly there is no validity behind U.S. apprehensiveness of possible Soviet aggression; and (3) that 

U.S. and other bases on foreign territories are not conducive to the Soviet ideal of peaceful coexist ence. Concludes: "The latest Soviet proposals for disarmament and liquidation of bases on foreign territory show the real way to liberate people from the threat which these foreign military bases represent. In insisting on the liquidation of military bases on foreign soil, the Soviet Union is guided by the fact that their presence aggravates tension among states, promotes the growth of distrust, encourages the development of colonialism and shakes the foundations of peaceful coexistence."

VENEZUELAN GUERRILLAS, by V. Listov, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 12 (De cember 1963) 54-58. 

A praiseworthy appraisal of the operations of 

Venezuelan guerrilla detachments against the

Betancourt government and against American owned property in Venezuela. The objectives of the guerrillas are defined in the following terms: "Early this year, Pompeyo Marquez, a leader of the Venezuelan Communist Party, spoke about the well-known Marxist principle that forms of struggle do not arise of themselves nor are they invented, but are the direct consequence of a definite relation of forces at a definite political movement. 'In present-day Venezuela,' he said, 'we must not ignore the fact that it is necessary to employ diverse forms of struggle, from armed self-defence to parliamentary struggle. . . . It is necessary to develop all forms of struggle because this is required by the Venezuelan political crisis and holds good not only for the given moment, but also for the prospects of the crisis, its development." Describes some of the guerrilla operations which include: "the blowing up of oil and gas pipe-lines, setting fire to factories and warehouses owned by North American companies, and clashes with army patrols, raids on police headquarters and the homes of army leaders, seizure of radio stations for the purpose of broadcasting a message to the people." 

In his concluding remarks the author notes: "The growth of the national-liberation movement in other Latin-American countries is an important factor promoting the guerrilla movement in Venezuela as one of the forms of the struggle for national liberations." 

"WORLD GOVERNMENT" in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 12 (December 1963) 101102. 

A reply to a reader of International Affairs who wrote to its editors: "The bourgeois press often writes about 'world government' and a 'world state.' I shall appreciate a detailed explanation." 

The editors label all such plans as "utterly reactionary and unacceptable" and also as "unfeasible and fallacious." They see the solution of world problems "not in the replacement of the U.N. by a 'world parliament' or 'world government,' so wildly acclaimed in the U.S.A., but in persistent efforts with the participation of the United Nations too-to solve urgent international problems and thus create favourable conditions for general and complete disarmament and for lasting world peace. 99 

WORLD PARLIAMENT ASSOCIATION, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 2 (Febru ary 1963) 111-112.

An editorial in which the World Parliament 

Association (which was set up in London in September 1951) is defined as "An international organisation of members of parliament who favour the establishment of a world government. It con- 766-250-65 33

siders itself the 'parliamentary column' of the so-called Mondialist Movement. This pacifist organisation arose after the Second World War among bourgeois intellectuals and became quite widespread in Western capitalist countries." The 

Editors of International Affairs find that "the majority of those who support the movement for world government sincerely act for peace, against the arms race, atomic bombs, military bases and for East-West reconciliation," but it does not follow that the program of the World Parliament Association is acceptable to the Soviet Union. 66 ... This panacea is in fact directed against the national sovereignty of states and presupposes the replacement of the principle of international negotiation, founded on mutual respect for the rights of nations and states, by the principle of subordination to world legislation, to the principle of compulsion. That is why plans for the establishment of a 'world government' are supported by reactionary circles in the Western countries, particularly the United States who want to use the Mondialist Movement for suppressing peoples and subordinating sovereign states to American dictation." The Editors note that in the autumn of 1961 the Association sent a large delegation to Moscow to discuss with Soviet members of parliament and representatives of the Soviet Peace Committee matters related to disarmament, and that questions of preserving peace were also discussed at the last conference of the Association held in Paris on 23-26 October 1962, and conclude by saying "We of course cannot agree with the methods by which members of the World Parliament Association expect to achieve their objectives, but its ultimate aims-peace, disarmament and improvement of peoples' welfare-place the 

Association, despite the reactionary essence of some principles of the 'Mondialist doctrine,' in the ranks of organisations opposed to war."

C. Western Europe: Defense Concepts and Objectives 1. Miscellaneous Aspects of Defense ARMS AND STABILITY IN EUROPE; A REPORT, by Alastair Buchan and Philip Wind sor. New York, Frederick A. Praeger, 1963. 236

THE BALANCE OF POTENTIAL POWER, by Adm. E. Biörklund, in NATO's Fifteen Nations, v. 9, no. 1 (February-March 1964) 34-40 plus.

The author provides an analysis of the strategical conditions of Northern Europe, "an orbit em bracing the Taimyr Peninsula-Moscow-Ber lin-London and up to the North Pole." This is a study of the actual power potential contained in this area. With tables showing comprehensive military strength of nations in Northern Europe.

COUNTRY PROBLEMS: SWITZERLAND, by Urs Schwarz, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 1963/64) 34–42. "The sole function of the Swiss army is to act as a deterrent against possible aggressors and maintain neutrality. The Swiss government would therefore welcome a treaty excluding the atomic armament of possible aggressors, since it does not (at present) possess weapons of this nature itself. But Switzerland cannot accept any important reduction of its own military preparations, which are considered to be the minimum compatible with security, unless in the context of general disarmament. Such disarmament would make necessary revisions of the Swiss Constitution and of Swiss customs. Switzerland is not a member of the United Nations and has not been active in disarmament negotiations since the failure of the Geneva Conference in 1934. Both government and public opinions would be caught unprepared if a treaty on general and complete disarmament were signed in the near future. This should be remedied, and Switzerland should begin again to take an active part in negotiations."

EUROPE BETWEEN ATOMIC AND CON- VENTIONAL ARMS, by Altiero Spinelli, in 

NATO's Fifteen Nations, v. 8, no. 2 (April-May 

1963) 46-49 plus. 

"The defense of Western Europe Today resides in the common commitment on the part of its member-nations and the United States of America to build up a military organization capable of pre- 34

venting possible Soviet aggression against the area, which, from the defense angle, can already be regarded as a consolidated whole. . . . What 

Europe needs is not a shield sufficient only to sustain an initial attack, but adequate conventionally armed forces of its own to ensure that it can take care of its own defense against a conventional attack by the Soviet Union, albeit a mass attack. 

The American nuclear strike force would go into action only if the U.S.S.R. were to switch from a conventional war to an atomic war in Europe; and this, in view of the balance of nuclear terror, would be very unlikely."

FIVE NUCLEAR OPTIONS FOR THE 

WEST, by Elliot R. Goodman, in NATO's Fifteen 

Nations, v. 9, no. 5 (October-November) 65-67 plus. 

"More than trade, fiscal and development aid policies, it is the nuclear issue that will shape the future of the West. . . . It poses the ultimate questions about who will have the power to decide who will die for whom and under what circumstances 

The West does not have a single coherent answer. Instead, there are at least five major proposed solutions, some of which stand in direct conflict with the others. The five nuclear options open to the West might be called: (1) nuclear nationalism, (2) the multilateral force, (3) 

U.S. nuclear trusteeship, (4) a nuclear partnership between the U.S. and Europe, and, (5) an integrated Atlantic nuclear deterrent." 

2. Arms Control and Disarmament a. Miscellaneous Aspects DISARMAMENT IN EUROPE. London, The Institute for Strategic Studies, 1964. 25 p. 

(Adelphi Papers No. 10.) 

The U.S. and Soviet Union have for some time committed themselves to variations on the theme of general and complete disarmament. Their suggestions for the way this should be done have been considered for two years by the Eighteen 

Nation Disarmament Conference in Geneva.

Their principles will be studied and modified over many years. This paper is limited in its contribution to such study and is confined to a single aspect of the proposals: the effect the first stage of each of these plans would have on the military confrontation in Europe. The question this paper tries to answer is whether Europe could reasonably be secure during the implementation of either or both schemes. With following charts which are reproduced in Appendices H and I of the bib liography: Strategic Nuclear Striking Forces- Early 1964; and Total Mobilized Armed Forces- Early 1964. 

EXISTING ARRANGEMENTS FOR IN- TERNATIONAL CONTROL OF WARLIKE MATERIAL-I. WESTERN EUROPEAN UN- 

ION, by Raymond Fletcher, in Disarmament and

Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Autumn 1963) 144-154. 

"An Armaments Control Agency was established in 1954 by Treaty between seven Western

European powers (Belgium, France, Italy, Lux embourg, Netherlands, United Kingdom, Western 

Germany) for the mutual inspection of their armaments. In this paper the author considers to what extent the Agency has discharged the duties laid on it by the Treaty and whether its experience may be relevant to a possible East-West International

Disarmament Organization. [The author con cludes that the]... lukewarmness in implementing the Treaty is largely due to the fact that the member states, being allies, do not feel their security threatened by evasions and failures. A control organization including former adversaries would have a greater incentive to efficiency."

FIXED CONTROL POSTS AND EURO- PEAN STABILITY, by Johan J. Holst, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 3 (Summer 

1964) 262-298. "Fixed control-post systems for detecting preparations for surprise attacks in Europe are analyzed. Systems in forward areas (0-100 kms.) could not provide useful warning and would entail significant false-alarm problems. In the 100800 km. areas the most useful system would include posts at divisional headquarters. For the West, priority should be given to a control barrier at the gauge-change stations on the railroads connecting 

Russia with Eastern Europe, with some supplementary post. Manning and control by adversaries is preferred. The particular military environment on the Northern flank is analyzed with regard to the requirements for and utility of a system of fixed control posts."

OVERALL WESTERN POLICY AND PLAN FOR DISARMAMENT, by Rear-Adm. 

Anthony Buzzard, in The Army Quarterly (Great 

Britain), v. 86 (1963) 85-95.

A lecture by the author designed to find an overall policy and plan for Western defense and disarmament activities, because in his opinion "There is no agreed overall Western policy, or plan, for 35

defense and disarmament." Throughout this survey he takes account of the relevant political and economic factors, as well as military considerations. He also touches on the "controversial moral and legal factors involved." 

b. Arrangements for Control of Nuclear 

Energy

EURATOM; A STUDY IN COALITION POLITICS, by H. L. Nieburg, in World Politics, v. 15, no. 4 (July 1963) 597–622. "The two industrial nuclear-power blocs in 

Western Europe, EURATOM (European Atomic Community-the Common Market) and ENEA 

(European Nuclear Energy Agency, organized under the auspices of the Organization for European Economic Cooperation), represent a significant political cleavage in Europe. Both supernational groups are consortiums for the generation of industrial nuclear power, including full production, reprocessing, and isotope separation. EURATOM represents the continental Six under French-West 

German leadership; ENEA, a more loosely organized bloc under British sponsorship. The most significant factor underlying these bifurcated nuclear programs has been the policy adopted by the United States and Britain of excluding their continental NATO partners from independent nuclear-weapons production and coequal strategic control of the alliance."

EXISTING ARRANGEMENTS 

FOR

THE INTERNATIONAL CONTROL OF WARLIKE MATERIAL-2. EURATOM, by 

Jacques Van Helmont, in Disarmament and Arms 

Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 1963/64) 43–56. "Established in 1957 by Belgium, France, the 

German Federal Republic, Italy, Luxembourg, and the Netherlands, Euratom is the nuclear branch of emergent European Community, of which the other components are the Common Market and the European Coal and Steel Community. 

Its inspection and control system exists by virtue of Regulations under the Euratom Treaty. The system ensures that the Euratom Commission maintains a record of the amounts of raw and fissile materials each of the six member countries has in its territory, and of all activities with regard to such materials. It stops at the point where these materials are processed for military use, since although the Commission must know for what purpose the materials are intended, responsibility for defence remains vested in national governments. Control and inspection is carried out by collating data which all private and public enterprises in these activities are obliged to submit, as well as by on-the-spot inspections. No case of fraud (against which heavy penalties are provided) has been discovered to date. Each member country is obliged to respect the Euratom regulations with regard to its supply of materials and its access to them; recognising the multinational character of Euratom, the 

United States and United Kingdom, when supplying raw and fissile materials to it, have renounced the right of supervision over the use of such materials on which they insist when making supply agreements bilaterally with national governments."

EXISTING ARRANGEMENTS FOR IN- TERNATIONAL CONTROL OF WARLIKE MATERIAL 4: THE EUROPEAN NU- CLEAR ENERGY AGENCY, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 3 (Summer 1964) 250261. "The Convention on the Establishment of a Security Control in the Field of Nuclear Energy was signed in December 1957 to ensure that the operation of E.N.E.A. joint undertakings and materials, equipment and services made available by or through the agency would not further any military purposes. This objective has been met in practice by the surveillance of nuclear fuel in the change of the joint enterprises. The control is carried out by the Control Bureau acting under the E.N.E.A. Steering Committee and subject to judicial review by the European Nuclear Energy

Tribunal. Detailed security regulations are elabo rated for each type of undertaking and include examination of design, detailed record systems and reports. On the spot verification is carried out by an international inspectorate. Application of the system has provided valuable experience at very moderate financial cost." 

3. The Allies 

α. ΝΑΤΟ 

(1) Defense Concepts THE CONTROL OF WESTERN STRAT- EGY, by Alastair Buchan, in The Journal of the 

Royal United Service Institution, v. 108, no. 632 (November 1963) 303–310.

The Western Alliance is engaged "in a battle of political will as to whether one strategy-led by the United States-is to form the pattern of the 36

future as it has in the recent past; or whether the development of Europe into an independent economic centre, and the concept of 'Atlantic Partnership,' implies the ability of Europe to pursue an independent foreign policy and an independent strategy from that of the United States and it is... inevitable that it should centre around the control of nuclear weapons." Mr. Buchan concludes that "If the United States wishes to retain operational freedom of action . . . it is in her own interests to encourage Europe to be concerned with the problems of the free world as a whole rather than with one segment of the defence of Europe alone."

"Change in power ratios and factors of economic interdependence undoubtedly affect the historic function of the British Commonwealth of Nations as a unifying and stabilizing influence within the free world." This article discusses the elements of the British Commonwealth association which bear directly on the North Atlantic Alliance. Commonwealth in the Asiatic and Pacific areas.

NATO IN THE 1960's; THE IMPLICA- TIONS OF INTERDEPENDENCE, by Alastair 

Buchan. New York, Praeger, 1963. 179 p.

This is a revised edition of the book first published in 1960. The three premises on which this study was founded are: 1. That the world is unlikely to reach a stage of detente and stability which will make it possible for the Western 

Powers to dispense with NATO in the discernible future; 2. That the central problem that now confronts the North Atlantic Treaty nations-is the diversification of the strength of the Soviet bloc into many new forms of military, economic, and political power; and 3. That the NATO countries will insist on retaining as much of their national sovereignty ... as is compatible with the security and the flexibility of the Alliance as a whole.

In order to clarify some of the problems that seem likely to confront the government and peoples of the fifteen NATO countries in the coming years, the author opens the book with the chapter on Soviet Means And Soviet Ends in which he attempts to evaluate the trends of Soviet policy and military power. Appendixes include information on the military power of the Soviet 

Union, and other Communist bloc nations. Also, table of comparative estimates of strategic strength of Western alliances and the Communist Bloc.

NATO'S STRATEGIC CONCEPT: WISH- FUL THINKING OR A REALISTIC AP- PROACH? by Commodore M. W. J. M. Broek meijer, in NATO's Fifteen Nations, v. 8, no.

5 

(October-November 1963) 52-56. 

"The main task of military power must be to safeguard peace." The author states that he is concerned about some ideas being discussed within 

NATO circles, about the military and political realities of today and NATO's attitude with regard to Soviet aggression. He further states that "the threat alone of NATO's nuclear power will work in favor of mankind." However, this means also that Europe needs those nuclear weapons, which fill up the gap between Soviet strength and NATO's military strength in Europe. He thinks that it is of the greatest importance that there exists a military nuclear power in European hands (British V-bomber force and the French "force de frappe" as well as the multilateral strike 37

squadrons of NATO). "It is very important from a political point of view that the U.K. and 

France have the independent power to use nuclear weapons in defense of the territory of free Europe."

NONNUCLEAR DEFENSE OF EUROPE, by H. Ashton Crosby, in Bulletin of the Atomic 

Scientists, v. 20, no. 4 (April 1964) 30-31. "Especially with the development of an effective West German reserve force and emphasis on NATO stockpiles of equipment and supplies, current NATO forces (including reserves) can defend existing NATO boundaries with conventional forces. The use of tactical nuclear weapons would insure only a bigger and better Gotterdammerung."

NUCLEAR 

NASSAU, by Air Marshal John Slessor, in 

Journal of the Royal United Service Institution, v. 108, no. 630 (May 1963) 139–144.

DETERRENCE AFTER 

The author attempts to clarify the policy of nuclear deterrence vis-a-vis the East and West, limiting his scope of thinking to the next ten years or so, realizing that technological advances may well outdate policies in less time than that. He believes it important that Britain and France should have a nuclear capability which should be at the disposal of NATO, agreeing with those clauses of the Nassau communique which agree to assign existing strategic forces to NATO and envisage the creation of a multilateral force under NATO control. He touches on the question of foreseeing "any conceivable circumstances in which Britain or France, or the two of them together in a European deterrent, would or could engage in a nuclear exchange with Russia independent of the United States?"

NUCLEAR FORCES AND THE FUTURE OF NATO, by Brig. Gen. E. Vandevanter, Jr., 

Santa Monica, Calif., Rand Corp., 1964. 15 p. "A review of the current attitudes of NATO members toward their mutual defense problems.

The author discusses the multilateral forces, the importance of tactical versus strategic strength, the status of West Germany in the eyes of other European powers, and suggests modifications in American policy that might ease certain points of friction."

The POLITICS OF WESTERN DEFENSE, by F. W. Mullay. New York, Praeger, 1962. 282 p. 

A British member of Parliament "examines the principles upon which Western defense has been based since the late nineteen-forties and discusses the success-and . . . the shortcomings of

NATO. [The book] set out . . . information about the strength and character of NATO forces and what lies 'on the other side of the hill' under 

Soviet leadership. It examines . . . some of the popular assumptions about nuclear strategy and asks the . question of whether disarmament can ever be achieved." With tables, glossary, and chapter bibliographies. 

(2) Problems of the Alliance FRENCH THOUGHTS ON THE ALLI- 

ANCE, by Paul Stehlin, in NATO's Fifteen 

Nations, v. 9, no. 4 (August-September 1964) 2026.

The former chief of the French Air Force Staff states that "If someone who cares for the defense of the West considers the future of the Atlantic 

Alliance, he has to go back into the past in order to better understand the current state of affairs and to be able to make a constructive contribution to the form which the North Atlantic Treaty Organization should take from 1969, when the question of its continuation will be raised." After he discusses NATO's past and present, he concludes that "perhaps a united Europe itself, America's partner at an equal level in the defense of the free world, is only a stage in an Atlantic Community which will unite both of them institutionally at the level of the new dimension having become necessary for their defense and their prosperity."

THE FUTURE OF THE ATLANTIC COM- MUNITY; TOWARD EUROPEAN-AMERI- CAN PARTNERSHIP, by Kurt Birrenbach. 

New York, Praeger, 1963. 94 p. "A comprehensive and compact survey of the problems involved in the building of this Community, and the necessity for doing so. . . . Dr. 

Birrenbach remains . . . optimistic about the future, despite the remaining obstacles outlined by him . . . ." All aspects of the problem of Atlantic unity-economic, political, military, and cultural -are dealt with.

NATIONS IN ALLIANCE: THE LIMITS OF INTERDEPENDENCE, by George Liska. 

Baltimore, Johns Hopkins University Press, 1962. 

301 p. "A theoretic analysis of the structure and dynamics of alliances. . . . The reasons for alignment, dealignment, and realignment have been 38

basically the same since the Stone Age. The cohesion and efficacy of alliances have varied in each -generation in accordance with the skill of the statesmen. Alliances of the present day face an additional difficulty in that a regime depending on popular support is loath to admit original misassessment of intervening setbacks. Approximately the first half of this book is devoted to an analysis of the above factors, as illustrated by past and present alliances. This provides a basis for a study of the trends and policies of the contemporary international system, the concepts of nonalignment and neutralism, and the future of alliances."

NORTH AMERICA AND NATO, by John 

Gellner. Toronto, Baxter Publishing Company, 1964. 20 p. In English and French. (Behind the Headlines Series, v. 24, no. 1) 

A look at NATO from the Canadian and

American points of view and the attitudes and approaches of both countries to NATO's problems, and what must be done to solve these problems. 

Points out that "there is at present time formal, but no real agreement on NATO strategy, because the question of control of nuclear weapons has not been resolved in such a way as to satisfy some of the most important NATO partners. This has been a constant irritant which has blighted the 

Alliance: It caused the Franco-American rift, brought on indifferences, and put off the realization of the original dream of Atlantic community. 

It is the one problem which must be got out of the way if the Alliance is again to prosper. There can be no effective reform of NATO, on the spiritual plane of aims and purposes or in physical organization, as long as the nuclear issue blocks the road."

THE POLITICS OF THE ATLANTIC AL- LIANCE, by Alvin J. Cottrell and James E. 

Dougherty. New York, Praeger, 1964. 264 p. "After outlining NATO's organization, the authors discuss 'NATO, Germany and the USSR' in which Russia's attitude, one of fear of Germany and capitalist encirclement, is called 'old Russian Paranoia' and the Soviets are held responsible for the creation of NATO. In the next chapter, NATO's military strategy is explained, followed by two chapters: 'Politics of European and Atlantic Economic Integration' and 'Politics of the Alliance'." 

THE PROBLEM OF COORDINATING THE POLICIES OF THE NATO COUN- TRIES OUTSIDE THE TREATY AREA: 

THE SIGNIFICANCE FOR AMERICAN NA- TIONAL SECURITY POLICY, by Maj. John 

B. Bellinger. Washington, Georgetown University, 1963. 163 p. (Unpublished M. A. Dissertation.)

This paper makes a "complete" study of one of NATO's major problems. The problem is that of coordinating the foreign policies of the NATO members outside the Treaty Area. The problem is analyzed to determine its significance from the viewpoint of the national security policy of the U.S. This line of study "suggests itself as a potential means of reducing the impasse which has been reached between the United States and the 

European NATO members over the independent nuclear deterrents issue." With bibliography.

SEEDS OF DISCORD, by Dorothy Shipley 

White. Syracuse, N.Y., Syracuse University 

Press, 1964. 471 p. 

De Gaulle, Free France, and the Allies. "France is still and will probably always be, 'the keystone of European defense.' To understand her today we need a clear perception of a movement that became the Provisional Government for a time, as well as of its Chief, now one of the foremost statesmen of Europe." With this as a thesis, the author traces the story of the rise of Free France and of her relations with Great Britain and the United States through some of its important turning points, until a different epoch began with the 

North African invasion in November 1942. The author feels that events which occurred during this period and the attitudes taken then "could not later be forgotten or abandoned."

A VIEW OF U.S./EUROPEAN RELA- TIONS IN 1964, by Horst Mendershausen. Santa 

Monica, Calif., The Rand Corp., 1964. 54 p. (RM-4334-PR.) 

This study covers some of the major political, economic and military aspects of the U.S./European relations during 1964, as well as an analysis of the "quieting of the central conflict" between the East and the West. The author points out the growing diversity in U.S./European relations and suggests the possibilities of a complex web of partnerships rather than a single partnership between the United States and a United Europe. Such a web might comprise NATO, bilateral partnerships on political and military matters, and various multilateral groupings in the economic fields. The evolution of such a web poses many problems for both the U.S. and for Europe, notably Germany; 39

yet it may be a practical way to give meaning to the idea of a U.S./European partnership.

WHY EUROPE FEARS US, by Raymond 

Aron, in The Atlantic, v. 214, no. 6 (December 

1964) 47-52. 

By way of leading up to what Europeans fear, the author outlines his interpretation of U.S. and 

Russian strategy in the nuclear age. The American strategy of graduated response aims at minimizing the risk of the use of the only weapons to which the American mainland is really vulnerable.

To the European, keeping hostilities from escalating means turning Europe into both the theater and victim of operations, with U.S. participation limited to an expeditionary force. Furthermore, Europeans tend to resent the fact that the entire American deterrent remains under completely American command. The graduated response doctrine, even when completely understood, stung the sensibilities not only of many Frenchmen and Germans but also of other Europeans. 

All of this does not mean, however, that the Atlantic alliance is doomed, but neither is there as yet anything to indicate that it will end in accord. 

See January 1965 issue of Atlantic for Mr. Aron's forecast of the spread of nuclear weapons, including a consideration of Red China. 

b. Nuclear Strategy (Including MLF "Dia logue") 

(1) Miscellaneous Aspects THE CASE FOR NUCLEAR SHARING, by 

F. O. Miksche, in Orbis, v. 5, no. 3 (Fall 1961) 292-305. "The Atlantic Pact has become outdated by events . . Because the European nations did not meet their obligations under the Lisbon Program [the program agreed upon by NATO in 1952], atomic weapons have increasingly become the crutches of NATO strategy. This has meant that an inordinate share of responsibility for 

NATO defense has fallen to . . . the United

States The acquisition of atomic weapons by Atlantic countries other than the United States and Great Britain has become essential." The 

U.S. according to the author, can no longer ignore this problem with a policy of withholding from friendly powers nuclear technical information. "The expansion of the Atomic Club. . . is inevitable."

CONTROL OF NUCLEAR STRATEGY, by 

John Slessor, in Foreign Affairs, v. 42, no. 1 (October 1963) 96-106. "... The future not only of NATO but of the

Atlantic Community as a whole depends today on the ability of Western statesmanship to find a politically acceptable and militarily sensible solution to the problem of how to give all the NATO allies a share in a common responsibility for defense of the West in the nuclear age

Common re- sponsibility and authority for decision on defense including nuclear weapons-that is the problem of control." The object of this article is to attempt a definition of what we mean-or should meanwhen we talk about control of nuclear strategy. 

DECENTRALIZING NUCLEAR CON- TROL IN NATO, by Timothy W. Stanley, in 

Orbis, v. 7, no. 1 (Spring 1963) 41–48. "Solutions to the nuclear control problem have proved elusive in part because the problem has been regarded as a monolithic whole rather than as several related but separate issues." This article shows that what appears impossible for the whole may become more manageable by treating each of the component parts in accordance with its own particular requirements. "The details of the possible arrangements suggested here are merely illustrative; they are only one approach to the problem. But the central point is to recognize that although there is an interrelationship between the uses to which nuclear weapons might have to be put, they fall into different categories which can and should be looked at individually."

One section of the article deals with strategic weapons while a second deals with tactical weapons. The proposals suggested by the author would involve several separate command and control systems for NATO's nuclear defenses. "The concept of decentralization of control by categories may offer the alliance a way out of the deadlock and lead to more effective management of the West's nuclear powers."

NATO'S NUCLEAR PROBLEMS: CON- TROL OR CONSULTATION, by Frederick W. 

Mulley, in Orbis, v. 8, no. 1 (Spring 1964). 

"NATO has more than enough nuclear capability . . . . The essence of the problem is that 

95 percent of NATO's nuclear striking power is in the hands of . . . the United States, and that the

U.S. nuclear force is just as much a national force as the emergent French force de dissuasion, i.e., it is subject only to the control of its president. 

But indirectly the nuclear conflict and the consequent breakdown of communications within the alliance threaten the defense of the West • · 40

The survival of NATO... is in question 

The recognition of the problem marks a great step forward. However, the proposals for a multilateral sea-based Polaris force in surface ships (MLF) suggest imperfect appreciation of the situation in Europe and also confusion about what is the real issue." The author attempts to clarify the nuclear requirements of NATO strategy and assesses how far the conflicting military and political needs can be reconciled. This is followed by an examination of the merits of the MLF.

THE NUCLEAR DETERRENT AND WESTERN STRATEGY, by F. O. Miksche, in 

Orbis, v. 8, no. 2 (Summer 1964) 221-237. "Just as it is impossible to fight with nuclear weapons, so it is impossible to rely on them exclusively to ensure peace. Nuclear strategy is a rigid, lifeless strategy. The . . . theory of nuclear deterrence . . signifies the renunication of all active policy, and . . . forsakes the initiative without which one cannot pursue a flexible policy and, consequently, attain successfully one's political ends 

There are many ways of outflanking the nuclear Maginot Line established by the West. ... Only an offensive political strategy can constitute an effective deterrent We should assure ourselves of a wide range of political maneuver by creating the military forces necessary to support such a flexible Western policy It is high time to develop such a strategic force posture. . . ." • ...

THE CASE FOR THE MLF: A CRITICAL EVALUATION, by Robert E. Osgood. Wash ington, The Washington Center of Foreign Policy Research, 1964. 57 p. ... (This is a sequel to Mr. Osgood's Nuclear Control in NATO, published in 1962.) "Increasingly, the international politics of the Western world revolve around divergent views on the Soviet- 

American nuclear balance, nuclear strategy, and the ownership and control of nuclear weapons.

The very inhibitions against actually using nuclear weapons have magnified their significance as sources and objects of political agitation among the NATO allies... The MLF (multilateral force) has arisen from a variety of political and military considerations related to the American government's desire to satisfy allied desires for greater control over the use of nuclear weapons and yet discourage the spread of independent nuclear forces to France and beyond." CRISIS IN THE WEST, by Frank J. Johnson, in Washington Report (American Security 

Council), WR-64-42 (14 December 1964) 1-4. "The author sympathizes with President de 

Gaulle's opposition to the MLF and agrees with his demand for an independent European deterrent." ." De Gaulle will use its deterrent in defense of Europe. What is needed instead of MLF is for the U.S. to acknowledge and accept an independent European force. The U.S. should drop the "flexible response" strategy and reassert the concept of "maximum deterrence," renounce bi-lateral deals with the USSR and adopt something like the "Directorate for Global Strategy" proposed by

De Gaulle. In return, the U.S. could then insist on Britain's admission into the Common Market and on a federal rather than a confederated concept of European unity in which the U.S. would participate in decisionmaking.

MLF: THE NEW NATO SWORD? by Capt. 

Edward T. Kenny, in U.S. Naval Institute Pro ceedings, v. 90, no. 2 (February 1964) 25-35.

In a May 1961 address to the Canadian Parliament, Pres. John F. Kennedy said, "We look to the possibility of eventually establishing a NATO seaborne force which would be truly multilateral in ownership and control." Only when the necessary conditions had been fulfilled was the multilateral seaforce to be launched. To date, the conditions have not been fulfilled. The current hints in the press indicate that the 1964 concept of the new NATO force involves about 25 surface missile warships each carrying a crew of 250 and up to 

16 Polaris missiles. "Regardless of the form it takes, any system of control which restores the sense of partnership will be a welcome one."

MULTI-NATIONAL OR MULTI-LATER- 

AL? by Maj. Edgar O'Ballance, in The Army 

Quarterly (Great Britain), v. 89, no. 1 (October 

1964) 46–52.

A critical review of U.S. policies in NATO. 

"The questions of national ownership, control, veto and right to develop nuclear weapons and methods of delivery are amongst the most searching and serious that NATO has to consider today. 

Having dominated NATO since its inception, America is now faced with possible proliferation and the reluctance of certain European countries to... acquiesce to her continuing on in this dominant role. America is being compelled to consider plans which ... would satisfy the rising nuclear ambitions of certain NATO countries and at the same time allow her to retain the final veto. These . . . requirements have produced two schemes which have come to be known as 'Multi-Nationalism' and 'Multi-Lateralism"." 

The author presents arguments for and against each.

NATO TODAY: THE GREAT POKER GAME, by Alexander Werth, in The Nation, v. 

199, no. 17 (30 November 1964) 399–401.

The author compares the negotiations over the 

MLF to the game of bluff, Poker. Germany and

Britain appear indefinite as to whether they want to stay in or get out. "Johnson appears determined to press his hand, and the stakes go higher. 

De Gaulle, intending to raise the stakes, threatens to desert not only NATO, but the Common Market also. The French view with apprehension the 

American-British-German meetings fearing the three intend to combine (against popular rules) and with that power, force France to throw in her cards. But Werth warns that the 'Great Poker 

Hand' has only begun, and none can say where it will end, for De Gaulle has not really yet shown his hand."

THE NUCLEAR DEFENSE OF NATO, by Gerard C. Smith, in The Department of State 

Bulletin, v. 50, no. 1299 (18 May 1964) 783–790.

The author discusses how the U.S. can share strategic deterrent responsibilities with its NATO allies without promoting independent national nuclear forces. The solution lies in MLF (the multilateral force-the proposed missile fleet for NATO).

THE NUCLEAR DETERRENT AND THE ATLANTIC ALLIANCE, by George W. Ball, in The Atlantic Community Quarterly, v. 1, no. 2 (Summer 1963) 199–204. 

"The massive deterrent nuclear power which the United States has built serves more than our narrow national interests. We hold that force as trustees for the security of free men and free institutions everywhere.... But we cannot assume that all members of the Atlantic Alliance will be forever content to accept this as a permanent solution . .

The alternatives to concentrating nuclear power under one government or proliferating power among many must lie on some arrangement for the pooling of effort on a basis of international management and control. We have, therefore, made known our willingness to join our NATO partners... in attempting to design and build a new multilateral force that could supplement the national forces already in existence."

WHAT PRICE MLF? by Edmond Taylor, in 

The Reporter, v. 31, no. 10 (3 December 1964) 12 plus.

The interpretation of the North Atlantic Treaty as presented by Manlio Brosio, NATO's new secretary-general, before the conference of NATO parliamentarians carried important implications for the future of the alliance. Brosio said that 43

every member of the alliance has the power to veto any major change in NATO's present military and civilian organization. The implication of this interpretation is that President de Gaulle can block any incorporation of MLF into NATO.

This could result in creating a "rump NATO” alongside the legal one, and NATO would soon be dead and De Gaulle absolved of any blame for its decease. 

4. Canada

CANADA AND WESTERN DEFENCE, by 

Klaus Knorr, in International Journal, b. 18, no. 1 (Winter 1962-1963) 1-16. 

The author feels personally that he must give priority to the goal of arms control and disarmament to make major war impossible or less probable. For the time being, however, he is pessimistic about the changes of effecting so drastic a change in the organization of the world. Therefore, for the present, he feels, we must make do with strategic deterrence and we must prepare to limit damage in war and secure a better rather than worse outcome. With this as a background he discusses "Canada and Western Defence," stating that "To meet the problem of North American

Defence Canada and the United States are joined in a highly co-ordinated effort. There is a general agreement that this joint enterprise was invaluable in the past and, in all likelihood, is still invaluable in the present. It is about its value in the future that doubts and disagreement have arisen in Canada."

CANADA'S DEFENSE WHITE PAPER, in 

Survival, v. 6, no. 3 (May-June 1964) 105-111.

The White Paper sets Canada's defense policy within its proper international and technological context. The Paper, which appeared in March, lays down the main lines of Canadian policy over the next ten years, and contains a number of significant decisions. The most significant are reprinted here and touch on: the international outlook, NATO strategy, defense of Canada, peace-keeping, etc.

POWER POLITICS IN THE NUCLEAR AGE; A POLICY FOR BRITAIN, by Ste phen King-Hall. London, Victro Gollancz, 1962. 

224 p. “If . . . one feels that it is in the highest degree unlikely that a mounting arms race (at astronomical cost) can reasonably be expected to continue and preserve the peace more or less indefinitely, or at any rate until the end of the century without disaster, and if one has to recognize that multilateral disarmament is making no progress, the 

64,000 dollar question is whether there is anything else which can usefully be done? This book says there is and that British should do it." The author recommends that Great Britain "should, preferably with like-minded nations, but if necessary alone, renounce the use of nuclear energy for military purposes and do so for two reasons. First, because it would make a substantial contribution to the world problem of the nuclear arms race; and secondly, because the adoption of this policy would restore Great Britain to that pre-eminence as a great Power which she enjoyed during the 19th century when her sea-power allied with industrial and commercial strength underpinned the Pax 

Britannica to the benefit of many others beside the British. The evidence in support of these beliefs is set forth in this book."

D. Communist China 1. Foreign Policy COMMUNIST CHINA'S FOREIGN POL- 

ICY, by R. G. Boud. New York, Frederick A. 

Praeger, 1962. 147 p.

This work is based on research undertaken in the Department of International Relations at the Institute of Advanced Studies in the Australian National University during 1959-1961. An assess ment is made first of the state of the nation, then of its foreign relations and objectives, and finally of its threat to Asia and the West. In chapter 7OBJECTIVES, STRATEGY AND CAPABIL- 

ITIES, the author makes the following observations: "The first external objective of the Chinese 

Communist regime must be the preservation of its own security. This imposes very important limits on what China can do in order to expand her power. It requires avoidance of the risks of major war with a modern state, and it calls for the diversion and draining of Western military strength, the exploitation of divisions among the West, and the encouragement of friendly neutrality among other Asian countries. Finally, in the opinion of the Chinese Communist authorities, China's security demands a belligerent attitude towards the 

West, because firm opposition will discourage the 'Imperialists' from aggression. China's security is listed first among her national interests, but these are understood in the context of an expansionist outlook which is ideological, and, to an increasing extent, chauvinist. The maintenance of China's security is thus something to be understood within a kind of forward strategy, and this forward strategy itself is considered to play a vital role in the maintenance of her security; the security factor however does mean that in the forward strategy the way must always be left open for a retreat from any belligerent position, so that the regime will not be obliged to face unacceptable risks. Scope from such retreats is provided by much-publicised dedication to a 'peaceful coexistence'."

THE SINO-SOVIET CONFLICT, 1956–1961, by Donald S. Zagoria, Princeton, N.J., Princeton 

University Press, 1962, 484 p.

An analysis of the Sino-Soviet conflict, tracing the conflict and exploring its causes. In this con text the author discusses: The Seeds of Conflict- 1956-1957; The Dispute Over Communes-1958– 1960; The Origins of the Dialogue on Intra-Bloc Relations and Global Strategy-1957-1958; The Brink of Schism-1959-1960; and Neither Split nor Solution. With bibliography. 

2. Armed Forces CONFLICT IN RED CHINA'S ARMY, by 

Ellis Joffe, in Military Review, v. 44, no. 12 (December 1964) 70-82.

One of the most pressing needs of the Communist Chinese when they established their regime was to convert the semi-guerrilla force which had brought them to power into a modern army capable of maintaining that power. This effort by Red 

China's leaders solved one problem and created another—the professional officer developed views and values which have brought the army into conflict with the party on a number of issues. Since 

1960 there has been a notable absence of public criticism of the army. Perhaps Moscow's disregard for Peking's views, especially on the test ban treaty, has convinced the officers that China has only herself to depend upon. If so, the widening of the Sino-Soviet rift, which several years ago tended to drive the military and political leaders apart, has now drawn them closer together. Nevertheless, there is no sign that the sources of potential tension have been eliminated, although there is no reason to expect any major crisis in the relations between the party and the army in the present circumstance. (Digested from The China 

Quarterly, Great Britain, April-June 1964.) 

3. Nuclear Weapons Potential RED CHINA'S AIRPOWER, By Asher Lee, in Military Review, v. 43, no. 8 (August 1963) 80-84.

Translated and digested from Wehrkunde (Fed. 

Rep. of Germany) March 1963. Communist China has never been a truly independent airpower. 

During the period 1951-53, Peking's air force units enjoyed top priority in the Kremlin's program of MIG allocations. During the past seven or eight years, the progressively deepening rift between Moscow and Peking precipitated a fast and disastrous decline of Chinese airpower. The author also discusses air transport shortages, fuel supplies, and China strategic limitations. He concludes that "Red China has only very limited potentialities in the development of a conventional air force and in nuclear weapons."

4. Recent Literature on China's A-Bomb CHINA AFTER THE BOMB, by Roderick 

Macfarquhar, in Current, no. 54 (December 1964). 

The implications of Khrushchev's deposition and the almost-simultaneous explosion of China's first nuclear bomb are analyzed. Coupled with 

China's entry into the nuclear arena, the removal of Khrushchev underlines the actual and the potential power of a nation that is determined to spare no efforts until it ranks equal in all respects with the superpowers. This determination is the main lesson to be learned from the Chinese explosion. The basic causes of the Sino-Soviet split will remain to keep the two Communist giants estranged, and if China is ever to eliminate American military might in the Far East it will still have to be through her own efforts. The explosion of her first atomic bomb is a warning that however long it takes, China is determined to achieve the military parity that might make that possible.

THE CHINESE BOMB MENACE, by T. 

George Harris, in Look, v. 28, no. 24 (1 December 

1964) 28-29.

An analysis of the world-wide implications of the Communist Chinese atom bomb. Notes that the tendency in the West is to minimize the technological, political, and military aspect of the recent Chinese achievement, just as years ago the implications of the Russian A-bomb were underrated, but the true picture is that the Chinese 

A-bomb can only be a menace to the free world. 

Points out that it will be the business of U.S. policy to make sure that China does not start using her new weapon.

QUEMOY, MATSU AND THE BOMB, in Washington Report (American Security Coun cil), WR 64-39 (23 November 1964) 1-4. 

Red China's explosion of a nuclear device intensifies the Communist threat to the offshore islands of Quemoy and Matsu. The author recalls the

Senate resolution authorizing the President to take war action in Taiwan, the Pescadores, and

Quemoy and Matsu if the need arose. Cool judgment and firmness will be needed if the Red Chinese throw down an atomic threat to incinerate the islands if the Nationalists do not evacuate. At the same time, major threats have not forced us out of Berlin, and they should not change our policy toward the Republic of China and the offshore islands.

57, no. 26 (28 December 1964) 28-32. "Is Red China on the way to becoming a missilearmed nuclear power of real significance? The day may be closer than you think. That's the conclusion of an expert who has made a searching study of developments inside Communist China.

E. Egyptian-Israeli Arms Race BOMB SHOP IN THE NILE: TARGET ISRAEL, by Terence Prittie, in The Atlantic, v. 

214, no. 2 (August 1964) 37-40.

The evidence that Egypt's Nasser is planning to destroy Israel is examined "on the basis of his openly declared statements of intention . . . Nasser's campaign to compass the destruction of Israel has several main features. He is building up his conventional armed strength. In addition, he has made considerable progress with a crash program to produce ground-to-ground rockets in sufficient numbers to be able to flatten Israel's few cities and decimate its closely concentrated population. Finally, he has been experimenting with the production of weapons of genocide of both a nuclear and 'subnuclear' type." Egyptians with the help of 

German and other foreign scientists have been working on a crash program for the production of long- and medium-range rockets. Nasser's rocket program, used with his other conventional weapons, would be of sufficient scope to cripple Israel and secure her total military defeat. Further, "Nasser intends to close the Eastern Medi terranean and the Red Sea to the West. Compli mentary to this intention is Nasser's desire to build a firm alliance with the Soviet Union . . ." The 

Israelis are "desperately worried by Nasser's arms 49

· • buildup," and the author feels that the "United

States and Britain... [should] guarantee Israel's existing frontiers and offer a similar guarantee to Arab nations ." Other steps are further suggested by the author to avert a war. 

Detailed information on types of destructive capabilities, and numbers of missiles in Nasser's present arsenal, and his projected strength in approximately two years when he intends to unleash them against Israel. "There is a paramount need for close collaboration in the Middle East between the United States and Great Britain. Their immense joint stake in the Middle East oil is reason enough for this But much more important is the very real threat of war breaking out, if not today or tomorrow, then in all probability in the near future."

The arms race as it has been going on more specifically, in Egypt and Israel. How it started, how momentum gathered, the race for aircraft and rockets, and the nuclear potential of both countries, which have been working on nuclear projects. "The balance of military power between

PREVENTIVE DIPLOMACY IN IN THE MIDDLE EAST, by Walter Goldstein, in New 

University Thought, v. 4, no. 1 (Summer 1964) 14-22. "A new factor has lately erupted in the politics of the Middle East, and it now threatens to unhinge the fragile balance of power that has prevailed in the last decade. Its source is the growing magnitude of the arms race In the strictly • • limited wars of 1948 or 1956, the prospects of a decisive war were remote The stockpiles of heavy arms have suddenly introduced a revolutionary new element in Middle Eastern affairs: total war as an instrument of foreign policy." 

The suggested plan by the author not only provides a step toward international disarmament, but offers a policy "which could bring hope to this strife-torn area."

F. East-West Relations and the Strategic 

Balance 

1. Arms Control and Disarmament Proposals A COMPARISON AND EVALUATION OF CURRENT DISARMAMENT PROPOSALS AS OF MARCH 1, 1964, by Marion H. McVitty. 

New York, World Law Fund, 1964. 

43 p.

Two government proposals are under active negotiation and those two proposals are the focus of this comparison and evaluation. The analysis is confined to fundamental principles and major provisions and reflects the fact that disarmament, as defined in this pamphlet, is primarily a political and legal problem, rather than a military-technical problem as in the case of proposed programs for the mere reduction or limitation of armaments.

195 p. "This book... [was] published. as a convenient tool for .. those who desire to follow closely the evolution of the long effort for a disarmament treaty." To this end, it contains, besides a foreword by Mr. Justice Douglas of the

A speech delivered by the then British Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs, at the Disarmament Conference, Geneva, 25 February 1964. The

The time for generalities is over 

We must leave the platform for the laboratory." He lists a number of cardinal points upon which he thinks we should try to reach agreement if we are to make practical progress. He also lists a number of other fresh proposals which he thinks are worthy of particular attention and study. ... • 

DISARMAMENT: PROPOSALS AND CONCESSIONS, by Wayland Young, in Sur vival, v. 4, no. 3 (May-June 1962) 127-129 plus. 

Reprinted from The Guardian, 20, 21 March 

1962, and relates the "recent" American and Russian disarmament proposals to the joint statement of September 1961, and indicates the concessions that have been made by both sides, as well as the factors which will work for speed or delay in achieving disarmament.

THE LIMITS OF DEFENSE, by Arthur I. 

Waskow. New York, Doubleday, 1962. 119 p. nesses.

The author "examines such major policies of 

American defense as counterforce theory, the balanced deterrent theory, etc., and points up weak 

He puts forward the theory of Disarmament-plus, a policy of total disarmament, enforced and safeguarded by a highly mobile and effective world peace force, in which all means of competition between nations except violence would be legitimate."

THE PRICE OF PEACE, by James J. Wads worth. New York, Praeger, 1962. 127 p. "The former U.S. Ambassador to the UN and

U.S. negotiator at disarmament conferences suggests that a world without war is to the selfinterest of all peoples and all nations, regardless of political ideology. He recommends 'minarmament' a phased reduction of arms to the point where no individual nation is any longer capable of committing successful aggression against its neighbor-matched by increasing use of international instruments of enforcement." 

2. East-West Relations DISARMAMENT ECONOMIC GROWTH AND INTERNATIONAL CO-OPERATION, by Oscar Lange. Leeds, Leeds University Press, 1962. 11 p.

The author is the Deputy Chairman of the Council of State of the Polish People's Republic. After examining the merits and the need for disarmament, economic growth, and international cooperation, he concludes that: "... Caught between these two poles, that of threatening destruction, and that of immense possibilities of progress which modern science has created, we all, in whatever country we live, have the responsibility of making the proper choice. The political difficulties which have to be solved are great, but I am confident that finally the choice will be made in the right direction. Mankind will choose life and progress."

EAST-WEST RELATIONS; DISARMA- MENT, by David Ormsby Gore, in Vital Speeches of the Day, v. 30, no. 9 (15 February 1964) 286288. 

A speech the British Ambassador delivered at a meeting of The Mile High Club, Denver, 22

January 1964, in which he discusses the possibility of improving relations between East and West. 

In his summation he states that "On the one hand, we have a duty to try and end the arms race, to improve the machinery for the settlement of disputes between East and West and to remove the threat of nuclear war. On the other hand, we have a duty to maintain our military preparedness and our alliances in order to preserve the military balance of power upon which the peace of the world depends."

THE ESTABLISHMENT OF A DIRECT COMMUNICATIONS LINK BETWEEN THE SOVIET AND U.S. GOVERNMENTS, in Inter national Affairs (Moscow), no. 8 (August 1963) 140-141. 

Text of the Memorandum Of Understanding Between the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics and the United States of America Regarding the

the speedy establishment of direct contact between the heads of both governments in the event of an emergency threatening international peace and security.

THE KREMLIN'S HORIZON, by N. Leites. 

Santa Monica, Calif., Rand Corp., 1963. 50 p. (RM-3506-ISA.) "An analysis of present Soviet leaders' public statements from 1957 through the spring of 1962. 

Although the Soviet Union's basic hostility is undiminished, its earlier aggressiveness, largely motivated by fear of being destroyed by Western capitalist nations, has probably lessened. While present Soviet leaders view the West as less powerful than it appeared to their predecessors, they fear the possibility of a desperate attempt to halt the 'inevitable' growth of socialism. Russian threats and claims to preeminence are described in the context of some Soviet leaders' feelings of inadequacy in relation to the West. Despite these negative aspects, there are indications of a relaxation in the Soviet leaders' attitudes towards the West."

THE MEANINGS OF COEXISTENCE, by 

Philip E. Mosely, in Foreign Affairs, v. 41, no. 1 (October 1962) 36–46.

In the deeply divided world of today, one main obstacle to achieving a genuine state of peaceful coexistence is the gap in the meanings attached to these two words in different societies and political systems. Another gap lies in the confrontation of absolutes, the insistence on the total good of one ideal and the total evil of the way of life that it seeks to displace and destroy. Finally, the ideological armor that encases the Communist leaders is wrought of that contradiction in terms, "scientific revelation." Its theoretical bases, which were laid down over one hundred years ago at the beginning of the industrial era, must be proven to be uniquely correct and infallible today; and therefore those who dare re-examine or question any part of its fundamentalist dogmas must be silenced or destroyed. Moscow regards the present period of "peaceful coexistence" as a prolonged contest in which it must exert its full strength and will in order to make decisive gains by all means short of nuclear war.

THE "NEW MYTHS” AND “OLD REALI- TIES" OF NUCLEAR WAR, by Chester C. 

Ward, in Orbis, v. 8, no. 2 (Summer 1964) 255291. "It has recently become fashionable to talk about the old myths and new realities of American foreign policy. The makers of new myths assert that a strategic stalemate has developed between the United States and the Soviet Union and that, therefore, nuclear war is unthinkable. Among the well-briefed realities being discarded is the all-tooreal Soviet objective of world conquest. It is alleged that the Soviets no longer seriously consider the attainment of this goal, particularly as it can be achieved only by the use of nuclear weapons. The time has come to assess the new and the old myths, the new and the old realities." 

The author discusses: The Twin Assumptions of U.S. Strategy; Recent Evidence of Soviet Inten tions; A Reinterpretation of Khrushchev's January 1961 Speech; A look into the Future; U.S. 

Defense Trends; Risks and Costs; Soviet Strategic Capability; The Genocidal War Plan; Can the Minimum Deterrent Deter?; The Case Against the Genocidal Strategy; etc.

ON SELF-ENCAPSULATING CONFLICT, by Amitai Etzioni, in The Journal of Conflict 

Resolution, v. 8, no. 3 (September 1964) 242-255. "The advocate of general and complete disarmament can find little support for his stand in human history, political science, or contemporary international relations. Still, he has more to draw upon than just the fear of thermonuclear war and the promise of utopian fruits awaiting a warless world. His best case lies in the qualities of sociopolitical conflicts that are self-encapsulating. 

East-West relations may now be undergoing such a transformation." With references.

PEACEFUL COEXISTENCE; A COMMU- NIST BLUEPRINT FOR VICTORY. Chicago,
