What Is Peaceful Coexistence? What Does 

Peaceful Coexistence Accomplish? The "Victories" Of Peaceful Coexistence; The "Correla- 52

tion" Of Forces; Can Capitalism and Communism Reconcile Their Differences? Will The Two Systems Converge? The Role of Wars; Violence Or Non-Violence? The "Transition From Capitalism to Socialism;

Movement: The Sino-Soviet Split; The Communist View Of The West: An Exercise In Distortion; Conclusion: The Challenge To The West. 

Points out the Communists have not given up their long-range goal of destroying capitalism and of world domination. That peaceful coexistence as Communists understand it works to their advantage, and that they "hope not only to gain through an American Western slowdown in armament, but also to subvert and paralyze hostile governments in the hope that at the critical moment such governments will capitulate or will be incapable of offering effective resistance.". . . "The great paradox of our time may well turn out to be our inability to recognize that the cold war has in reality become more intense despite the increasing appearance of peace. It need not be emphasized that the overwhelming sentiment of the free world is to live in peace. But to mistake the illusion of peace for genuine peace would be a profoundly dangerous, perhaps fatal mistake."

THE STRATEGY OF PEACE; DISARMA- MENT IS THE GOAL, by John F. Kennedy, in 

Vital Speeches of the Day, v. 29, no. 18 (1 July 

1963) 558-561. 

Speech delivered by President Kennedy at the 

10 June 1963 Commencement, American University. The topic chosen by the President was the 53

topic of peace "the kind of peace that makes life on earth worth living-and the kind that enables men and nations to grow and to hope and build a better life for their children-not merely peace for Americans but peace for all men and womennot merely peace in our time but peace in all time." 

In his speech the President focused "on a more practical, more attainable peace-based not on a sudden revolution in human nature but on a gradual evolution in human institutions-on a series of concrete actions and effective agreement which are in the interests of all concerned." The U.S. and the U.S.S.R. "are both caught up in a vicious and dangerous cycle with suspicion on one side breeding suspicion on the other, and new weapons begetting counter-weapons. In short both the 

United States and its allies, and the Soviet Union and its allies, have a mutually deep interest in a just and genuine peace and in halting the arms 

We must persevere in the search for peace in the hope that constructive changes within the Communist bloc might bring within reach solutions which now seem beyond us The only major area of . . . negotiations where the end is in sight. . . is in a treaty to outlaw nuclear tests." The President concluded his speech by saying that "while we proceed to safeguard human interests, and the elimination of war and arms is clearly in the interest of both . . . this generation of Americans has already had enough of war and hate and oppression. We shall be prepared if others wish it. We shall be alert to try to stop it. But we shall also do our part to build a world of peace where the weak are safe and the strong are just." race 

THE THIRD ROUND, by W. W. Rostow, in Foreign Affairs, v. 42, no. 1 (October 1963) 1–10. 

"We are evidently at the beginning of the third major effort since 1945 to establish whether or not it is possible for the Soviet Union and the West to live together on this planet under conditions of tolerable stability and low tension. The first effort occurred in the immediate aftermath of the 

Second World War; the second, in the years after 

Stalin's death; and historians may well date the third from the aftermath of the Cuban missile crisis of last October."

NATO's Fifteen Nations, v. 8, no. 2 (April-May 

1963) 40-43 plus.

In examining the problem of total war potential, the author studies: NATO Versus the Warsaw 

Pact Powers, and summarizes by emphasizing that "as NATO has a total war potential that is 70 percent greater than that of the Warsaw Powers, it is comprehensible that Khrushchev in 1962 asked the satellites many times to increase their war potential, and had to fall back from his offensive weaponry of Cuba. The Soviet Union has not the strength to attack NATO, and NATO has no idea of attacking the Soviet Union, which diminishes the risk that a surprise attack would radically change Western superiority."

Khrushchev. It's a field where Russia despite boasting, hasn't kept pace. And Reds will drop further behind in months to come." Latest numerical estimates are included.

ARMS CONTROL: A NEW APPROACH TO DISARMAMENT, by Brian Crane. To ronto, Canadian Institute of International Affairs, 1962. 20 p. (Behind the Headline Series, Feb ruary 1962.)

A definition of arms control and disarmament, noting the differences between the two. The author discusses accident and surprise attack, limited nuclear war, a nuclear test ban, disengagement, limiting the spread of nuclear weapons, and he analyzes the role of international organizations in arms control and disarmament negotiations.

DISARMAMENT AND ARMS CONTROL IN THE MISSILE AGE, by Hedley Bull. New 

York, Praeger, 1961. 215 p.

A basic introductory book inquiring into the arms race and the measures by which it may be controlled. "It differentiates between arms control and disarmament, discusses arms control through disarmament (nuclear, conventional, chemical and biological, general and complete dis- 55

armament) and arms control without disarmament (the expanding nuclear club, surprise attack, outer space, and so forth)."

A selection of essays written in the years between 1939 and 1962, covering a wide range of subjects from science, education, disarmament, and international affairs to science in Russia. The author is editor of the Bulletin of Atomic Scientists.

DISARMAMENT: THE CHALLENGE OF ⇓ THE NINETEEN SIXTIES, by James P. War burg. Garden City, N.Y., Doubleday, 1961. 288 p. "This book is concerned with what the author believes are the three imperatives of survival in the atomic age: 1. The discovery of a modus vi vendi between two apparently irreconcilable and powerful systems of ideological belief and social organization. 2. The abolition of war as a means of settling the conflict between these two intensely competitive systems, as well as all conflict which may in the future arise between nations or groups of nations. 3. The rapid elimination of those major deprivations, injustices, and inequalities of opportunity which, so long as they continue to exist, will impel men to violence in spite of law and law enforcement." NEW APPROACHES TO ARMS CON- TROL, by Jerome H. Spingarn. New York, For eign Policy Association, 1962. 62 p. (Headline Series.)

The author, a specialist in international law, "discusses the political and technical problems of arms control, the factors which might cause miscalculation or accident, the Nth country problem, and U.S. and Soviet proposals."

THE NEW MEANING OF ARMS CON- 

TROL, by Col. Wesley W. Posvar, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 46, no. 6 (June 1963) 38-40 plus. "Disarmament, per se, is an impossible dream in the world today. But certain techniques and policies of arms control can play a part in safeguarding world peace."

NUCLEAR ABOLITIONISM, by Paul Kecsk meti, in Commentary, v. 36, no. 1 (July 1963) 43-48. "By now all discussion about the problem of how to avert a nuclear catastrophe has been pretty clearly polarized into two opposite positionsusually known as deterrence and disarmament, but more fairly and accurately, characterized by the terms 'stabilization' and 'abolition.'. . . 'stabilizers' stress the positive role that nuclear armaments themselves have played in preserving peace in spite of the high political tension... 'Abolitionists' see no adequate assurance of continued peace in all this." The author reviews the arguments used by both sides to promote their theories for "our best chance for peaceful survival." The author raises "several questions about the soundness of the idea that an international police force with a monopoly of nuclear weapons could preserve peace, law, and order in a disarmed world."

PEACE AND OPINION, by Evan Luard. 

London, Oxford University Press, 1962. 170 p. "The methods of securing peace need to be • • • adapted to the realities of the world to which they are applied." The author "examines in successive chapters nine problem areas-disarmament, ax arms control, aggression, frontiers, colonies, civil war, authority, law, and opinion-pointing out how current practice has moved ahead of current pe thinking, how the rush of world events has outstripped our capacity to analyze their meaning. 

In the field of disarmament . . . he contends that the governments concerned have been concentrating their efforts on types of armaments that are already obsolete or least likely to be employed."

RESTRAINTS AND STRATEGY, by George 

E. Pugh, in Military Review, v. 44, no. 3 (March 

1964) 69-80.

The article was digested from the original which appeared in Orbis no. 2 Summer 1963. "In the present international climate, it seems unlikely that the goal of a disarmed world under international law will be achieved in the near future.

It is, therefore, appropriate to inquire what steps can be taken, in the absence of enforceable world law, to improve international security and contribute to an environment where world law would be possible. While the ultimate goal may involve 58

revolutionary changes in the international environment, those steps which can be taken in the absence of world law are necessarily evolutionary. 

These must be soundly based on military and political realities and well integrated with other aspects of national strategy." Contents: The His torical Role; Violations; Deterrent Strategy;

THE STABILITY OF TOTAL DISARMA- IMENT, by T. C. Schelling. Washington, Institute for Defense Analyses, 1961. 51 p. (Special Studies Group, Study Memorandum, No. 1.) 

"A sharp distinction is often drawn between arms control and general disarmament; the former

I involves some concept of mutual deterrence; the latter, it is alleged, does not. The former seeks to structure military incentives and capabilities; the latter, it is alleged, eliminates them. This paper argues that military capabilities cannot be eliminated. "Total disarmament' does not preclude either war or rearmament A supra-national military authority would be no substitute for stable mutual deterrence. It would be, if anything, a mechanism for trying to achieve it." Partial con tents: The Idea of Stability; The Nature of a Dis armed World; Deterrence of General War in a 

Disarmed World; Limited War in a Disarmed

World; The Deterrence of Rearmament; An International Security Force; The Transition to Total 

Disarmament.

THE STRATEGY OF DISARMAMENT, by Henry W. Forbes. Washington, Public Affairs 

Press, 1962. 158 p.

This study attempts "to present an overall survey of recent disarmament trends and to establish as objectively as possible the chances for disarmament in the 1960's." Touches on vehicles, including missiles, of nuclear weapons.

WINNING WITHOUT WAR, by Amitai Et zioni. Garden City, N.Y., Doubleday, 1964. 271 p. "The United States can observe and react more successfully to the constantly changing international situation if the trend of present events is examined and the range of new possibilities assessed; if, in short, our strategy is oriented toward the future rather than bound to the past." It is the main thesis of this book "that the various peacemeal, and segmental adjustments that have already been made now point to a new overall approach. Only, when the new approach, implicit in these measures, is faced squarely and explicitly will the full value of past innovations be realized, and the need for complementary ones be clear."

In this context the author discusses: The Global 

Stalemate; The Future of Blocs-China and France; The Contest Over the Third World; Intervention for Progress; To Arms Control and Be yond; and International Relations in the Nuclear Age.

B. Administration THE POLITICS OF ADMINISTERING DISARMAMENT, by Lincoln P. Bloomfield, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Au tumn 1963) 118–132. "Present Western disarmament policy is guided by the fundamental assumption that significant regulation and reduction of armaments cannot be safely undertaken without the creation of supranational machinery to manage the program. On all the evidence the Soviet Union is most unlikely to accept a non-Communist world government whose establishment it would doubtless regard as a plot by the West to check Communist progress. 

It is not certain that the West it is ready for genuine world authority in any case. The author concludes that it is neither necessary nor wise to try and transfer into the disarmament arena arrangements which would elsewhere be unattainable because they are politically unacceptable. Proposals for inspection should in the future aim at the minimum intrusion necessary to secure vital compliance information...."

C. Biological Aspects PROBLEMS OF BIOLOGICAL DISARMA- MENT, by Ivan Málek and Karel Raška, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 2 (Spring 

1964) 150-156. "The danger of biological warfare is too often underestimated when compared with nuclear warfare. This danger lies in the greater ease of production and the difficulty and delay in detection of an attack. The 1925 convention should be reviewed to include an international agreement for mutual protection against biological weapons." 

The author lists five purposes for which aid should be sought from international organizations like 

UNO and WHO. 

D. Civil Defense Aspects ARMS CONTROL AND CIVIL DEFENSE, ed. by D. G. Brennan. New York, Hudson Institute, 1963. 47 p. (Prepared under Contract 59

ACDA/IR for the U.S. Arms Control and Dis armament Agency.)

A study of the interactions of arms control and civil defense. Among the subjects under discussion: the strategic impact of civil defense on arms control; a theory of arms control tendencies; impact of arms control and the arms race on civil defense; civil defense as arms control; some special problems of NATO (interactions with other countries). This document is part of the overall report submitted by Hudson Institute to the U.S.

Arms Control and Disarmament Agency on a study of the interactions of arms control and civil defense. The complete report consists of this summary report together with four separately bound annexes, which treat some of the topics in this summary in greater depth. The annexes are:

I. The Question of Crisis Evacuation, by Jeremy J. Stone; II. Civil Defense and Arms Control Objectives, by Raymond D. Gastil; III. Civil Defense Programs in the Present World, by Elizabeth Crawford; IV. The Domestic Political Interactions, by A. J. Wiener. 

MILITARY TECHNOLOGY AND THE SURVIVAL OF CITIES, by Clark C. Abt. Bed ford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 70 p. 

(Project Unicorn BR-2247.) 

"The effects of modern strategic weapons on urban-industrial areas are considered from the aspects of weapon technology, military strategy, and arms control considerations. The possibilities for active and passive defenses against likely threats are evaluated, and the major alternatives for unilateral means of reduction of war casualties and damage are considered. Quantitative costbenefit estimates are developed for these alternatives, which include hardening, dispersal, active defense, and mixes of these. A degree of urban dispersals satisfying peacetime feasibility and offering peacetime utilities, together with fallout shelters, is found to provide a solution superior in cost-effectiveness to blast shelters and/or active defenses, although the latter are not necessarily ruled out for strategic reasons. Some economic and social constraints on urban dispersal are considered, together with means for making it feasible."

UNILATERAL CONTROL OF MILITARY TECHNOLOGY, by M. Kolker and others. Bed ford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 73 p. 

(Project Unicorn BR-2248.) 

"A major arms control problem area is that of the qualitative escalations, qualitative arms and qualitative restraints on the research, development, and testing of new military weapons technology.

This volume discusses some of the possibilities and consequences of unilateral control of technological developments in military information systems (command and control, reconnaissance, etc.), weapon systems (ICBM'S, CBR, etc.), and logistics. The results of the analyses are complex and mixed-in most cases the question is much more complex than that of survivability."

Bedford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 91 p. 

(Project Unicorn BR-2249.) 

"To provide a basis for determining the capacity of the U.S. to execute unilateral arms controls,

U.S. Command and Control capabilities-both present and future-require understanding. This volume presents a review of the field in terms of technology, exemplar systems, command organizations, and policy considerations. By establishing this groundwork, specific elements of command and control can be analyzed to ascertain their contribution to the successful accomplishment of unilateral arms controls. . .”

G. Demilitarization and Denuclearization Proposals: By Region 

1. Africa and Middle East

A PROPOSAL FOR AN AFRICAN AND NEAR-EASTERN ZONE FREE FROM CER- TAIN WEAPONS, by David H. Frisch, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1 (Summer 

1963) 66-73. "This agreement outlined by the author is intended to prevent the development, testing, stockpiling and development of all weapons of mass destruction in a zone including the whole of Africa and the adjacent Near-Eastern lands up to the 

Southern border of Turkey and the South-Western border of Iran. Problems on delineation and of local conflict situations abound, but the emphasis placed on weapons of mass destruction might appeal to the nations in question as an idealistic and human step beyond anything that has been accepted by the big powers.' 

REGIONAL DISARMAMENT: AFRICA, by J. H. Mensah, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Autumn 1963) 171-183. "A scheme is proposed for the disarmament and demilitarization of the African continent. The 

United Nations Disarmament Commission should convene an African Disarmament Conference at which an Intra-African Disarmament and Non- Aggression Pact should be negotiated. This pact will be unconditionally underwritten by the states members of the United Nations, jointly and severally, with the understanding that any external aggression will be met by effective resistance. It is essential that both NATO and Warsaw Pact countries subscribe to this guarantee and that resistance on a scale commensurate with the aggressive action shall be provided automatically and rapidly. The resources necessary for adequate actions should be accepted as a charge against all the powers guaranteeing the Pact. But an attempt to persuade African governments to disarm will meet the same resistances as are frustrating world disarmament. The reluctance of some govern ments to disarm is due to their fear that their regimes would not survive without military support. Countries must be brought to will their own disarmament." 

2. Australia A REGIONAL PROBLEM: THE AMERI- CAN SIGNALLING STATION IN WESTERN AUSTRALIA, by Arthur Lee Burns, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 1963/ 

64) 23-33. "The strategic policies of a leading power (e.g. 

U.S.A.) may not harmonize with its arms control policy, which may also have internal incoherences: both redeployments and proposals for denuclearized zones may increase nuclear risks for some allies while reducing them for others. Moreover, systems like the Polaris Submarines militate against denuclearizing oceanic Regions. The 

U.S.A. has favored some denuclearization e.g. in 

Latin America and Africa, and has not outright condemned it in the Pacific. But from 1960 it negotiated the establishment of a VLF station on the Western Australian coast. Meanwhile the 

Australian Labor Opposition opposed its Govern ment's negative reply to U Thant's enquiries about nuclear abnegation, and in May 1962 proposed denuclearization of the whole Southern Hemis phere. In March 1963 an ALP [Australian Labor 

Party] Conference laid down strict conditions, including joint political control and avoidance of

Australia's automatic involvement in war, for the establishment of the VLF station. Harriman, while careful not to involve the U.S.A. in the resultant intra-Australian political conflict, agreed to an ANZUS Council's denigration of denuclearizing the Pacific, despite his earlier more favorable attitude." 

3. Europe

THE ANATOMY OF DISENGAGEMENT, by Col. John E. Dwan II, in Military Review, v. 

42, no. 2 (February 1962) 2-15. 61

The author feels that the concept of disengagement merits objective analysis to determine its validity as a step toward a more peaceful world order within which the interests of the U.S. and her allies might be more secure. After defining disengagement, he outlines the Eden Plan, the

Gaitskell Plan, the SPD (Social Democratic Party of West Germany) Plan, the Kennan pro posals, the Slessor proposal, the Rapacki Plan, and a composite disengagement plan. He questions the assumptions of these plans and re views the consequences of disengagement. The author feels that disengagement in central Europe, while superficially attractive, would lead to an imbalance in the power situation in Europe to the disadvantage of the West.

NUCLEAR FREEZE IN CENTRAL EU- 

ROPE, by Karol D. Lapter, in Disarmament and

Arms Control, v. 2, no. 3 (Summer 1964) 299-309. 

"A 'memorandum on the freezing of nuclear and thermonuclear armaments in Central Europe,' the so-called 'Gomulka Plan,' was presented by the 

Polish Government on 29 February 1964. It proposes to freeze the existing nuclear status quo in 

Poland, Czechoslovakia, the German Democratic

Republic and the Federal Republic of Germany. 

At the same time, the Governments maintaining armed forces in these areas (which includes West 

Berlin) would undertake not to produce, introduce, or move about within the area, nuclear or thermonuclear weapons. It also provides for the establishment of an appropriate system of safeguards. As the socialist countries have already declared their support of the Plan, the final decision rests with the Federal Republic of Germany and her allies. If necessary, the Plan could be implemented without the consent of the Bonn 

Government, by the Western nuclear powers undertaking not to hand over nuclear arms to the 

Bundeswehr." The author is the head of the De partment of Contemporary History and International Relations at the Warsaw School of Social Sciences. 

ON DISARMAMENT ISSUES: THE POL- ISH PLAN, by Betty Goetz Lall, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 6 (June 1964) 41-43. "A significant obstacle in the way of an international agreement to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons is the Soviet fear that the Germans will obtain control of nuclear weapons despite any agreement as a result of the U.S. Proposal for a multilateral nuclear force . . . . Considering the statements of U.S. and West German officials that there is no intention, now or in the future, of giving West Germany control of nuclear weapons, a possible solution for this stalemate may be found in the recent Polish proposal for an atomic freeze in Europe. On March 5, 1964, the Polish government proposed that no additional atomic weapons be introduced in the territories of Poland, the 

Federal Republic of Germany, the German Peo ple's Republic, or the territorial waters of these countries."

ZONAL ARMS LIMITATION IN EUROPE, by James E. Dougherty, in Orbis, v. 7, no. 3 (Fall 

1963) 478-517. "Nothing better illustrates the complex relationship between disarmament or arms control planning and international political problems than the disengagement debate which has flared periodically since 1955.... Disengagement, under most of the forms which have hitherto been suggested, amounts to partial disarmament-disarmament on the geographical installment plan." The lack of success of the past, however, does not "mean that the United States and its European allies can never reach any agreement with the Soviet Union and its East European bloc partners on some form of zonal arms limitation." The author surveys in a summary manner some of the principal proposals for disengagement in Europe which have been advanced either by the Soviet bloc or from within the West. He also analyzes the substantive arguments which have been offered for and against disengagement or zonal arms limitations in Europe. 

4. Latin America POLITICAL IMPLICATIONS OF THE PROPOSED NUCLEAR FREE ZONE IN LATIN AMERICA, by E. W. Gude, Bedford, 

Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 16 p.

(Project 

Unicorn BR-2525.) 

An exploration of possible political implications of the proposed nuclear free zone in Latin America, as well as an exploration of the possible types of inspection. Offers suggestion on the course to be taken by U.S. to protect its interests in Latin America. 

5. Southeast Asia.

REGIONAL: DISARMAMENT: SOUTH- EAST ASIA, by John W. Burton, in Disarma mer 62

iment and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Autumn 1963) 

155-170. "The procedural of negotiating approach to disarmament does not take sufficient account of the prerequisites to agreement. Conditions might first have to be created in which there are no fundamental conflicts among major nuclear nations, except those arising directly from competition in the development of modern weapons and means of delivery. A prerequisite to negotiations may be, therefore, the isolation of the major powers from regional tensions. It is reasonable to expect that in their own interests the major powers will endeavour to render their own direct relationship as uncomplicated as possible by withdrawal from situations not of vital or territorial interest to them.

Tensions would remain, however, in all regional relationships; and disarmament and withdrawal of the major powers is not possible while minor ones which are concerned with problems of regional and non-Cold War security increase their armament and reach a nuclear level. South-East 

Asia demonstrates the buildup in arms outside the 

Cold War. By exercising their sovereign rights, the smaller nations can pursue policies of nonalignment and can thereby limit the competitive rivalry of the major powers; and the major powers by refraining from giving support to unpopular governments or intervening in regional disputes, can ensure the regional resolution of most conflicts. 

Full understanding of problems outside the direct Cold War conflict could help to bring the problem of disarmament and arms control to the stage of technical and procedural negotiations between the major Cold War powers.' 

VIETNAM: THE FOURTH COURSE, by Gilbert F. White, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scien tists, v. 20, no. 10 (December 1964). 

For seven years, Cambodia, Laos, Thailand, and

South Vietnam have been working with little publicity and without disagreement on a huge program to develop the lower Mekong Basin. The author suggests that with the withdrawal of national military units, and the substitution of a 

U.N. security force, the U.S. could depart gracefully. The U.S. could then continue to support the 

Mekong development program as a constructive and humane "fourth course" to peaceful settlement in Southeast Asia. The program has progressed in spite of war, politics, and language diversity. In areas where work is under way, even guerrillas have not interfered. With security in the area insured by U.N. forces and heavy financial support from other nations, it is possible that out of this most incredible of places may come a new pattern for international action in harnessing nature's riches to achieve peaceful change. 

6. The Antarctic THE ANTARCTIC TREATY OF 1959, by 

Howard J. Taubenfeld, in Disarmament and Arms 

Control, v. 2, no. 2 (Spring 1964) 136–149. "In 1959, the twelve nations which conducted research in the Antarctic during the International Geophysical Year managed, after extensive negotiation, to draft and sign a treaty setting the pattern for future conduct of national activities there. 

This article analyzes the Treaty in detail, especially with respect to its neutralization, disarmament, denuclearization and inspection features. 

Its potential success as an essentially permissive regime for the area and its utility as a precedent for more immediately strategic regions are also surveyed." With references.

H. Deterrence and Credibility THE CALCULUS OF DETERRENCE, by Bruce M. Russett, in The Journal of Conflict Res olution, v. 7, no. 2 (June 1963) 97-109. "A persistent problem for American political and military planners has been the question of how to defend 'third areas.' How can a major power make credible an intent to defend a smaller ally from attack by another major power? Simply making an explicit promise to defend an ally, whether that promise is embodied in a formal treaty or merely in a unilateral declaration, is not sufficient. There have been too many instances where 'solemn oaths' were forgotten in the moment of crisis. On the other hand, more than once a major power has taken up arms to defend a nation with whom it had ties appreciably less binding than a formal commitment." With references.

DEFENSE, DISARMAMENT AND WORLD ORDER, by Lawrence S. Finkelstein. 

Toronto, Canadian Institute of International Af fairs, 1962. 12 p. (Behind the Headline Series.) 

Discusses the effect of nuclear arsenals on the political relations between nations-the balance of terror. The author also examines the role of disarmament which, he says, should not be regarded as an end in itself but as a means to the goals of peace, security, and stability of deterrence.

THE DETERRENCE OF LIMITED AG- GRESSION: STRATEGIC AND NONSTRA- 

63

TEGIC INTERACTIONS, by John Ponturo, in 

Orbis, v. 6, no. 4 (Winter 1963) 593-622. "The military evaluation of both arms control and unilateral defense measures must include the consideration of, first, the effect of such measures in reducing the threat of all-out attack upon the U.S., and, second, the effect of such measures in reducing the threat of limited aggression short of all-out attack upon the U.S. This paper is addressed to the second problem."

DETERRENCE UNDER FIRE, by Herman 

S. Wolk, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 45, no. 

3 (March 1962) 31–35. "Here is a consideration of anti-deterrence both as a policy and a philosophy. However, this article does not discuss the finite and counterforce positions but rather directs its attention toward the broad spectrum of naked anti-deterrence." 

DETERRENT AND DISARMAMENT, by 

Alastair Buchan, in Military Review, v. 42, no. 2 (February 1962) 73–86. 

Digested from the Journal of the Royal United

Service Institution (Great Britain) May 1961. 

"There is a twilight area of thought where most of the pioneering work still lies ahead of us. This is in the relationship between the strategy of deterrence, which has hitherto been the cornerstone of international security, and the proposals for disarmament that are gathering greater and greater political strength throughout the world." 

The author examines the contemporary arms race, looks at the practical validity of the types of disarmament schemes most commonly put forward, and examines the measures of control or restraint that are the essential connecting link between a world of uncontrolled and increasing armaments and one in which their level has been lowered. 

DISARMAMENT AND DETERRENCE, by 

Allen R. Ferguson. Santa Monica, Calif., The 

Rand Corp., 1962. 41 p. (P-2553.)

The purpose of this discussion is to lay out some of the questions relevant to disarmament, to suggest some ways of approaching them, and, in par- ticular, to suggest some avenues for discussion and for serious future work. This paper is an attempt "to put into perspective deterrence, disarmament, and approaches to peace. The three are viewed as complementary elements in a solution to the problem posed by the political conflict between the democracies and the Soviet Union when both parties have the power of massive destruction." 

"MINIMAL DETERRENT” VS. SATURA- TION PARITY, by Leo Szilard, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 3 (March 1964) 6-12. "We are close to the point where America and Russia could destroy each other to any degree and, therefore, one would perhaps think that the arms race is about to come to an end. In fact, a new arms race might be just around the corner

In the course of the last year, Russia has accepted the notion that America as well as Russia may retain a small strategic striking force until the 'end of the third stage' of the 'disarmament agreement,' and that inspection shall not be limited to equipment which is to be destroyed, but be extended also to equipment which is being retained. 

We shall have to explore whether the Russians means the same thing as we do when they appear to accept the principle of 'minimal deterrent.' We shall be able to discover this, however, only if we first find out what we mean ourselves when we speak of this principle 

It is... [the author's] contention that we need to reduce the strategic striking forces down to the level of the 'minimal deterrent' as soon as possible, because of the perils we face when we reach the end of the current transitional period." 

PRESENT-DAY DETERRENTS, by Air 

Vice Marshal E. J. Kingston-McCloughry, in The 

Royal United Service Institution Journal, v. 108, no. 629 (February 1963) 47–52.

The principal features of all present-day deterrence are three-fold: solidarity of alliance or national aim, adequate forces strategically deployed in the area or areas concerned; and the capacity to retaliate and the will to war. The dimensions assumed in the public imagination by the deterrent of thermonuclear weapons tend to overshadow the fact that some features are characteristic of the lesser exigencies of limited, local, and even cold war.

THE ROMAN EMPIRE VS. PERSIA, 363502: A STUDY OF SUCCESSFUL DETER- 

RENCE, by Vern L. Bullough, in The Journal of 

Conflict Resolution, v. 7, no. 1 (March 1963) 55–68. "For some four hundred years Rome and Sassanid Persia were the two great powers of Western 

Eurasia. Between them they dominated the world from the Atlantic to the Indus. Throughout most 64

of this period they represented antagonistic ideologies, with each seeing the other's emissaries as ideological subversives whose propaganda and actions threatened internal peace and security. Each would have destroyed the other if possible, yet early in their rivalry they realized that a decisive victory of one over the other was highly unlikely, if not impossible. Each had other enemies. which helped stabilize peaceful relationships between them because it led to believe that peace with each other was important, if not essential, to their continued survival. Perhaps because of these factors the peace which was established in 363 A.D. lasted for almost a century and a half." The author compares the deterrence factors present during this period of peace. With references. 

STALEMATE-OR? by Hanson W. Baldwin, in U.S. Naval Institute Proceedings, v. 90, no. 4 (April 1964) 48–55. "... Two topical problems in this nineteenth year of the atomic age-the stoppage of nuclear weapons and the proliferation of nuclear weapons-emphasize the grotesque dimensions of the so-called stalemate of terror in which we live.. 

There have been, within the past year or so, several major developments in the Soviet Union and in the United States in the continuing attempts... to define nuclear grand strategies calculated to strengthen their security. These have included both military developments and theoretical expositions. (The author discusses Russian and U.S. strategic concepts.) The author concludes that "the greatest deterrent to war will be not merely and not only a clear-cut U.S. nuclear superiority.

The will to use it in defense of vital interests is an essential element of the deterrent, and it must include a demonstrable . . superiority by the 

United States in military technology and in other forms of power throughout the military spectrum.’ 

USES OF VIOLENCE, by H. L. Nieburg, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 7, no. 1 

(March 1963) 43–54. "The threat of violence, and the occasional outbreak of real violence (which gives the threat credibility), are essential elements in conflict resolution not only in international, but also in national communities. Individuals and groups, no less than nations, exploit the threat as an everyday matter. This fact induces flexibility and stability in democratic institutions and facilitates peaceful social change."

WHAT IS A WAR DETERRENT? by Dale 

O. Smith, in Orbis, v. 7, no. 1 (Spring 1963) 96104. "Broadly speaking, the search for a war deterrent has given birth to two conflicting schools of thought. One, the traditional school . . . is that of military superiority. Through our military superiority, according to this school, the rival will be deterred from attacking. The other concept is rather new to military thought and has been arrived at through intellectual speculation. It is born of the despair of achieving a true military superiority." This second school argues that “if each country possesses relatively 'hard' intercontinental nuclear weapons and neither coun- try can intercept the other's missiles, each country's cities are hostage to the other. In this way, each side can withstand a first strike and retaliate with enough nuclear power to destroy the other's principal centers of population. A mutual threat of so frightening a kind is believed to provide a mutual deterrent to war. Such an arrangement is called a stable deterrence." The author concludes that "if military superiority provides the best chance of deterring war and of winning should war occur, and of causing fewer casualties, a policy of seeking to assure this superiority would seem to offer the American people the best protection available in the nuclear age."

X FACTORS IN DETERRENCE, by George 

Fielding Eliot, in Military Review, v. 44, no. 9 (September 1964) 22–30. "The stock-in-trade of a deterrent strategy is credibility . . . . In the specific instance of American deterrence of the Soviet Union from the offensive use of nuclear weapons against the United States or her allies, the criterion of the credibility of our deterrent power is primarily whether the Soviet leadership believes itself to be, in fact, deterred-believes that the consequences of nuclear war to the Soviet Union would be inescapably disastrous, and, hence, that nuclear war is not an acceptable instrument of Soviet policy The result of continued stop-and-go, rationed application of force under conditions which forbid the effective attainment of whatever military objective may be indicated, could, if long continued, have a fatal effect on the credibility of our deterrent against nonnuclear aggressions of any variety." 65 766-250-65-

I. Economic and Sociological Aspects ALTERNATIVES TO ARMAMENT EX- PENDITURES, by Wassily Leontief, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 6 (June 1964) 19-21.

In assessing the long-range implications of alternatives to armament expenditures, the author also examines the uses to which the output of our economic machine is being put now, before disarmament.

Julius Duscha, in Harper's, v. 228, no. 1366 (March 

1964) 39-64.

This is the first part of three articles on the interlocking relationship between Congress, the Pentagon, and defense contractor-and on its cost to the taxpayer. The second part appears in the 

April issue, and the third part, in the May issue, and is entitled, "The Disarmament Blues." concluding part estimates our chances for a safe economic passage to disarmament, and suggests plans for overcoming the fears of depression that held us back.

The ARMS BUDGETS AND THE SPACE FRONTIER, by Edward C. Welsh, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 47, no. 7 (July 1964) 43–44.

Under conditions of defense cutback, careful government-industry planning could allow for diversion of a sizable portion of funds, engineering capability, and management skills to an increased space effort.

CAN OUR ECONOMY STAND DISARMA- MENT? by Gerard Piel, in The Atlantic, v. 210, no. 3 (September 1962) 36-40.

The author prefaces his remarks with statements showing that our civilian population is vulnerable to nuclear attack, as he summarizes the nuclear strategy of the U.S. He shows that in the process of evolving our strategy, the military is of a magnitude which has a very significant effect on our economy. In cases of disarmament, he feels, we can offset the reduction in the arms budget by worthwhile and "overdue investment in the upgrading of our human and material resources and the enhancement of our domestic existence." He also is of the opinion that "we are unlikely to get disarmament unless we are ready to embrace and vigorously advance the economic alternatives to armament." He admits that in all he has said he has "dealt with the state of our nation in isolation from the world-wide political crisis that so heavily conditions our domestic situation."

CAN WE AFFORD A WARLESS WORLD! 

by Kenneth Boulding, in Saturday Review, v. 45, no. 40 (6 October 1962) 17-20.

The author discusses the economic effects of disarmament. He considers the U.S. transition problems soluble, "but only given astute efforts by U.S. politicians to educate people. The danger to the 

Communist system lies in increased affluence leading to increased liberty."

"A study of the economic impact of disarmament, written by fifteen economists, presenting specific possibilities for general and complete disarmament that would avoid economic catastrophe and would take advantage of a unique opportunity to release resources-both human and financialto serve major national goals. An appendix tabulates the world's burden of national defense.

DISARMAMENT AND THE ECONOMY, by 

Ben B. Seligman, in Commentary, v. 5, no. 35 (May 1963) 369–377. "Disarmament need not result in economic catastrophe: the necessary economic knowledge is at hand for dealing with the problems that would arise. What remains to be created is a sense of urgency in high places over the need to prepare now, and an awareness throughout our society that with proper planning and forethought, disarmament would be an economic contingency to be welcomed, not a disaster to be feared."

The Economist Intelligence Unit is sponsored by the United World Trust. This survey assesses the economic consequences of disarmament in the 

United Kingdom, and has two objectives: to ascertain the extent and character of the economic resources at present employed by the current total defense budget of the UK government, and to consider in the light of this information a number of alternative policies for reemployment of economic resources which would be released in the event of disarmament in the UK.

ECONOMIC IMPACTS OF DISARMA- MENT. Washington, United States Arms Con trol and Disarmament Agency, 1962. 28 p. 

(Economic Series 1.) 

This report was prepared by a panel of experts under the Chairmanship of Professor Emile Benoit of Columbia University. The report ex amines the problem of the economic impact of disarmament on the basis of a disarmament model devised by the panel, and concludes that, "while the economic problems which may be expected in the event of disarmament are by no means insuperable, these problems do require the development of sensible adjustment policies and vigorous government leadership for solution. The impact of disarmament would only accentuate structural problems which already exist as the result of technological advance and other developments in our economy.... If these problems are overcome, achievements of major national goals will be greatly facilitated by the use of the human and physical resources released from the defense program." 

THE ECONOMIC IMPACTS OF DIS- ARMAMENT, by Francis P. Hoeber, in Orbis, v. 7, no. 3 (Fall 1963) 631–647.

In recent years several efforts have been made to assess the economic implications of disarmament. "The best known and in many ways the most valuable of these" is the Report of the Panel on Economic Impacts of Disarmament, the socalled "Benoit Panel." This panel, a group of nine economists from government, industry, and labor unions working under the chairmanship of 

Prof. Emile Benoit of Columbia University, was organized at the request of John J. McCloy while he was Adviser on Disarmament to President

Eisenhower. The panel addressed itself in effect to the question of whether or not disarmament would create a serious depression in the U.S. This article evaluates the report on two levels. First, does it adequately answer this question? Second, has the panel asked-or was it asked-the right question?

NONECONOMIC FACTORS IN THE IN- STITUTIONALIZATION OF THE COLD 

WAR, by Irving Louis Horowitz, in American 

Academy of Political and Social Science, Annals, v. 351 (January 1964) 110-120. "The problem of converting world-war industry to peacetime enterprise is being seriously posed for the first time since the outbreak of World War II.

Thus far, the center of concern has been the economic feasibility of the transition for an industrial complex. There are also noneconomic factors. which produce a lag between technical know-how for the reconversion process and the practical implementation of steps toward industrial reconversion.... 

Disarmament and conversion to peacetime industrial policies would have to be phased in sociologically as well as economically. A policy of heavy arms expenditures has generated profound social and political disorientation. No major shifts in the production orientation of American industry will occur until doubts about the future of American economy are either assuaged or overcome by still greater trepidations about the future of the human community as such."

NUCLEAR ARMS CONTROL, by Michael G. 

Macdonald and Arnold Mitchell. Menlo Park, Calif., Stanford Research Institute, 1961. 20 p. (Report no. 107.) "International nuclear arms control agreements would have 2 types of effects on U.S. military markets. The need for inspection against violations would create numerous instrumentation, communications, and other specialty markets. The existence of arms control agreements governing specific weapons systems could have dramatic impacts upon military procurement by forcing strategic reliance on nonbanned weaponry. Almost any nuclear arms control measure would increase expenditures for national security for at least 3 to 5 years after its inception. Military spending will drop only when, and if, successful arms control can erode international mistrust and lead to general disarmament. A ban on testing of nuclear weapons would probably cost participating nations $5 billion to $7 billion over 3 to 

5 years 

A ban on placing nuclear weapons in orbit would necessitate teams of on-site inspectors operating systems capable of detecting nuclear warheads hidden in launch vehicles. A ban on the possession of nuclear weapons is, at the moment, technically infeasible because of the impossibility of detecting purposely concealed 

A ban on production of nuclear weapons would require massive industrial inspection systems coupled with extensive laboratory analysis facilities and radiometric land surveys."

SOME DIFFICULTIES OF SELF- ENFORCING ARMS AGREEMENTS, by Paul Y. Hammond, in The Journal of Conflict Resolu tion, v. 6, no. 2 (June 1962) 103–115. "The search for reality and practicality in arms controls proposals has become an exercise in extended speculation and an extended effort to eliminate the unessential. The ending place for the latter is now clearly in sight: proposals for unilateral measures to be taken to reduce the risk of unintentional warfare . While the prospects of these proposals are bright, their possibilities are limited. Lying just short of them, with dimmer prospects and seemingly greater possibilities, is self-enforcement-that is, arms control agreements which do not require an international agency, or some other third party, to enforce them In the inquiry developed, in the paper, the author confines himself to the two general cases which seem to impose the least burden of viability upon the negotiated arrangements."

THE IMMEDIATE STEPS TOWARD DIS- ARMAMENT, by Franklin A. Long, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 2 (February 

1964) 7-9. 69

The author presents the view that "complete and general disarmament is a desirable end goal, but that it may perhaps be best approached by means more modest and indirect than frontal assault." 

IMMEDIATE STEPS TOWARD GEN- ERAL AND COMPLETE DISARMAMENT, by F. A. Long, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 1963/64) 1–9.

This paper is a slightly shortened version of one presented to the Eleventh Pugwash Conference on 

Science and World Affairs at Dubrovnik, Yugoslavia, in September 1963. "The attempt to negotiate a full treaty for general and complete disarmament in a world where no disarmament has yet taken place and where suspicion and uncertainty abound presents several difficulties, one of which is that it tends to lead to more stringent inspection requirements than might have been imposed in a different political climate. If the total disarmament program could be negotiated in a step by step fashion, it might be possible at each step to lighten the inspection burden and also to conduct successive negotiations in an improving political climate. The cumulative effect of disarmament steps in improving attitudes and removing suspicion is probably one of the most important features of the disarmament process. A significant initial reduction of some of the most important types of weapons could be safely carried out without inspecting the remainder. After the first step, further processes should be relatively more easy."

A review of the status of negotiations and the result of the "years of meetings and discussions."

Also the current proposals in Geneva; and comparison of positions on reduction of military potentials, balanced stages, timing and transition, verification and control, peace-keeping machinery, signatory states, and measures included in the draft plans aimed at reduction of the risk of war. 

2. Disarmed World: Problems and Prospects ALTERNATIVE MODELS OF A DIS- 

ARMED WORLD, by Arthur I. Waskow, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 

1963/64) 59-74. "The models of a disarmed world put forward by the United States and the Soviet Union are analyzed. The first is found to imply a world government, with the difficulties inherent in attempting to manage such an institution in the absence of world political concensus; the second to imply a world of intense political conflict unmitigated by a transitional institution capable of enforcing the disarmament agreement, with the difficulties inherent in attempting to keep such a world disarmed. A third model is proposed, in which a world police institution is so deployed as to be capable of enforcing disarmament but incapable of becoming a government, and in which states continue to pursue their own national interests through the use of 'non-lethal equivalents of war","

ARMS CONTROL AND WORLD GOVERN- MENT, by Lincoln P. Bloomfield, in Survival, v. 4, no. 6 (November-December 1962) 260-266. "Comprehensive proposals for arms control demand a degree of world government. If this concept is ever to become a reality, the organization and distribution of power must be carefuly considered beforehand. This article discusses the problems to be solved and suggests how strategic analysis may help to do so." Reprinted from 

World Politics, July 1962. 

[NATIONS WITHOUT ARMS], in SRI 

Journal, v. 6, no. 2 (1962) 18-48.

This issue consists entirely of the three following papers: Altered Patterns of Power, by Michael 

A. Macdonald and Jean W. Nelson; Altered Patterns of War, by Leonard Wainstein; and Altered 

Patterns of Economics, by William S. Royce. The authors offer insights into that hypothetical world where arms have been largely eliminated but where conflict between nation-states remain. They discuss the problems which general and complete disarmament might create.

THE ROLE OF DETERRENCE IN TOTAL DISARMAMENT, by Thomas C. Schelling, in 

Foreign Affairs, v. 40, no. 3 (April 1962) 392–406.

This paper is "a warning against the notion that there is any once-for-all solution to the problems of world peace and government. It is against the notion that if only disarmament is 'total' enough, we can forget about deterrence and all that. It is against the notion that under 'total' disarmament there is no military potential to be controlled, balanced and stabilized." To determine whether and how disarmament might make war less likely, we have to look at what the mili- 70

I tary opportunities, risks, dangers, fears and potential capabilities would be in a disarmed world. If nations now suspect each other of contemplating war, we have to suppose that they might suspect each other of contemplating rearmament. If nations are willing to risk war, or to threaten it, they i certainly might risk rearming or threatening to rearm. Nations thought capable now of being panicked into war might be panicked into rearmament. "To suppose the contrary is to assume away the problem that disarmament is intended to help solve." Contents: General War in a Dis armed World; A War of Nuclear Mobilization;

Limited War in a Disarmed World; The Deter rence of Rearmament in a Disarmed World; Attitudes Toward Rearmament; An International Military Authority.

THE SOVIET VIEW OF A DISARMED WORLD, by J. I. Coffey, in The Journal of Con flict Resolution, v. 8, no. 1 (March 1964) 1-6.

It appears to the author that one basic cause for the failure of the U.S. and U.S.S.R. to agree on a program for general and complete disarmament lies in the differing concepts of a future world order held by the Soviet Union and the U.S. In this paper the author outlines the Soviet view of a disarmed world, and he assesses some of its implications for U.S. national interests. With references.

THE WEST IN A WORLD WITHOUT 

WAR, by Neil W. Chamberlain. New York, McGraw-Hill, 1963. 85 p. "Two alternatives confront both the old nations and the new. Either the great powers will reach agreement on controlled nuclear disarmament or they will not. If they do not, the probability of a nuclear holocaust grows in proportion both to the increasing complexity of weapons systems, which can malfunction, and to the acquisition of atomic arms by others. But if an agreement is reached, it must be enforced, and the enforcement agency acquires with the functions of control all of the power inherent in the frightful alternative to obedience by the signatory nations. The control agency may be expected to be part of the 

United Nations machinery; and the United Nations can be dominated by its Asian and African majority, who would no longer need to fear the threats and counterthreats of the advanced Western nations-including both the Soviet Union and the United States. These are the conditions of the future which Professor Chamberlain explores." 

M. History

DISARMAMENT AND THE COLD WAR IN THE THIRTIES, by Hugh R. Wilson. New 

York, Vantage Press, 1963. 87 p. • • "There are certain close parallels between the period through which we are now passing and the nineteen thirties 

In the nineteen thirties, the great powers of the world made several fruitless attempts at disarmament." Examining the files belonging to his father, a career diplomat, the author "found. some material on disarmament and some light on what might be called the cold war prototype." The author states that "we are at present entering a period of disarmament negotiations particularly concerned with nuclear weapons. 

The generation of the thirties viewed unrestricted air bombardment of cities, which was then still largely speculative, as an unmitigated horror which might destroy civilization itself . . . The air weapon was regarded much as we consider the possibilities of the Intercontinental Ballistics Missile." With short bibliography. 

DISARMAMENT IN HISTORICAL PER- SPECTIVE, by Stanley L. Falk, in Military Re view, v. 44, no. 12 (December 1964) 36–48. "Early disarmament attempts were . . . crude or brutal, and arose less from complicated rational or moralistic concepts than from the simple notion that weapons in the hands of an enemy were a constant and deadly threat. More sophisticated rationales developed later, but the basic reasoning behind man's first efforts has changed but little over the centuries. A glance at the early history of disarmament attempts is thus both revealing and instructive-it indicates that recent endeavors and frustrations are not without precedent. The goals and objectives are generally the same, although the weapons and the price of failure are obviously changed.”

POWER, by Philip Van Slyck. Boston. Beacon 

Press, 1963. 186 p. “A guide ... on problems of disarmament and strengthening the United Nations." With a preface by Senator Hubert H. Humphrey. Each of the nine essays in this book analyzes some important aspect of the present world system-from the use of force to the uses of law. Each essay also discusses various proposals for modifying, strengthening or reconstructing the present world sys- 71

tem, including proposals for strengthened international law and for total disarmament under world law. O. Moral Issues

ARMS AND THE WESTERN CON- SCIENCE, by James E. Dougherty, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 47, no. 7 (July 1964) 36-40. "To emphasize that Western society is by no means perfect is not at all to prove that our civilization is no better than that of our totalitarian antagonists. When the spiritual ideals, social values, and the institutions of the West are measured against the realities of the Communist system, Western liberal civilization emerges as superior and worth defending, with force if necessary.

The mere existence of modern weaponry may be tragic but it is not immoral, for morality is determined by purpose and intention. Deterrence is not the total answer to the defense of the West, but without deterrence the long road to peace with honor and freedom cannot be safely traversed."

NUCLEAR WEAPONS AND THE CON- FLICT OF CONSCIENCE, ed. by John C. Ben nett. New York, Charles Scribner's Sons, 1962. 188 p. 

The ethical issues involved in the nuclear arms race. Included in the book are two chapters which state the problem in the light of history of warfare, the current international situation and scientific estimates about the effect of nuclear weapons; then there are four chapters which are intended to deal with assumptions which should govern the attempt to find solutions to our problem. The final chapter discusses the effect of man's new destructive powers on the meaning of history and on faith in providence and human destiny. The chapters are: International Politics and the Nuclear Dilemma, by John H. Herz; The 

Nature of Nuclear War, by David R. Inglis; Ethical Aspects of Nuclear Dilemma, by Kenneth W. 

Thompson; Moral Urgencies in the Nuclear Context, by John C. Bennett; Explorations Into the 

Unilateral Disarmament Position, Erich Fromm;

The Case for Making "Just War" Possible, by 

Paul Ramsey; and Faith and the Perilous Future, by Roger L. Shinn. Reading List. 

PACEM IN TERRIS, by Pope John XXIII, in Survival, v. 5, no. 4 (July-August 1963) 146147.

The Encyclical Pacem in Terris, of which extracts are printed here, considers the possibility of accidental war today, advocates a suspension of nuclear tests and in particular questions whether it is possible to conceive of a just war fought with nuclear weapons.

P. Negotiation Problems and Techniques AMERICAN SCIENTISTS AND NU- CLEAR WEAPONS POLICY, by Robert Gil pin. Princeton, N.J., Princeton University Press, 1962. 352 p. "A study of the political behavior of American scientists since 1944. The place of scientific developments in our economic and military systems has become so important that the need for the scientists' advice on policy-making is quite generally recognized. Gilpin's thesis is that such advice cannot be purely scientific but must necessarily contain a very large political component, reflecting the scientists' views of our national goals and their estimates of the political and military effects of the various possible lines of action." The author's "chief emphasis is on the role of scientists in the controversy about a nuclear test ban." 99 

ARMS AGREEMENT: A MODEL OF STALEMATE, by Robert A. Levine, in The 

Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 6, no. 4 (1962) 308-318. "In reviewing the efforts of the past decade and a half to limit by international agreement the numbers and uses of military weapons, one theme dominates all others-that of failure. Over the entire period, little progress has been made toward 72

I any agreement which would limit any significant aspect of armaments. This paper is an enquiry 

1 into the underlying causes of this stalemate in the hope that analysis of these causes may assist in finding paths along which it is possible to move forward. It may well be that the basis for the failures lies in viewpoints and interests of the world's two major military power blocs which are so divergent that they are and will remain impossible of reconciliation, but the hypothesis here is that while these interests may indeed be irreconcilable today, this is not necessarily the case for all time." 

With references.

BALANCE SHEET ON DISARMAMENT, by John J. McCloy, in Foreign Affairs, v. 40, no. 3 (April 1962) 339-359.

As a new round of arms control and disarmament negotiations began between the U.S. and 

U.S.S.R. in early 1962, the author felt that it would be worthwhile and useful to set down "at least some of the salient affirmative and negative factors involved, and then try to form a judgment as to the chances of constructive action in the measurable future." In appraising the possibilities of obtaining constructive disarmament results, he records another set of negative occurrences which took place mainly in 1961; and discusses the underlying factors on which the prospects for success in disarmament can be judged. He not only identifies points of mutual interest, but also the underlying points of divergence for a realistic appraisal. 

A BARGAINING MODEL FOR DISARMA- MENT NEGOTIATIONS, by Eugene Singer, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 7, no. 1 (March 1963) 21-25. 

"Conventional types of verbal bargaining have, at least until now, proved. . . inadequate to deal with the immense difficulties involved in negotiating thousands of different classes of weapons. If countries ever seriously decide to disarm themselves of weapons, new methods of bargaining will... be needed. Without considering the important problem of inspection or the political wisdom of disarmament, this paper explores the application of the bargaining technique of 'divide and choose' to disarmament negotiations. . . In this paper one party, who is called the divider, is allowed to place the total weapons of both countries into two groups. Each group includes some weapons which the countries will maintain, and others which they will disarm. The other party, called the chooser, is then able to determine the final disarmament solution by selecting one of the two weapon groups."

COMMUNICATION AND AND AMERICAN- SOVIET NEGOTIATING BEHAVIOR, by Ed win H. Fedder, in Background, v. 8, no. 2 (August 

1964) 105-120.

This paper is concerned with one aspect of the negotiation process, that of communication among negotiators with special reference to U.S.-Soviet negotiating behavior. With references.

THE CONFRONTATION AT GENEVA, by Homer A. Jack, in War/Peace Report, v. 4, no. 5 

(May 1964) 6-8.

The author feels that although the talks at the 

Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Conference in Geneva "often seem discouraging . . . they serve a highly useful purpose." He presents a capsule survey of the proposals under consideration presenting both U.S. and Soviet positions.

Communist Party.

This attack, however, must be a cautious one, conducted with sensitivity to the complexity of the terrain and its built-in pitfalls, and in as highly coordinated a fashion as United States policy apparatus will permit."

INTERNATIONAL COMMUNICATION AND DECISION MAKING: AN EXERCISE OF THE INTER-NATION RESPONSE CY- 

CLE, by Anton S. Morton. Bedford, Mass.,

Raytheon Company, 1963. 84 p. (Project Unicorn BR-2246.) "A response cycle model of international relations was constructed to help understand the possibilities of unilateral arms control. The model centers around communication and decision making. The basic unit of the model is the act, which may be consummatory (carried through to movement, change, or other external effect upon the world, as in the everyday sense of 'act') or anticipatory (policy statements, symbolic gestures, announcements of intent). In the communications stage of the model, an act is emitted by a source nation, perceived (or not perceived) by the peripheral sensors of other nations, reported (or not) to medial evaluators, evaluated for its effects upon the interest of the medial evaluators' nation, and then reported (or not) to Central Decision 

Makes (CDM). At each stage, the information about the act is evaluated for credibility. In the model's decision making process, six basic types of action are possible. The type of action to be taken depends upon the nature of the act to which CDM are responding and upon their operational codes. The act type under consideration is ex- DO EC 20 JE 74

amined for its estimated effects upon the problem situation induced by the other nation's act, and upon its estimated effects upon other military, economic, external political, and internal political interests of the CDM. The action type, as it would be carried out by various means, is examined for its cost-utility. CDM choose the cheapest means. 

When the responding act has been emitted, a new response cycle begins. This model formed the framework for a detailed analysis of some aspects of the Korean War. The purpose of the analysis was to examine the usefulness of the model in contributing to new insights about arms control in a specific situation, and to point up gaps and inadequacies in the model. Conclusions were derived from the analysis. Comparisons were then made, within the framework of these conclusions, with the 1962 Cuban crisis. Contrasts between the state of the art of arms control in 1950 and 

1962 were pointed out. 

i-TRUST: A NATION OF TRUST IN THREE-PERSON GAMES AND INTERNA- TIONAL AFFAIRS, by Bernhardt Lieberman, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 8, no. 3 

(September 1964) 271–280. "In the many debates about the conduct of international affairs, though not necessarily in the actual conduct of such affairs, there is considerable concern with the notion of trust. A number of questions are often debated. Among them are the following: should we trust another nation or group of nations? Is it important, possible, or sensible to trust a nation, or a group of nations, in the conduct of international affairs? Once a treaty is signed to limit arms, to refrain from arming, or to take or refrain from some action, can or should a nation or group of nations be trusted?" 

With references.

THE KEY TO DISARMAMENT. London, Her Majesty's Stationery Office, 1964. 71 p.

NEW BOOK ON DIPLOMATIC LAW, by P. 

Vladimirsky, in International Affairs (Moscow), no. 10 (October 1963) 97-98. 

A review of the book DIPLOMATIC AND CONSULAR LAW, by I. P. Blishchenko and V. 

N. Durdenevsky. Moscow, Institute of International Relations Publishing House, 1962. 480 pp. 

(Diplomaticheskoe i konsul'skoe pravo. Moskva, Izdatel'stvo IMO, 1962 god. 480 str.) "The present book is the first systematic study of this subject to appear in Soviet legal literature. It describes the system of foreign relations bodies (including temporary bodies such as diplomatic delegations, delegations to special administrative conferences, commissions at international exhibitions, etc.), analyses the question of the people as the decisive factor in international law and parliament's role as a foreign relations body, and describes the specific forms of diplomatic relations between the Socialist countries and their contribution to the development of diplomatic and consular law. . . One of the book's undoubted merits is that the establishment and development of new legal norms and institutions are analysed in the light of the changed role of foreign relations bodies in dealings between states. Two factors affect the process: the predominant, internal factor a state's social and economic structure, the aims and tasks of its ruling class, and the external one-the international situation, world alignment of forces, the nature of a state's foreign ties. 

After deep and thorough analysis the authors conclude that diplomatic and consular law is 'that form of dealings between states in which it is possible to solve international issues, preserve and develop peace and peaceful co-existence, and strengthen the community of the Socialist nations in order to ensure the building of Communism'." 

The reviewer regrets that the authors of the book gave little attention to the UN "whose importance today is due to its dual role as the chief international forum and the arena of struggle between the two opposed social and economic systems."

POLITICAL NEGOTIATION AS A PROC- ESS OF MODIFYING UTILITIES, by Fred 

C. Ikle and Nathan Leites, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 6, no. 1 (March 1962) 19-28. 

A variety of bargaining processes in real life can be understood by means of theoretical models. 

But when we wish to study political negotiations, these theoretical tools seem to be of limited use for the complex reality. "We must first come to grips with the reality of political negotiations in a more systematic fashion before we can relate current bargaining theory to it. This paper constitutes the initial output of a continuing study on political negotiations. The aim of the larger study is to develop a theory of negotiations; the aim of this paper is to present a formalization that can be used towards the construction of such a theory." With references.

THE POLITICS OF ARMS CONTROL: 

TROIKA, VETO, AND INTERNATIONAL INSTITUTIONS, by L. P. Bloomfield. Wash ington, Institute for Defense Analyses, 1961. 28 p. 

(Special Studies Group, Study Memorandum No. 

3.) 

This paper examines "some of the more acute and controversial problems of organization and politics that arise in seeking to negotiate agreements for disarmament and arms control. One of the central issues of disarmament has to do with the organization of controls to prevent cheating and, beyond that, the elaboration of appropriate international institutions to fill the military-and political-vacuum which significant elimination of national arms would presumably leave.

Negotiations on a nuclear test ban seem to have foundered on the first count, and negotiations for comprehensive disarmament will predictably run afoul of the second. This paper explores some aspects of the organizational-institutional problem as it bears on the negotiating posture of the United 

States on both counts.

THE POLITICS OF DISARMAMENT: A STUDY OF SOVIET-AMERICAN GAMES- MANSHIP, by John W. Spanier and Joseph L. 

Nogee. New York, Praeger, 1962. 240 p. "The authors analyze the role disarmament negotiations play in the attainment of foreign policy objectives, how negotiations themselves are used as a weapon in the cold war. Traces the disarmament negotiations from 1946 proposals for international control of atomic energy to the 18 Nation Disarmament Committee meetings in Geneva, and analyzes both the objectives sought by each side and the means used to realize them.”

POSTWAR NEGOTIATIONS FOR ARMS CONTROL, by Bernhard G. Bechhoefer. Wash ington, Brookings Institution, 1961. 641 p. "A complete historical review and analysis of 

Post World War II arms control and disarmament negotiations through five phases: (1) UN negotiations, 1946–48, (2) complete frustration, 1948–51, (3) comprehensive proposals, 1952-54, (4) search for partial disarmament, 1955-57, and (5) recent negotiations, 1958-60."

THE ROLE OF THE NEUTRALS IN THE GENEVA NEGOTIATIONS, by M. Samir 

Ahmed, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1 (Summer 1963) 25–32. "The author assesses the activities of the eight neutral delegations now that a year has elapsed since their appearance at Geneva. They are 

Brazil, Ethiopia, India, Mexico, Nigeria, Sweden, and U.A.R. It is generally agreed that the inclusion of these new members had a sobering and moderating effect on the atmosphere of the negotiations. . . . The eight have proved themselves impartial in their criticism, and have constituted a loose diplomatic group rather than an old-fashioned bloc. They should be able to continue to perform valuable services as conciliators between the main parties."

SOVIET NEGOTIATING BEHAVIOR AT THE GENEVA DISARMAMENT CONFER- 

ENCE, by Bryant Wedge and Cyril Muroncew. 

Princeton, N.J., Institute for the Study of National Behavior, 1963. 45 p.

Prepared for U.S. Arms Control and Disarma ment Agency in support of grant study ACDA/ E-28, this paper "attempts to construct a model of Soviet perception of current disarmament negotiations, based on the thesis that the Soviet Government views the world in terms of assumptions generally shared by its members. Soviet representatives must interpret the negotiations in terms of the reality world of the Soviet Government, as this is the only world they know."

THE STATESMAN'S APPROACH TO DIS- ARMAMENT, by Vincent P. Rock. Washington, Institute for Defense Analyses, International Studies Division, 1963. 34 p. (Research Paper 

76

P, Study Phoenix Paper 4-For the U.S. Arms Control and Disarmament Agency.) This paper was written for Study Phoenix: 

Common Action for the Control of Conflict, on analysis of the present and potential scope of interdependence between the U.S. and the Soviet 

1 Union. "Two interrelated issues have been the chief obstacles to disarmament agreements since 

1945: the preservation of the security of the parties to an agreement and the method of initiating disarmament. The 'disarmament expert's approach' - to disarmament, which has dominated postwar negotiations, has failed to cope adequately with these problems. The 'stateman's approach' to disarmament, as set forth in this paper, would be based on the realization that at each step in the disarming process both sides will attempt to maximize their power at the lower force levels. The 'stateman's approach' would accept this fact explicity, if not publicily, recognize that the occasion may arise when nations will find it necessary to reverse the disarming process for a time.”

UNREALITY AT GENEVA, by David Lilien thal, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 46, no. 4 (April 1963) 114-115. "Disarmament negotiations now-at the wrong time and under the wrong circumstances-actually can and do increase the very tensions they are designed to diminish. Getting rid of weapons in a dangerous world is no panacea for peace. It is a myth we ought to eschew. In a world of expanding technology . . . we should instead search for peace through an attack on the causes of conflict rather than attempting to treat the symptoms • • 

Q. Partial Reduction of Weapons and Ve hicles

ARMS RACE AND DISARMAMENT, by Jeremy J. Stone, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scien tists, v. 20, no. 7 (September 1964) 20-24. "The choice between arms race and disarmament has seldom been so clearly defined as it is today. 

It is hard to imagine a more likely beginning of a new round of the arms race than the decision, presently under consideration, to procure antiballistic missile systems. At the same time, it is hard to imagine a stronger case for a first major act of disarmament than the case for heavy bomber dismantlement, and, in fact, such an act would be similar to suggestions already made by the major powers. It seems useful to discuss these issues in parallel precisely because they seem to represent the present weapon-related choices between arms race and disarmament."

BOMBER DISARMAMENT, by Jeremy J. 

Stone, in World Politics, v. 17, no. 1 (October 

1964) 13-39. "This article deals with bomber disarmament as a partial measure which might be agreed upon by the two major powers in the absence of more general progress toward disarmament. However, much of it is relevant to the suggestion that bomber disarmament be linked to the U.S. proposal for a freeze on procurement of strategic weapons. The relationship between a strategic freeze and bomber disarmament is very close." In his conclusions the author suggests that both sides simply phase out (and destroy) B-47's and Badgers, and that they begin proportional (heavy) bomber disarmament by phasing out (and destroying) Soviet 

Bears and Bisons along with U.S. B-52's over the next few years.

THE ELIMINATION OF ROCKETS AS NUCLEAR DELIVERY VEHICLES, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Autumn 

1963) 184-188. "The implementation of disarmament proposals involving the elimination of rockets as nuclear vehicles would raise the problem of differentiating such rockets from booster rockets intended for legitimate space research, the manufacture of which would be allowed to continue. This risk can be minimized by an International Disarmament

Organization supervising the manufacture and launching of all satellites and space craft and ensuring that none of the growing civil potential is diverted to establish a nuclear delivery system. It would be a help if all launchings could be brought under international control."

QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS ON THE U.S. PRODUCTION FREEZE PROPOSAL, by Betty Goetz Lall, in Bulletin of the Atomic Sci entists, v. 20, no. 10 (December 1964) 30-34.

The U.S. proposal to freeze the number and characteristics of U.S. and U.S.S.R. offensive and defensive nuclear vehicles is one of the interesting measures introduced into the disarmament and arms control field during 1964. Essentially, the freeze means curtailing production of all strategic bombers, long-range missiles, and antimissile missiles. The author believes the freeze would result in substantial reductions in the projected U.S. 77

strategic force and would have a significant effect in curbing the arms race. Officially the Soviet negotiators object to the freeze, but privately some say it is a good proposal, though not yet "ripe" for solution. This may mean that the Soviet Union wants more time to increase its strategic forces before freezing production. The author, who believes the selection of vehicles to be included in the freeze is probably more favorable to the U.S. than to the U.S.S.R., suggests the formation of a panel of experts to recommend ways to make the freeze proposal more "equitable" to the Soviets.
