PEACEKEEPING AND THE UNITED NA- 

TIONS, in International Affairs, v. 40, no. 2 (April 1964) 191–204.

The author underlines the need for the UN to find ways to strengthen its peace-keeping capacity. "Peace-keeping in the widest sense, from conciliation and good offices to observation, truce supervision and the use of international military forces, has now become a practical necessity in the conduct of international affairs. It should be provided for, planned for, and it must be paid for, one way or another." The author also traces historically the concept of peace-keeping machinery. 

PEACE-KEEPING BY UN FORCE, FROM SUEZ TO THE CONGO, by Arthur Lee Burns and Nina Heathcote. New York, Praeger, 1963. 

256 p. (Princeton University Center of Inter national Studies.)

The authors "examine UN peace-keeping policies and machinery . . . from 1957 to January 

1962. They show how attitudes toward the use of 

UN forces and the actual use of these forces changed and developed during the period. The authors believe that ONUC (Organisation des Nations Unies au Congo) departures from strict impartiality occurred through 'contingent frailty and not from logical necessity,' and that 'on numerous and difficult occasions the UN... acted with scrupulous objectivity.' They suggest that the 

UN might well encourage neutral nations to train riot-police. ... whom it could make available for international operations."

PEACE-KEEPING: PROBLEMS FOR SMALLER COUNTRIES, by Hidejiro Kotani, in International Journal, v. 19, no. 3 (Summer 

1964) 308-325. "As demonstrated by the UN forces which were sent to various places since World War II, smaller countries can make a great contribution to pre- 79

venting a recurrence of local conflicts, to the maintenance of local peace and order, and to the stabilization of the local social, economical and political situation. If a future UN force sticks to the principle which the UN Emergency Force or the UN

Operations in the Congo adopted the principle that troops from the five permanent member countries of the UN Security Council shall be excluded from UN forces-the smaller countries' contribution will remain great."

UNITED NATIONS PEACE-KEEPING AND PROBLEMS OF POLITICAL CON- 

TROL, by G. S. Murray, in International Journal, v. 18, no. 4 (Autumn 1963) 442–457. · • • "In recent years, increasing attention has been given, both in official and private circles, to the peace-keeping role of the United Nations. 

The emphasis on peace and security has been sustained throughout the period of U.N. development. . . . The organization could stand or fall on the basis of its continuing achievement in this vital role [of peace-keeping operations].... The development of the United Nations' peace-keeping role, particularly in the past decade, has been largely a practical response to changing needs. . . . 

Experience points to the need for peace-keeping machinery, easily and promptly available. This international method has passed the experimental stage.... By beating out the brushfires of war, the United Nations can hope to hold the line for peace, while powers concerned move toward the kind of political accommodation which this survey has indicated rather than prescribed in detail." 

THE UN'S PEACE-KEEPING CRISIS, by 

Louis Halasz, in War/Peace Report, v. 4, no. 11 (November 1964.)

The background for the U.S.-U.S.S.R. collision over Soviet indebtedness for UN peace-keeping operations. Money is only an incidental part of the crisis; the underlying problem is the politics of peace-keeping, which is connected with the basic issue of national sovereignty versus world author ity. The Soviet Union contends the Security 

Council is the only body authorized to take action in the maintenance or restoration of international peace. The U.S. places this responsibility in the 

General Assembly. Although the UN faces a crisis in which its very existence may be at stake, the author feels a clash will be averted.

RESEARCH ON DISARMAMENT, by Rich ard J. Barnet, in Background, v. 6, no. 4 (Winter 

"The study of disarmament-how it might be achieved and how it might affect international and national behavior-is peculiarly elusive. . . . AFRICAN DISARMAMENT-A POSAL SECONDED, by A. H. Katz. Santa 

Monica, Calif., Rand Corp., 1964. 15 p. (P-2868.) "A summary of a proposal for African disarmament made by J. H. Mensah of Ghana with arguments for and against. ARMS CONTROL IN THE DEVELOPING AREAS, by Edward W. Gude and others. Bed ford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 155 p. 

(Project Unicorn BR-2253.) 

Eight essays on the strategic implications of

United States relations with the developing areas. "The first is a survey of United States strategy with respect to the developing areas outlining the major factors such as the relationship of these areas to the world environment of the 1960's, guerrilla warfare, communist strategy, the major threat to the developing countries, counterinsurgency tactics, and arms control implications within this arena. The second paper explores in greater detail the political and military context of the developing areas including the objectives as seen from their point of view, and United States capabilities in this environment. The third paper discusses the various arms control problems of the developing countries such as militaristic totalitarianism, primitives arms races, and potential armed action requiring control. The next paper is a more theoretical discussion of conflict and change in the developing countries. A construct of stability is developed for the purpose of providing a means for analyzing the impact of various measures on internal political violence

Also the impact of the army as an institution is analyzed in terms of the variables relevant to the construct. The fifth paper is a series of three preliminary case studies of the military in developing countries qualitatively analyzing their impact. The sixth paper discusses the limitations on civic action as a means to achieve both economic development and arms control objectives in the developing countries. The military in developing countries is analyzed in terms of five characteristics that affect its acceptance, implementation, and reaction to civic action The last paper is a discussion of arms control in the environment of Southeast Asia. The major United States security problems in the area are discussed in terms of post war political and military development." [For information on what 

Soviet strategy for arms control in the developing areas is as elucidated in Soviet literature, see DIS-

MILITARY TECHNOLOGY IN DEVELOP- ING COUNTRIES, by H. Roberts Coward. 

Cambridge, Massachusetts Institute of Technol ogy, Center for International Studies, 1964. Various paging. (C/64–5.) 

Prepared under contract with the U.S. Arms

Control and Disarmament Agency. Even as de veloping countries have urged the great powers to put aside their arms, these countries have found themselves facing urgent security needs at home.

In the resulting search for patterns and doctrines, a gap exists in the literature, with little attention being given to the current state of the military art in some developing smaller powers. What is necessary, therefore, is: a careful analysis of the requirements of the emerging nations for both external and internal security; a picture of the current stage of a country's military development, including its ability to absorb new techniques; and the role of the military in emerging countries. 

Partial contents: Military Forces; Resources for Military Efforts; The Diffusion of Military Technology. THE ROLE OF MILITARY ASSISTANCE IN THE PROBLEM OF ARMS CONTROL: 

THE MIDDLE EAST, LATIN AMERICA, AND AFRICA, by Catherine McArdle. Cam bridge, Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Center for International Studies, 1964. 97 p. (C/64-24.) 

Prepared under contract with U.S. Arms Con trol and Disarmament Agency. "Any examination of arms control proposals with respect to the developing areas must take into full consideration the role of military assistance with respect not only to the absolute level of regional armaments but also to the interests and commitments of both the aided nations and the major powers." The attempt is made in this paper "to sketch out a basis for such consideration, to describe the nature and scope of military assistance programs in the Middle East, Latin America, and Africa, and to suggest the significant problems these programs pose for any future regional arms control arrangements."

THEORETICAL ASPECTS OF UNILAT- ERAL ARMS CONTROL, by Clark C. Abt and others. Bedford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 93 p. (Project Unicorn BR-2250.) 

"This study examines some of the underlying theoretical problems of arms control doctrine, including assumption about the nature of military and international conflict. Part I is concerned with operational distinction between intrinsic and reciprocal unilateral arms controls, and some of the associated informational and doctrinal problems. Part II is a collection of essays of both the recent history of how and why wars began, and some hypothetical extrapolations from these historical studies significant for arms control policy. 

Concepts of escalation and stability are developed and criticized. Part III is a historically derived theory of alliance formation. The significance of alliance for war initiation, escalation, and termination is discussed with respect to arms control objectives. Part IV offers discussions of some substantive future problems areas, including the 

1970 spectrum of security situations, possible weapon systems operational by 1975, and some problems of future war termination and the aftermath."

UNILATERAL ARMS CONTROL: A SUR- VEY, by Barry M. Casper and John Phelps. 

Washington, Institute for Defense Analyses, Economic and Political Studies Division, 1963. 34 p. (Study FAIR Volume 3, Research Paper 

P-45.)

This paper, one part of the results of Study FAIR, Focus on Arms Information and Reassurance, was specifically supported by the Navy's Project Michelson. The aim of this paper is to "draw together some insights on unilateral arms control which have been developed in the last three or four years, and to consider briefly some specific possible control measures." The emphasis is practical rather than theoretical. The authors consider mainly "controls aimed at enhancing military security, as distinguished from initiatives aimed at tension-reduction per se." Their interest is "focused on the period 1965-75." Contents:

The Foreseeable Strategic Balance; Unilateral or Negotiated Controls?; Limited Information

Sources and Unilateral Arms Control Measures; 

Some Specific Aims of Unilateral Arms Control; 

Some Specific Measures; and Miscellaneous Read ings Pertinent to Unilateral Arms Control Questions.

UNILATERAL ARMS CONTROL AND FIXED OVERSEAS BASES, by James B. 

George and others. Bedford, Mass., Raytheon

Company, 1963. 68 p. (Project Unicorn BR- 

2245.) "Part I of the study, 'U.S. Overseas Bases in a 

Changing Strategic Environment,' examines the military, political, and economic utilities associated with fixed overseas bases in fourteen environmental contexts, and compares them with the utilities of our other types of bases in the same contexts. 

Weights are assigned to the military, political, and economic cost effectiveness of fixed overseas bases, comparing them to the effectiveness of the other four types of bases in the strategic environments of general war, limited war, internal warfare, and cold war. The areas available for U.S. unilateral arms control actions regarding overseas bases, as constrained by legal, political, and military-security factors, are then defined, and some potential uses of the criteria developed in the study are given. Part II of the study, "The Political Sig nificance of the Turkish Bases,' examines in some detail the historic, political, economic, and cultural factors which might operate as constraints on unilateral arms control actions by the U.S. in a spe cific area. Part III of the study, 'Arms Control Cost-effectiveness Analysis, An Illustrative Application to Fixed IRBM Overseas Bases' applies crude qualitative costs effectiveness considerations to the military political, and economic arms control, elements of a specific type of overseas base."

UNITED STATES UNILATERAL ARMS CONTROL MEASURES AND THE SETTING FOR DISARMAMENT NEGOTIATIONS, by 

Richard B. Foster and others. Menlo Park, Calif., Stanford Research Institute, 1961. 51 p. 

Prepared for the Office of the Assistant Secre tary of Defense for International Security Affairs. 

This paper presents and discusses the thesis that one possible policy which would service national security within certain rather stringent constraints would be one based on selected and consistent unilateral actions which demonstrably further the general objectives of arms control. The thesis may also be expressed as follows: "United States arms control policy should include the set of unilateral measures and policies that will provide the framework for selective and controlled competition with the Soviet in the fields in which a majority of the natural advantages lie with the West." 

X. Violations, Verification, and Inspection APPLICATIONS OF STATISTICAL METHODOLOGY TO ARMS CONTROL AND DISARMAMENT, SECTIONS I-IV, by

ARMS INSPECTIONS, by Lawrence S. Fink elstein. New York, Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, 1962. 89 p. CHEATING IN A DISARMED WORLD, by 

Robert Neild, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Autumn 1963) 133-143. "Using the technique of the strategic analyst and simplifying a disarmed world so that two gambling robots, free of all inhibitions, face one another, one may conclude that a nation which cheated would have to make a big surprise attack if it wanted to be reasonably sure of avoiding a counterattack. A threat of an attack based on concealed weapons would probably be inadequate, since the other side could threaten retaliation on the same basis: one side which succeeds in cheating must recognize that the other side could have cheated too. To be safe, the cheating nation would 85

have to cheat over defensive weapons as well as offensive. In other words, the cheating would have to be on a large scale and it would be technically possible to introduce an inspection system which would detect it. . . . Analyzed closely, the risk of cheating does not seem as awful as it is sometimes suggested."

FIRST STEP-SEALED RECORDS 

CACHES? by John C. Polanyi, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1 (Summer 1963) 3–19. 

"Any technique which might be used in the early stages of disarmament to check the validity of declared inventories without the assistance from a body of roving inspectors is worth examining.

Sealed records caches might be helpful in this connection. The contents of such caches become available only when the proper stage of disarmament has been reached. If agreed upon without great delay, this measure would provide a body of records more credible than those (covering the same period) which may become available some years hence as a consequence of disarmament. Moreover the collection of records in caches is something which can be undertaken right away and will constitute a confidence-building measure on the way to disarmament. The records would cover inventories of each of the categories specified in the disarmament proposals. The release of these records section by section, during the course of disarmament will reduce uncertainties in declared inventories, and make possible a corresponding postponement of the onset of inspection."

A GENERAL SURVEY OF RESPONSES TO VIOLATIONS, by Louis B. Sohn, in Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (April 1963) 95–104. 

"Various methods of providing graduated responses to disarmament treaty violations are discussed, and the use of legal arrangements to provide these responses is reviewed." Contents: 

Enforcement Through National Channels (Enact ment of National Legislation, National Investigations, National Tribunals, Advantages and Disadvantages); International Enforcement Against

Individuals; Unilateral Responses by Governments; International Responses (Removal of the 

Violation, Imposing a Fine on the Violator, Economic Sanctions, Military Sanctions); etc.

VERIFICATION, by Betty Goetz Lall, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 8 (October 

1964) 43-45. "One aspect of verification in a disarmament agreement that has been given insufficient attention is the exchange and utilization of information about inventories of national armaments, armed forces, military production, and expenditures.

This information is expected to be provided at the beginning of any major disarmament agreement (including a treaty for general and complete disarmament and any other disarmament agreement covering substantial reductions in weapons and production limitations). Despite the emphasis at the disarmament negotiations on the need for effective verification, neither the Soviet Union nor the United States has proposed concrete verification procedures."

INSPECTION AND PROTECTION IN ARMS REDUCTION, by J. David Singer, in Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1 (January 

1963) 56-72. "A control agency under an arms control agreement must provide assurance that the agreement is being adhered to and protection if the agreement is being violated. An inspection system provides information inputs as stimuli to such an agency, and the agency provides protection by means of adequate responses to counter, and deter, violations. The relationship between these two major activities is discussed." Contents: Some Basic Requirements; The Information-as-Stimulus Ac tivity; The Response-to-Information Activity;

Protection and Arms Transfer; Deterrence of V10

lations; Political Acceptability; etc. With list of references.

MEDIA ANALYSIS IN INSPECTION FOR DISARMAMENT, by J. David Singer, in Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 3 (July 1963) 248-260. 

"Open sources when analyzed by the method of content analysis may provide an important source of information to the signatories of an arms control agreement. The usefulness of this information and the techniques for obtaining it are discussed in this article." With references. 

ORIGINS OF THE UNITED STATES' IN- SPECTION POLICY: 1926-46, by Richard Dean 

Burns, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 2 (Spring 1964) 157–169. "The United States had a well-defined policy toward arms inspection and control long before the advent of the atomic bomb. This policy was the product of disarmament deliberations during the 1930's. While President Coolidge opposed in ternational inspection and control in principle (as he did U.S. participation in the League of Nations), his policy was a reflection of the prosperous, optimistic 1920's. As storm clouds gathered over the international community, President Hoover cautiously accepted inspection and control. President Roosevelt in 1933, however, fully embraced the concept of international, automatic arms inspection and control." With references.

p. (Publication 13.)

PROGRESSIVE ZONAL INSPECTION OF DISARMAMENT, by Clark C. Abt, in Disarma ment and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 1963/ 

64) 75-89. "Progressive zonal inspection of disarmament is analyzed in functional terms of zone configuration, selection, sealing, verification of declared contents, and search; for operational requirements, security threats to inspected and inspecting nations, and limitations of effectiveness in verifying disarmament. Aerial, mobile surface, and fixed location inspection operations are compared. Technical intelligence, and target, production, and inventory information, are related to disarmament verification and residual military security risks are compared for progressive zonal and nationwide graduated access inspection. Advantages and risks of zonally inspected disarmament are identified, and improvement is suggested by combination with nationwide inspection."

U.S. OUTLINES VERIFICATION MEAS- URES TO ASSURE COMPLIANCE WITH PROPOSED FREEZE OF STRATEGIC NU- CLEAR VEHICLES, by Clare H. Timberlake, in The Department of State Bulletin, v. 51, no. 1317 (21 September 1964) 413-417.

To Be Declared, Facilities To Be Declared, Verifi cation and Inspection Procedures, and Inspection of Launchers and Launching Sites. 

VERIFICATION, by Wayland Young, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 3 (Summer 

1964) 342-352. "The words 'inspection,' 'control,' 'checking' and 'verification' are examined etymologically and semantically. 'Verification' is preferred. Only statements can be verified. A given physical system for the collection of data may sometimes be able to verify statements of more than one type.

Some types of arms-control statements might be verified by systems set up not for arms control but for other purposes."

VERIFICATION AND RESPONSE IN DIS- ARMAMENT AGREEMENTS. Washington, Institute for Defense Analyses, 1962. v. 3 (Woods Hole Summer Study 1962.) On 20 June 1962 the U.S. Arms Control and 

Disarmament Agency contracted with the Institute for Defense Analyses to administer a Sum mer Study on Inspection and Control in Disarma ament Agreements. The study focused on verification and response but necessarily considered a number of related topics as well. The emphasis on verification reflected the view that while the problem of verification is by no means the only obstacle to disarmament, divergence on how verification shall be obtained has been sufficient to block progress. Response to suspected or proven violations was considered to be integrally related to the determination of what kind of verification is needed.

VERIFICATION OF THE DESTRUCTION OF ROCKETS AND BOMBERS, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 2 (Autumn 1963) 

189-192. "Methods for the actual destruction of nuclear delivery vehicles are considered, and also methods of checking that the weapons destroyed were up to operational standard and that substandard material was not deliberately sacrificed whilst the quality weapon was hidden. In the case of rockets, launchings would appear to provide the best answer, since they both demonstrate the quality of the material and see its ultimate destruction. An alternative would be the testing and destruction of some central ground station of the specialized navigational and other equipment intended for the rocket. In the case of aircraft, a demonstra- 200 e di

tion of quality by means of flight tests should not be difficult."

VERIFICATION REQUIREMENTS FOR RESTRICTIONS ON STRATEGIC NU- CLEAR DELIVERY VEHICLES. n.p. Ben dix Corporation, 1964. 31 p.

(Prepared for the

This pamphlet is a condensed and unclassified version of the final report of work performed by

"The objectives of the study were to develop verification requirements for various possible international agreements on the reduction of strategic nuclear delivery capabilities. VIOLATIONS OF DISARMAMENT AGREEMENTS, by Richard J. Barnet, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1 (Summer 

1963) 33-48. "Violations of a disarmament agreement are divided into substantive and procedural 

There may also be violations of obligations outside the disarmament agreement proper, such as making war on a small scale (so-called low-level conflict) while disarmament is in progress. While extreme cases of substantive violation (surprise ultimatum and attack) must be allowed for, the intention of the violator is, more likely to be determined by the extent to which the technical limitations of the inspectorate system or the irresolution of the other signatories might permit him to whittle away his obligations. Just as in the case of individuals, a combination of motivations would usually lie at the root of any violation. The dominant motivation should be recognized so that the reactions of the other signatories are kept on the right level. Over-reaction may be as dangerous as under-reaction. Ambiguous violations should be given the benefit of the doubt and everything 

I possible should be done to see the loss of 'face' does not motivate response.

ZONAL INSPECTION, by Louis B. Sohn, in 

Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 2 (Spring 1964) 204-207.

The concept of zonal inspection and disarmament has been modified greatly since 1960. It has already been proposed: to increase the number of zones; to select them more frequently; to restrict zone configuration. In addition, one could: start with a small number of zones in early years, increasing the number later; allow division of zones into non-contiguous sub-zones; limit inspection at first to major cities; and provide for a graduated inspection of factories, starting with outside inspection, then inspection of materials entering and leaving a factory, later inspecting production and shipment. Through such devices a proper proportion between disarmament and inspection can be achieved."

III. NUCLEAR ENERGY AND WEAPONS A. Diffusion and Proliferation ASPECTS OF NUCLEAR DIFFUSION, by 

Stanley L. Harrison, in Military Review, v. 52, no. 

9 (September 1962) 92–99.

Nuclear diffusion to additional nations could well be a factor in an eventual realignment of the international structure of relations among nations. 

The probability that additional nations will gain some measure of nuclear weapons capability in the future is very real. The author discusses: the growing nuclear club, the role of militant neutrals, allied and uncommitted nations, participation by Communist China, etc.

THE DISPERSION OF NUCLEAR WEAP- ONS; STRATEGY AND POLITICS, ed. by R. 

N. Rosecrance. New York, Columbia University

A collection of essays on diffusion proliferation of nuclear weapons.

THE DISSEMINATION OF NUCLEAR 

WEAPONS, by Charles T. Stewart, Jr., in Military Review, v. 44, no. 10 (October 1964) 3-8.

U.S. foreign policy has been founded on the premise that the dissemination of nuclear weapons to many nations would be regrettable and that every effort should be made to discourage it. Yet, some spread of nuclear weapons has already taken place, and a further spread seems inevitable. A

U.S. response, whether taking the form of efforts to prevent or slow, or to hasten and facilitate dissemination, cannot be described in generalities; it must be tailored to each unique set of circum stances. Contents: Who Has the Bomb?; War 

Risks; Catalytic War; Most Plausible Fear (Risk of Wars Between Smaller Nations); Deterrence 

Deliberate Dissemination; Containment; Game 

Theory; etc. 766-250-65 -7 89

THE NONPROLIFERATION OF NU- CLEAR WEAPONS, by Bernard T. Feld, in 

Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 10 (December 1964) 2-6. 

At present there are five nuclear nations, only two of which have adequate delivery systems. 

However, pressure to gain control and possession of nuclear weapons will increase. The current proposals at Geneva will, at best, only inhibit, but not stop, proliferation of nuclear weapons. The 

International Atomic Energy Agency of Vienna has neither the means nor the power to exercise effective control over the spread. The fundamental condition for nonacquisition of nuclear weapons can only be reached if and when the two superpowers cease to rely on nuclear weapons as a major ingredient of their armed forces, or as a component in the conduct of international relations. Only then will other nations be willing to consider stringent limitations on their own nuclear weapons development. The author sees in the 

MLF a potential impediment to future nuclear disarmament negotiations. For once a nation possesses a share in a nuclear weapons system, the system cannot be abolished without that nation's explicit agreement.

PREVENTING THE SPREAD OF NU- CLEAR WEAPONS, by Marvin Kalkstein, in 

Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, v. 20, no. 10 (December 1964) 18-19.

A ban on the production of fissile weapons material, a basic component of all nuclear weapons, would seriously limit the capability of all nations to achieve a significant nuclear weapons capability. Such a ban, however, must guard against the two avenues of acquisition-clandestine production and diversion of fissile material from peaceful programs. Agreements to prohibit the transfer of nuclear weapons, weapons material, and weapons technology to nonnuclear powers and the establishment of nuclear-free zones should be coupled with a ban on production of fissile material. Positive actions by the U.S. toward these ends would be more meaningful than its present support of the MLF. In fact, the author believes the abandonment of the MLF by the U.S. would go along way toward clearing the air for serious negotiations on preventing the spread of nuclear weapons.

TION OF A MULTI-NUCLEAR FUTURE, by Richard A. Brody, in The Journal of Conflict 

Resolution, v. 7, no. 4 (December 1963) 663-753. "The effectiveness of future deterrence systems depends in part on the future state of affairs. One uncertain aspect of a deterrence system is the nature of international affairs in a world of many rather than few nuclear powers: the so-called 'ncountry problem.' The approach in this study follows from the posting of a theoretical model of the 'cold war' interaction system, the prediction of changes in this system which flow from the diffusion of nuclear weapons capability, and the exploration of the theoretical model in a laboratory simulation tailored to the study of these phenomena." THE SPREAD OF NUCLEAR WEAPONS, by Raymond Aron, in The Atlantic v. 215, no. 1 (January 1965) 44–50.

This paper by the French Professor of sociology has been drawn from the concluding chapter of his forthcoming new book (to be published by 

Doubleday): The Great Debate: Theories of Nu clear Strategy.-"One interest that has for the past few years been common to both Russia and the United States is the nondissemination of atomic arms, or... the limiting of thermonuclear power to the United States and the Soviet Union." Professor Aron attempts to provide some facts to shed light on the reasons for Soviet reluctance to limit nuclear testing, and some results of United States-Soviet negotiations in this area. rationally justify the American policy" concerning the dissemination of nuclear weapons. He then discusses France and Britain, the two atomic powers within the Atlantic Alliance. Also, to what extent will the qualitative arms race continue to slow down?

B. International Control 1. Miscellaneous Aspects EXISTING ARRANGEMENTS FOR IN- TERNATIONAL CONTROL OF WARLIKE 1 MATERIAL-5: THE UNITED STATES PROGRAM OF BILATERAL SAFEGUARDS, by Glenn T. Seaborg, in Disarmament and Arms 

Control, v. 2, no. 4 (Autumn 1964).

The successful launching of the Atoms for Peace =program was tied to the necessity for reasonable guarantees that nuclear technology and nuclear material would not be diverted toward military objectives. The U.S. undertook a series of bilateral safeguards which have paved the way for more lasting international agreements. The U.S. bilateral policy was, from the beginning, an "interim" program, to be replaced eventually by a system of international safeguards. Following U.S. ratifi cation of the International Atomic Energy Agency statute, IAEA safeguards were considered an inevitable and necessary replacement for the safeguards contained in bilateral agreements. Endeavoring to show good faith in IAEA, and to illustrate the feasibility of Agency control, the 

U.S. placed four reactors under IAEA safeguards, and has allowed the Agency to monitor U.S. bilateral transfer agreements. Thus, the U.S. bilateral safeguards system has helped to develop and strengthen the international system which may replace it. A detailed history of the U.S. program is given as an appendix.

THE INTERNATIONAL ATOM, by Glenn T. Seaborg, in The Department of State Bulletin, v. 51, no. 1327 (30 November 1964) 779–786.

An address at a Town Hall meeting at Los Angeles, 27 October 1964, at which time the Chair man of the U.S. Atomic Energy Commission reviewed his recent European trip where he attended meetings in Geneva, Brussels, and Vienna.

In Geneva, as Chairman of the U.S. delegation to the Third International Conference on the Peaceful Uses of Atomic Energy, he met with delegates from 75 nations to discuss progress in the peaceful applications of nuclear energy and our outlook for the future. In this present speech he addresses himself to: Developments in Nuclear Energy Since 

1958; Prospects for Growth of Nuclear Power; 

The International Atomic Energy Agency Safeguards System; How Nuclear Safeguards Came About; U.S. Interim Bilateral Arrangement; Ad vantages of International Safeguards; Some

Criticisms from Developing Countries; U.S.— EURATOM Agreement; etc. 

THE INTERNATIONAL CONTROL OF FISSILE MATERIAL PRODUCTION, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 1, no. 1 (Summer 

1963) 49-65.

This is a shortened version of a paper tabled by the British Delegation at the Conference of the Eighteen-Nation Committee on Disarmament in Geneva on 1 August 1962. The full version is published as Document 9 in Further Documents 

Relating to the 18-Nation Committee on Disarmament, London, H.M.S.O., Command 1857, November 1962. "The main conclusions of this study are based on experience in the U.K. Atomic Energy 

Authority. (1) After a few months operation, a control organization should be able to check current plutonium and U-235 production to within 

1-2 percent. (2) It is not likely that much more than this could be produced by operating clandestine plants, in view of difficulty of concealment. (3) The accuracy with which past production can be determined is very much less, especially in those countries which have an extensive nuclear weapons program. Errors of the order of 20 percent in stockpile assessment appear possible. (4) On the assumption of a worldwide control organization, it appears that a staff of 10,000, of which some 1,500 are scientists, will be required to achieve this result."

AGENCY, by John A. Hall, in Disarmament and

system for preventing diversions for military purposes of nuclear items supplied internationally for peaceful purposes, from the bare statutory framework into a generally accepted, operating reality. THE PEACEFUL ATOM IN FOREIGN POLICY, by Arnold Kramish. New York, Har per & Row, 1963. 276 p. (Published for the

Council of Foreign Relations.) 

"The three parts of this book 'deal broadly' E with three themes: firstly, the problems of applying to peaceful purposes a source of energy originally devised exclusively for war; secondly, the potentialities of nuclear energy in its peace-time role, with special reference to countries deficient in other energy-resources; thirdly, the ways in which international problems are now being tackled, with further suggestions to show how they might be overcome." This study is a review of a decade and more of the evolution of the peaceful atom as well as a critique of policy.

He brings the story up to March 

1963... Mr. Voss believes that the Soviet success in tying up the U.S. testing program with ... camouflaged techniques should provide a sobering warning." THE TEST-BAN NEGOTIATIONS: IM- PLICATIONS FOR THE FUTURE, by Harold 

K. Jacobson, in American Academy of Political and Social Science, Annals, v. 351 (January 1964) 92-101. "The record of the nuclear test-ban negotiations can well be read for its implications both for the procedure of negotiating and the substance of arms-control agreements. The history of the negotiations covers five years and includes an unpoliced moratorium on testing nuclear weapons which lasted nearly three years.... The experience of the negotiations suggests that areas where there is relative parity perhaps offer the most promise for arms-control agreements and that more progress might be made if greater emphasis were placed on national control or what has come to be called adversary or reciprocal inspection techniques."

IV. OUTER SPACE A. Miscellaneous Aspects OUTER SPACE IN WORLD POLITICS, ed. 

by Joseph M. Goldsen. New York, Frederick A.

B. Arms Control THE DETECTION OF R & D ACTIVITIES LEADING TO NEW MILITARY CAPABILI- 

TIES, by G. R. Pitman, Jr. El Segundo, Calif., Aerospace Corp., 1964.

24 p. (Report No. 

Prepared for the Arms Control and Disarma ment Agency.-Examines the problem of finding indicators which are associated with significant but unrecognized military applications of ballistic missiles and military space systems. The problem to be solved is one of technological intelligence evaluation associated with the acquisition of scientific and technological information for new military applications. The indicators which were available at the time of U.S. development of the atomic bomb and the U.S.S.R. development of 

Sputnik I are analyzed, and it is found that the principal problems of anticipating technological breakthroughs are those of postulating the events for which indicators are sought and then separating the indicators which are usually present from the background noise which accompanies them.

1963) 18-43. "The relation between the development of space weapons and the requirements for including them under an arms control agreement are discussed with reference to the usefulness of such weapons under varying strategies and the problems involved in subjecting these weapons to interna tional control." Reviews what the United States has done by its policy program to ensure the orderly and peaceful use of outer space through a system of regulation and control by the international community. C. Detection and Surveillance OBSERVATION SATELLITES FOR ARMS CONTROL INSPECTION, by Robert S. Roch lin, in Journal of Arms Control, v. 1, no. 3 (July 

1963) 224-247. "This survey of the potential capabilities of observation satellites indicates that they may constitute one of the most powerful tools available for arms control and disarmament inspection. As an example, a system is described which uses eight satellites to scan the entire surface of the earth each week with a ground resolution of 16 feet. 

Any suspicious objects found during this survey can be inspected with 20-inch ground resolution. 

Other types of sensors are also discussed." With references.

E. Law and Outer Space LAW AND STRATEGY IN SPACE, by Rob ert D. Crane, in Orbis, v. 6, no. 2 (Summer 1962) 281-300. "U.S. initiative in formulating space laws can serve not only to promote scientific research and economic progress and to facilitate the growth of a free and peaceful world order, but to implement on a higher moral level our military and political strategies. It would provide us freedom to employ necessary military defense and deterrent measures in outer space and to secure victory in the current war of ideology which is a primary goal of our military and political policies. The development of international space law oriented toward these strategic goals can help us to deal with the Communists on a higher moral level by making it politically more acceptable to counter military threats with military measures and by reducing the necessity of using military means to counter threats which are essentially ideological, political and economic."

SELF-DEFENSE IN OUTER SPACE. 

AND THE UNITED NATIONS, by John Cobb 

Cooper, in Air Force and Space Digest, v. 45, no.

2 (February 1962) 51-56. 

The author argues that the right of self-defense is a long-recognized principle of international law, that it is a right recognized by the UN, that the UN has given outer space international status, and that it follows that the right of self-defense extends into outer space. With references.

WHO OWNS THE UNKNOWN? by Joseph 

P. Roth, in Military Review, v. 44, no. 12 (December 1964) 13-19.

Examines the difficulties encountered involving the question of national sovereignty in outer space, the publicly expressed desire of most countries to preserve space for peaceful purposes, and the close link between sovereignty in outer space and disarmament. How can we foster space activities essential to scientific progress, and yet allow each state to protect the skies above it from hostile observation and attack? The answer must take a form of compromise between complete freedom of space, on one hand, and complete sovereignty of the subjacent state, on the other. 

F. Military Aspects THE FANTASTIC WEAPONRY, by Philip 

Siekman, in Fortune, v. 65, no. 6 (June 1962) 156159 plus. "Space, as a theatre of operations, is already a reality. 'Eyes in the sky' are in orbit. Satellite missiles may not be far behind. Military planners believe the U.S. needs manned interceptors as much as it needs to go to the moon."

INFLUENCE OF SPACE TECHNOLOGY ON HEMISPHERIC DEFENSE: AN INTER- AMERICAN DEFENSE COLLEGE LEC-

IADC in five sections: (1) the most immediate contribution of space technology to hemisphere security is through application to the U.S. strategic deterrent force; (2) in relation to national or regional security in the near future, the most important applications of rocket vehicles-the fundamental element of space technology-will continue to be LRBM's and satellite launchings; (3) communications satellites appear capable of overcoming the limitations of conventional radio and cable systems for reliable communications over long distances; (4) observation satellites are of interest because they can cover very large areas rapidly and take photographs for defense and other purposes. . .; meteorological satellites can be used to gather data for both immediate practical uses and long-term meteorological research."

SPACE DENIAL: COSTS AND CONSE- 

QUENCES, by Clark C. Abt, in Air Force, v. 46, no. 3 (March 1963) 45–52 plus.

A close analysis of the ramification, risks, advantages, and disadvantages of U.S. development of space-denial capacity suggests that although political, military, and economic costs would be high, the possibility that the Soviets may attempt to deny space to us and/or deploy spaceborne bombardment systems which could upset the world strategic balance requires our serious efforts to create space-denial techniques. Our use of such capabilities would require great restraint in the face of varied risks involved in their deployment.

V. PEACE AND WAR A. Miscellaneous Aspects CHANGE, HOPE, AND THE BOMB, by 

David E. Lilenthal. Princeton, N.J., Princeton 

University Press, 1963. 168 p.

Based on Stafford Little Lecture series given by the former Chairman (up to February 1950) of the 

AEC at Princeton University in February 1963, this book delves into US policy on nuclear weapons, disarmament, peaceful uses of the atom, the changing role of the scientists. The book is also a personal statement and "is necessarily a reflection of how the writer sees the world, a projection of his own special and unique experience of life applied to the gravest problems men have faced in many generations. . . . More specifically, this book about the Atom is an affirmation of what my own experience of life tells me about how men get things done, not in the theories and abstraction that are found in books, but in real life." The chapters are: The Imperatives of Change; The Solution Fallacy; Atomic Blind Alleys; The My thology of Nuclear Disarmament; What the Atom 

Is Doing To Science and Scientists; Whatever 

Happened To The Peaceful Atom?; Atomic

Anachronisms: The Atomic Energy Commission And The Joint Congressional Committee on 

Atomic Energy; Change And Hope. The author's conclusion is that man will not destroy either himself or the world in which he lives and that the more times goes by the stronger is the hope, and that the hope lies "in the vivid life-giving imagination-stirring waves of change upon change that are the chief characteristic of our times." . . ."I do not believe that God created man and endowed him with the capacity to unlock the energy within the very heart of matter in order that he should use that knowledge to destroy this beautiful world, which is the handiwork not of man, but of God."

2. Principles and Requirements es 290 200 

HAS MAN A FUTURE? by Bertrand Russell. 

New York, Simon and Schuster, 1962. 128 p.

The author presents his views on thermonuclear war, disarmament, and world government. He s "suggests that steps toward peace should include . . . a lessening of hostilities between East and West to prepare for disarmament, a ban on tests, an effort to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons, a general agreement to cease the manufacture of nuclear weapons under an inspection system."

ON THE CAUSES OF PEACE, by Werner 

Levi, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 8, no. 1 (March 1964) 23–35. "In the search for the solution of the problems of war and peace, there has been a striking preoccupation with the causes of war rather than the causes of peace. The often-heard definition that peace is the absence of war is indicative of the widespread endeavor to work on the abolition of war instead of the maintenance of peace. Strictly speaking, these goals are merely two sides of the same problem. Yet there is a significant difference in emphasis and psychological effect, resulting in a different research orientation." With references.

PEACE THEORY; PRECONDITIONS OF DISARMAMENT, by John W. Burton. New 

York, Alfred A. Knopf, 1962. 201 p.

The author states in his introduction that "The title Peace Theory has been given to this work, for that is what it is in methodological terms. It is, consequentially, also an analysis of policies which would be required in a disarmed world by nations wishing to avoid provocations leading to rearmament. It has, therefore, both a theoretical and a practical aspect, which explains the subtitle, Preconditions to Disarmament." He further states that "Unless the political scientist can draw a precise pattern of a stable condition of peace, unless there can be presented to the politician a picture of a disarmed world in which there will be no tendencies toward rearmament, any achievements TH 18 100

of peaceful international relationships will be largely accidental." This book is an attempt to provide some basic theory for those concerned with the formulation of policy.

PRINCIPLES OF PEACE, by Adm. Reginald A. R. P. Ernle-Erle-Drax, in The Royal United 

Service Institution Journal, v. 109, no. 634 (May 

1964) 155-159.

The author, feeling that it is high time that some learned body start work compiling a list of Principles of Peace, suggests fourteen of them himself. 

He strongly feels that such principles are urgently needed for the world "if we are to have any sure hope of avoiding major wars in the future."

(A thesis submitted to the faculty of Air War College, Air University.) 

"Through a study of the historical background of man, including his struggles, peace efforts and educational endeavors, the author discusses the feasibility, location, some methods of funding, faculty, curriculum, international cooperation for instigation and the probable effectiveness of an International Peace University as a means leading to elimination of war."

TEACHING WAR PREVENTION, by Saul H. Mendlovitz, in Bulletin of the Atomic Scien tists, v. 20, no. 2 (February 1964) 19–22. "PEACE RESEARCH" AND "INTERNA- TIONAL RELATIONS," by J. W. Burton, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 8, no. 3 

(September 1964) 281–286. "Peace" research organizations are now developing in many countries. Those concerned with "peace research" include both natural and social scientists. If peace research is to be taken seriously there are some pertinent questions to which satisfactory answers need to be given. Why did peace research develop outside existing disciplines, is it new, and are these established disciplines not able to deal adequately and more efficiently with the field covered by peace research? If there is a study of peace not being dealt with by an existing discipline what in precise terms is the field to be covered, or must it remain vaguely an "interdisciplinary" study with no defined boundaries?

The author maintains that the closer peace research is to universities, the more useful it will be. The appeal that can be made to charitable funds, to private enterprises, and to scholars is to help to get peace research "off the ground" with the prospect that, once off the ground, it will be a subject seriously supported by the formal educational institutions. 

PEACE RESEARCH-PAST, PRESENT, AND FUTURE, by Maurice L. Albertson, in 

Background, v. 6, no. 4 (Winter 1963) 29-40.

There has been increasing consideration given to the possibility of systematic study of basic principles which might directly or indirectly disclose methods and techniques of solving peacefully international conflicts, tensions, and problems which might lead to war. This type of study has been called "peace research." The author continues that "the challenge of today is for scholars and researchers from all disciplines to devote a significant amount of their time to research and study on these problems of a stable peace." He discusses: 

The Problem of Significant Variables; Current Activities; Peace Research Subjects Needing Study; Future Activities; Where To Conduct the 

Research; Professional Activities; and Appli cation of the Results of Peace Research and De velopment. With references.

A PROPOSAL FOR A SCIENTIFIC COM- PUTER-ORIENTED PROJECT ON WORLD 

101

PEACE RESEARCH, by Louis Fein, in Back ground, v. 7, no. 2 (August 1963) 97–108.

The author describes the possible objectives and operation of a world peace research center which he designates as PERC (Peace on Earth Research Center) and which would be a scientific computeroriented project with a staff of historians, social scientists, physical scientists, artists, and members of the computer profession. He proposes that a 

PERC type of organization be established with international sponsorship, and participation, with a charter to plan and carry out the kind of integrated and coordinated peace research program which he outlines.

THE PEACE 'CROWD', by Jerome Grossman, in The Nation, v. 198, no. 4 (20 January 1964) 6668. ... "Most of today's peace crowd [in the U.S.] wouldn't be caught dead with a picket sign. Protest has given way to approval of the token actions of the Administration. .. The narrow social base of the 'peace crowd' (or the 'peace movement') has often been deplored . . . the composition has been an amalgam of college-educated housewives, university-centered intelligentsia and students. Periodically, calls go out for a widening of the 'movement.' The most 'recent' attempt... was a national conference organized by Turn Toward Peace and a group of prominent Americans in New York on December 13, 14, and 15, 1963.

The stated purpose was 'to examine and strengthen the role of American voluntary organizations in achieving a world without war.' . . . The conference failed to create the impression of a group bursting with vitality and independence."

A REPORT ON THE CONFEDERATION FOR PEACE, in Our Generation Against Nu clear War, v. 3, no. 1 (June 1964) 3–7. 102

-- 
-- 
On 11 January 1964 some 100 people from 50 organizations from 17 countries met in Tyringe, Sweden at the conference of The International Confederation for Disarmament and Peace. This is a report on the Confederation which "is a body [seeking] to co-ordinate organizations, many of which have sprung up in recent years . . . and all of which stem from the principle of working for peace from a position not aligned with the major power blocs. . . . [The conference] was the first time that such diverse groups as pacifists, unilateralists, multilateralists, religious groups, pressure groups, umbrella movements, information and research bodies, student and youth groups confronted each other within a common framework."

C. Conflicts and War: Roots and Causes COMPETITION AND DISSENSUS: TWO TYPES OF CONFLICT AND OF CONFLICT RESOLUTION, by Viehelm Aubert, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 7, no. 1 (March 1963) 

26-42. 

This paper "sets out to develop a simple typology of interpersonal conflicts. The evolving scheme of classification is based upon sources of conflicts as well as upon the modes of their settlement. 

Some systematic relationships between sources of conflicts and their settlements are suggested. Certain sources of conflict may tend to call forth a certain type of mechanism for conflict resolution, but the form of the conflict may also frequently be determined by the available means of solution." 

With references.

CRISES AND ARMS CONTROL, ed. by Anthony J. Wiener and Herman Kahn. Harmon-on- 

Hudson, N.Y., Hudson Institute, 1962. 415 p. (HI-180-RR.) 

Prepared for the Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense (International Security Affairs) for 

Disarmament. "There are many important reasons for studying the relationships between crises and arms control and even more reasons for studying crises themselves. Most obviously, a crisis can lead to a small or large war. The ways that a war can start have an important bearing on whether we can avoid having one or, failing avoidance, wage and survive it." Contents: Introduction (The Study of Crises, Arms Control and Crises, etc.); A Range of Crises (including Nth Country 

Problem); Escalation and Its Strategic Context;

Strategic Analysis-The Upper Rungs of the Es calation Ladder; Decision-Making and the Ad ministration of Crises; Strategy and Tactics in Crises; Arms Control Measures and Crises (Negotiation As an Arms Control Measure, The Role of Arms Control Agreements in Crises, Effects of Arms Control on Crises, Effect of Crises on Arms 

Control); The United Nations, International Law, and Crises; Crises and International Systems (including Crises, Arms Controls, and International Systems).

INTERNAL DYNAMICS OF CONFLICT AND CHANGE: ARMS CONTROL AND IN- TERNAL WARFARE, by Edward W. Gude. 

Bedford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 50 p. 

(Project Unicorn BR-2526.) 

"Much of the current writing on internal warfare has attempted to derive from operational experience the processes which determine the course of political developments in violent social change. 

This paper has attempted to do the opposite: to derive from theoretical social science concepts and empirical research on political development a framework with which to analyze the major factors influencing internal warfare. This framework is then used to analyze the operational aspects of internal war. By starting with concepts about the political system and analyzing the various actors, actions, and responses in internal warfare; a framework is established with which to view the tactical considerations. This framework-emphasizing such concepts as stability of a political system, the nature of legitimate force in a society, the impact of participant and pre-participant sectors, and the role of violence is then used to discuss arms control implications of internal warfare. It is the major conclusion of this paper that violence, 103

far from being the absence of politics and political institutions, is the essence of the political struggle in internal war. Insurgents have been remarkably adroit in using selective terror and violence to stimulate government repression and thus increase political alienation, increase political mobilization, and increase support for their cause. One of the most difficult tasks facing an insurgent cadre is to increase political mobilization-to politicize the peasantry. This is the task that they can only partially accomplish themselves. Indiscriminate use of napalm, rockets, and similar weapons creates more insurgents than it eliminates. Arms control objectives of limiting the probability of war, limiting escalation, and limiting damage if war should occur are seen as identical with strategic objectives in counterinsurgency. A regime cannot eliminate an insurgent movement without force, but excessive form carries a high cost. All tactics, including those utilizing a high degree of force and those using little force, must be seen in the light of the politics of violence. An insurgent movement uses violence to attain specific objectives: a regime must use both non-violent and violent means to deny these objectives. The insurgents seek indiscriminate repression and violence: the regime must respond with a spectrum of tactics designed to minimize violence."

THE JUST WAR DOCTRINE, by Ernest W. 

Lefever, in Survival, v. 4, no. 2 (March-April 

1962) 71-75.

The author shows "how moral arguments about coercion must be related to the different possibilities of war in the nuclear age."

STEPWISE DISARMAMENT AND SUD- DEN DESTRUCTION IN A TWO-PERSON GAME: A RESEARCH TOOL, by Marc Pili suk and Anatol Rapoport, in The Journal of Conflict Resolution, v. 8, no. 1 (March 1964) 36–49. "This article describes a method, i.e., an experimental game, which offers opportunity for control of several variables relevant to the resolution of two-party conflicts." The authors also provide a comparison of the game with other experimental games now in use. STRATEGY AND CONSCIENCE, by Anatol 

Rapoport. New York, Harper. 1964. 324 p.

This publication from the Center for Research 

On Conflict and Resolution, of the University of

Michigan, was written "as a protest against a glib and shallow fashion of contemporary thought that embodies and enhances man's inhumanity to man." 

The author presents the view that "many of our theories of strategic deterrence have hardly more claim to intellectual respectability than did the crude and misleading economic theories of the eighteenth century." He also thinks that extensive preoccupation with "images of terror and ruthless destruction" and so little with the "images of moderation, compromise, and mutual accommodation" will condition us to accept the thinking of strategists and will lead us into nuclear mass killing. "It is high time" the author suggests "that thinking about war and peace became again a matter of human conscience, of human insights, and of the multiple, interdependent, and developing values of mankind." In chapter 24 THE

CASE FOR IDEOLOGICAL DISARMA- 

MENT, the author shows the different problems that are involved in the approach to the subject in the Soviet Union and the United States. 

WHAT IS WAR? by Norman Precoda, in Air 

University Review, v. 14, no. 4 (September-October 1963) 46-55. "One of the most curious aspects of war is that, although millions of people have had firsthand experience with it, although nearly everyone has expressed opinions on the subject, we seem to know little and misunderstand much about war. What is war? What causes it? When, how, and why does it begin and cease? . . .

The re- curring vision of a world without war is probably chimerical. Rather, our hope and . . . our responsibility lie in ceaselessly striving to push the limiting line of international embitterment down scale and avoid or eliminate cataclysmic accommodations." 410 104

D. War 1. Abolition and Prevention THE ABOLITION OF WAR, by Walter 

Millis and James Real. New York, Macmillan, 1963. 217 p.

This book "describes the ages in which war was an unavoidable-even creative-instrument necessary for the ordering of the world. It begins and concludes with the premise that this method of dealing with the world's problems is hopelessly outmoded. Reference is made to the contemporary predicament, a state of affairs that is pushing all the great powers in directions they do not want to go and which, for America, can lead only to the continuing erection and maintenance of the fortress state, the price for which is loss of the very freedoms we are defending and the democratic goals we seek-both for ourselves and for others. Myth and power are examined, not as new or novel ideas, but because these intertwined components of the human condition seem not to have had sufficient notice in a world demused by its own technical virtuosity." The authors believe that although problems are immense they can and will be solved and that the means and reasons to abolish war forever are already at hand.

TURE, by Judd Marmor, in Bulletin of the

The author addresses himself to the question of whether war is an intrinsic manifestation of human nature and therefore unmodifiable. He contends that modern warfare is a social institution and therefore potentially eradicable as other social institutions-slavery, dueling, ritual human sacrifice, and cannibalism--have proved to be.

3. Catalytic A THEORY OF CATALYTIC WAR, by Donald H. Kobe, in The Journal of Conflict Resolu tion, v. 6, no. 2 (1962) 125-142.

One aspect of the Nth country problem is the possibility that a country could attack another country, with the intention of having the attack attributed to a third country. The country attacked would retaliate against the third country, thus touching off a chain reaction of retaliation and counter retaliation. This process is known as catalytic war because the initiating country would hope to emerge without suffering any damage. "This paper develops a theory of catalytic war which will enable the prediction, given information about the policies and capabilities of the various countries, of which countries will be affected, and to what extent, by the chain of events resulting from an attack on one of the countries. Therefore, it can be predicted under what conditions a country would find it favorable to initiate a catalytic war." The theory is developed by the author and illustrated by two examples. With references. 

4. New Methods and Dimensions COMMUNIST TECHNIQUES FOR CON- QUEST, by Allen W. Dulles, in Military Review, v. 43, no. 2 (February 1963) 49–56.

The author reflects on the character and methods of Stalin in order to understand how power is come by and how it is exercised in the Soviet Union. 

Mr. Dulles then considers the present state of affairs inside the U.S.S.R., noting that the dictatorship remains and Khrushchev is the dictator.

While Khrushchev now disavows any intention to extend Communism by direct injection of Soviet armed force, "he, nevertheless, forthrightly advocates the support of 'wars of liberation' so as to extend the Communist forms of government into as yet unbreached parts of the world."

INTERNAL WAR; THE NEW COM- MUNIST TACTIC, by Roger Hilsman, in Mili tary Review, v. 42, no. 4 (April 1962) 11-22. "The cold war has been with us for 16 years [as of the writing of this article]. And each year the Communist tactics are more subtle and complex. . . The new tactic is internal war-using military force not across national boundaries but inside them." The author discusses this new tactic, and what we can do to help responsible and friendly governments involved in such situations.

Kornfeder. Chicago, Regnery, 1962. 362 p. "The new frontier of war is political war, defined as 'a form of conflict between states in which a protagonist nation seeks to impose its will upon its opponents without the direct use of armed force.' Battleground'; they confine their analysis of Communist expansion to China, Southeast Asia, and

Latin America; 'Counterattack,' contains chapters on 'Training for Political Warfare' and "Target: Communist Vulnerabilities.' . . . The authors . . . see see a Western political counteroffensive as essential. . . . They favor a 'Freedom Academy' to train generalists in the techniques of fighting the cold war." With bibliography.

THE LIMITS OF NUCLEAR THINKING ABOUT THE DO-ABLE AND THE UN-DO-ABLE, by Paul Ramsey. New 

York, The Council on Religion and International 

Affairs, 1963. 54 p.

An analysis of the moral issues involved in nuclear deterrence, defending the thesis that "counterforce nuclear war is the upper limit of national, politically purposive military action." The author argues that "the No-Cities or Counterforce plus avoidance policy is the only just and sensible way to conduct war; that weapons analysts have sometimes overlooked the distinction between the unthinkable that has simply not yet been thought and the unthinkable that is un-do-able when thought about; that war becomes an abysmal and unlimited conflict of resolutions; that current discussions of 'general' war or 'limited strategic war' need to distinguish more clearly between longrange attacks on strategic forces and strategic attacks on cities; and finally . . .[he advances] tentatively a graduation of policy decisions designed to set 'firebreaks' between the steps in increasing violence, up to the Non-Cities or strategic counterforce policy." The author also takes up the moral and practical questions involved in 'deterrence during the war,' which at every stage seems necessary to keep war limited."

ONE HUNDRED MILLION LIVES: MAXI- MUM SURVIVAL IN A NUCLEAR WAR, by 

Richard Fryklund. New York, Macmillan, 1962. 175 p. "A nuclear war strategy based on control and restraint." The author advocates a "no city strategy," one where both sides would agree to spare cities and civilian populations, which the author believes, would save 100 million lives. 

6. Simulated

NUCLEAR STALEMATE AND SIMU- 

LATED WAR, by Charles T. Stewart, Jr., in Military Review, v. 42, no. 1 (January 1962) 78-89.

The society of nations lives in unstable equilibrium. At other times in history, nations had clashing interests and irreconciliable objectives, but stopped short of war for essentially the same reasons that exist today: the high costs of victory, the genuine chance of defeat, and its inconceivable consequences. Under such conditions, nations have found other means of resolving their differences, or of partly or wholly attaining their objectivesmeans which were not peaceful, yet not warlike. "Since nuclear stalemate could in time rule out the rational possibility of total war, it is proper to consider some of the alternative strategies." The term "simulated war" in this article means any procedure used as a substitute for full-scale war as a method of seeking decision. "The intent of simulated war is to obtain the decisions of fullscale war without its attendant costs and losses (including risks other than the risk of total defeat). Contents: simulated war in history; conditions for simulated war; simulation versus limitation; hostage cities; purpose of civil defense; function of information; the fallout deterrent; factor of mobility; unstable stalemate; bases for simulated war; etc. 

7. War Termination WAR TERMINATION STUDY, by F. J. 

Schell. Bedford, Mass., Raytheon Company, 1963. 28 p. (Project Unicorn BR-2523.) 

"In order to avoid being pushed beyond undesirable thresholds (e.g., from world power to local power, from power to base recovery level, from recovery level to disintegration), nations may wish to terminate a nuclear war. In order to do so they should make explicit gain/loss calculations to pick the proper conditions to initiate internal policy, seek ceasefire conditions, and bargain to an agreement. When the calculation indicates the possibility of advantageous termination, the job of thorough internal discussion must proceed in order to avoid any possibility of poor motives such as treachery; cowardice. If the internal discussion makes the desirability of attempting 107

termination very clear, attention-getting devices may be needed to promote serious ceasefire talks. 

The techniques of pseudo-surrender, pseudo-concession and real concession are presented as possibilities. Special use of missiles, with and without intentional waste are: the "shot at nothing" technique, with fanfare and the "shoot at the bottom of the target list" technique. Assuming positions of vulnerability in very limited circumstances can be very effective as an attention device if the risk can be justified. An abrupt change of one's targeting is also a possible indicator to the enemy that a bargain might be (or needs to be) struck. Enforcing a ceasefire agreement could imply an additional, new control structure, in the limit, international in scope and interconnected in the UN. On a lesser scale, great improvement in national capabilities are needed to implement a ceasefire, which must be done quickly and effectively. The need for broadcast communication to weapons could be met by a satellite system whose vulnerability to jamming would result in physical preservation until an agreement were reached and the jamming removed. Forms of international 

PAL and shared communications could also be arranged. Four limited war scenarios were developed which suggested the following possibilities (1) Considerable conventional war activity may occur in Europe without the outbreak of 

NATO war or Warsaw war. (2) In thinly populated areas, especially with logistic disparity TAC NUC may be the normal form of warfare with a minimum of serious escalatory problems. (3) In a conventional war over Cuba, responsibilities for defense actions including nuclear use, may be difficult to estimate and anticipate. (4) Whatever the emotional content of the Sino-Russian border differences and ideological quarrels, their relative power standings preclude a serious military clash."

PEACE RESEARCH ABSTRACTS JOUR- 

NAL, v. 1, no. 1 (June 1964) 164 p.

The Peace Research Abstracts Journal, which has, as of October 1964, published five issues, one per month, is prepared under the auspices of the 

Canadian Peace Research Institute, Clarkson, Ontario. The citations are culled from over 600 journals in several languages and they appear in the Journal with an abstract or summary. The subject classification includes ten major classes:

The Military Situation; Limitation of Arms; Tension and Conflict; Ideology and Issues; International Institutions and Regional Alliances; Na tions and National Policies; Pairs of Countries and Crisis Areas; International Law, Economics, and Diplomacy; Decision Making and Communi cations; and Methods and Miscellaneous. Each issue of the Journal includes about 1,000 abstracts.

COMMUNIST OUTLETS FOR THE DIS- TRIBUTION OF SOVIET PROPAGANDA IN THE UNITED STATES. HEARINGS BEFORE THE COMMITTEE ON UN- AMERICAN ACTIVITIES, HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, EIGHTY-SEVENTH CONGRESS, SECOND SESSION, MAY 9, 10, AND 17 AND JULY 11 AND 12, 1962. Wash ington, Government Printing Office, 1962. 2 pts.

The committee believes that propaganda directed from Soviet sources constitutes one of the greatest single threats to the security of the U.S. and the free world. The world Communist movement allocates many millions of dollars annually for the publication and distribution of propaganda documents. The committee held the hearings in 

May and July on the publication and distribution within the U.S. of Communist propaganda material originating in foreign countries, in order to develop information which would assist the committee in weighing the merits of amendments to the Internal Security Act and the Foreign Agents 

Registration Act. pertaining to the printing and dissemination of foreign propaganda, and also the administration of existing laws relating to this subject.

SOVIET SPACE PROGRAMS; ORGANI- ZATION, PLANS, GOALS, AND INTERNA- TIONAL IMPLICATIONS. STAFF REPORT PREPARED FOR THE USE OF THE COM- MITTEE ON AERONAUTICAL AND SPACE SCIENCES, UNITED STATES SENATE, [BY THE LIBRARY OF CONGRESS] 31 MAY 1962. Washington, Government Printing 

Office, 1962. 399 p. (87th Congress, 2d Session.)

This study has been divided into three major sections: Part I provides as an introduction a general survey of science in the Soviet Union; Part 

II deals extensively with Soviet Science in space and Part III concerns certain implications of the space activities of the USSR for international cooperation and international law. Chapters subsumed under these major categories attempt to provide a descriptive account of Soviet science and technology, an estimate of possible Soviet goals in space, an examination of the possible organizational structure of the Soviet space program, a review of some Soviet achievements in space and an anlysis of the Soviet attitude toward international cooperation in space and international space law. Among the many appendixes are summaries on Soviet space activities prepared by NASA from United States and Soviet press and radio.

EXTENDING THE NUCLEAR TEST BAN, by John Cockcroft, in Disarmament and Arms 

Control, v. 2, no. 1 (Winter 1963/64) 16-22. 

Presented to the Eleventh Pugwash Conference on Science and World Affairs at Dubrovnik,

Yugoslavia, in September 1963. "An extension of the test ban treaty would become much more probable if underground explosions could be distinguished from earthquakes by seismological stations located outside the country where the event took place. Great progress has been made in the long range detection and identification of earthquakes since 1958. The paper discusses the problems of identification and suggests lines of further development."

GENEVA NEGOTIATIONS AS A CON- ISTITUENT ASSEMBLY, by Gion Paolo Toz- 

Izoli, in Disarmament and Arms Control, v. 2, no. 2 (Spring 1964) 126–135. 1 "The Geneva Disarmament Conference is gen Ierally considered as a conventional negotiation, aiming at a compromise between opposing schemes by means of mutual concessions. But an agree- 

Iment on general and complete disarmament represents much more than a du ut des ['I yield in order that you may yield'] negotiation; it involves at least a partial and gradual change in the nature of the international community. The negotiating body concerned plays a revolutionary role, which is reminiscent of the function of a 

Constituent Assembly. The historical features and the juridical principles of the Constituent Assembly are surveyed [by the author], particularly the cultural movements leading to the 

French Revolution, and the international significance of the foundation of the United States. 

The author applies his conclusions to the procedure and context of the Geneva negotiations."

Library, 1964. 585 p. "The aim of this dictionary is to provide teachers, students, and laymen interested in the discipline which devotes itself to the study of politics with concise definitions and descriptions of the terms, events, and personalities used most frequently in the writing of political scientists."

Among the definitions in the dictionary: Arms 

Control; Arms Race; Atoms for Peace; Balance of Power; Balance of Terror; Collective Secu rity; Dialectical Materialism; Eisenhower Doc trine; International Treaties and Agreements; 

Racism; Red Chinese "Peoples Republic"; Search and Seizure; Sovereignty; Territorial Waters; 

Unconventional Warfare; United Nations Emer gency Force; U-2 Affair; War of Liberation; 

Zengakuren, etc.

ENGLISH-RUSSIAN DICTIONARY ON AVIATION AND ROCKET BASES. Anglo- 

Russkii Slovar' po aviatsionnym i raketnym bazam, comp. by S. M. Nikitin and Iu. I. Khrushchev. Moscow, Voennoe Izdatel'stvo Ministerstva Oborony Soiuza SSSR, 1962. 355 p.

Contains more than 8,000 words dealing with construction and utilization of airports and rocket bases. The terminology is based on that employed in U.S.A., Great Britain, or the Armed 

Forces of NATO. Appendixes include a listing of the names and location of some of the U.S. domestic and overseas air bases as well as those of other Western Powers.

The dictionary is based mainly on Soviet sources. де с at 116

Appendixes include conversion tables, abbreviations, etc.

RUSSIAN-ENGLISH PHYSICS DICTION- 

ARY, by Irving Emin. New York, John Wiley, 1963. 554 p.

The core of the vocabulary in this dictionary was derived from over 10,000 pages of Soviet physics journals published from 1955 to 1962. 

The dictionary enumerates the titles of the following sources that were also used for additional vocabulary: Russian language dictionaries and encyclopedias published in the U.S.S.R.; terminology lists published in the U.S.S.R.; Russian #physics books; Russian-English dictionaries; 

English-Russian dictionaries published in the U.S.S.R. Among the valuable features of this dictionary are: the actual vocabulary, including 

8: synonyms for many concepts, employed by Soviet physicists in reporting current research; a critical selection of vocabulary from numerous bilingual dictionaries published in the Soviet Union; Russian abbreviations for scientific units, journal I titles, institutions, and other expressions frequently encountered in the Russian periodical literature; Russian transliterations of the names of many prominent non-Russian scientists, past and present; the basic vocabulary of chemistry and electronics, and general technical terminology including the names of instruments, machine parts, and materials (especially alloys), that have been found in the physics literature; astrophysical and astronomical vocabulary, including the Russian names of constellations, stars, planets, satellites, meteors, lunar features; the inclusion of the terminology of all important branches of physics-theoretical, experimental, mathematical, and applied (nuclear-reactor); geophysical vocabulary, especially in the field of meteorology and geophysical prospecting. It has taken (Irving 

Emin and the Consultants Bureau staff of physicist-translators) seven years to prepare the dictionary.

TRILINGUAL GLOSSARY OF STRATE- GIC TERMINOLOGY, comp. and ed. by Laszlo 

Hadik. Geneva, Institut Universitaire de Hautes 

Études Internationales, 1964. 141 p.

The author compiled and edited this draft copy of the glossary while he was on loan for one year from The Institute of Defense Analyses to the Graduate Institute of International Studies. 

Louis J. Halle, of The Institute, writes in the introduction that this trilingual glossary (in English, French, and German) includes some definitions which were taken as found in various works, and others which were composed on the basis of what was believed to be the commonest usage. 

Examples of some of the words and phrases defined are: nuclear plenty, surprise attack, arms race, credibility, overkill capability, deterrence, first strike, preventive war, nuclear parity, general and complete disarmament, among many others.

Also included are: a short bibliography, an alphabetic table of French terms, and an alphabetic table of German terms. It is contemplated that sometime in the future a revised and enlarged edition will be published as a successor to this draft copy.

F. Directory (Space Science Personnel) SPACE SCIENTISTS AND ENGINEERS:
